Unlike complements for inner-products, a subspace need not be transverse to its symplectic complement. Indeed, in symplectic linear algebra, we are mostly concerned with subspaces which intersect their symplectic complements in interesting ways. Before we examine these subspaces, lets establish some foundational properties of symplectic complements.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a symplectic vector space and
A,B\subseteq V
two linear subspaces.

(1)

If
A\subseteq B
, then
B^{\omega}\subseteq A^{\omega}
.

(2)

(A^{\omega})^{\omega}=A
.

(3)

\dim(A)+\dim(A^{\omega})=\dim(V)
.

Definition 3.8

(Distinguished Subspaces of Symplectic Vector Space)
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a symplectic vector space and
C\subseteq V
a linear subspace. We say
C
is

(1)

isotropic
if
C\subseteq C^{\omega}
.

(2)

coisotropic
if
C^{\omega}\subseteq C

(3)

Lagrangian
if
C=C^{\omega}
.

(4)

symplectic
if
C\cap C^{\omega}=\{0\}
.

Example 3.9
.

Consider standard symplectic
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
and let
e_{1},\dots,e_{n},f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
be the standard Darboux basis. Let’s now look at standard examples of each kind of subspace introduced in Definition
3.8
.

(1)

Isotropic.

A=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\}

for
k\leq n
. A quick computation shows

A^{\omega_{0}}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n},f_{k+1},\dots,f_{n}\},

which in particular contains
A
. Thus
A
is isotropic. Note that due to (1) in Proposition
3.7
it follows that
A^{\omega_{0}}
is coisotropic.

(2)

Coisotropic.

B=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n},f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\}.

One can also immediately see that

B^{\omega_{0}}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{k+1},\dots,e_{n}\}

which is contained in
B
. Hence,
B
is coisotropic. Similar to the above, we also see that property (1) of Proposition
3.7
implies that
B^{\omega_{0}}
is isotropic.

(3)

Lagrangian.

C=\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}.

Using the computation in (a), we immediately deduce that
C^{\omega_{0}}=C
and hence
C
is Lagrangian.

(4)

Symplectic.

D=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{k},f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\}.

One can check that

D^{\omega_{0}}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{k+1},\dots,e_{n},f_{k+1},\dots,f_{n}\}.

Note that the restriction of
\omega_{0}
to both
D
and
D^{\omega_{0}}
defines a symplectic form on each subspace.

Let’s now summarize some easy properties about symplectic vector spaces that we will be freely using later on.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a symplectic vector space and
C\subseteq V
a symplectic subspace.

(1)

The restrictions
\omega|_{C}
and
\omega|_{C^{\omega}}
defines symplectic forms on each subspace.

(2)

V
is the internal direct sum of
C
and
C^{\omega}
. That is,
V=C\oplus C^{\omega}
.

So we see then that symplectic subspaces are precisely the subspaces where symplectic complements act more like complements associated to an inner-product. In particular, to each symplectic subspace we get a canonical projection map.

Definition 3.11

(Symplectic Projection)
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a symplectic vector space and
C\subseteq V
a symplectic subspace. Let
P_{C}:V\to C
be the canonical projection induced by the internal direct sum
V=C\oplus C^{\omega}
.

6.2
Linear Symplectic Reduction

Now to close up our introductory discussion on symplectic linear algebra, let us now discuss linear symplectic reduction.

Proposition 3.12

(Linear Symplectic Reduction)
.

Let
C\subseteq V
be a subspace. Then

V_{0}:=\frac{C}{C\cap C^{\omega}}

is a symplectic vector space with a unique symplectic form
\omega_{0}
satisfying

\pi^{*}\omega_{0}=\omega|_{C},

where
\pi:C\to V_{0}
is the canonical projection.

Proof.

Define
\omega_{0}
on
V_{0}
by

\omega_{0}([c],[c^{\prime}]):=\omega(c,c^{\prime}),

where
c,c^{\prime}\in C
and
[c],[c^{\prime}]\in V_{0}
are their corresponding equivalence classes. First we show that
\omega_{0}
is well-defined, then we show
\omega_{0}
is non-degenerate.

(i)

\omega_{0}
is well-defined.

Indeed, if
c,d,c^{\prime},d^{\prime}\in C
such that
c-d,\ c^{\prime}-d^{\prime}\in C\cap C^{\omega}
, then

\omega(d,d^{\prime})=\omega(d+(c-d),d^{\prime}+(c^{\prime}-d^{\prime}))=\omega(c,c^{\prime}).

Hence,
\omega_{0}([d],[d^{\prime}])=\omega_{0}([c],[c^{\prime}])
as desired.

(ii)

\omega_{0}
is non-degenerate.

Suppose
c\in C
so that
[c]\in\ker(\omega_{0}^{\flat})
. That is, for all
d\in C
we have

\omega_{0}([c],[d])=0.

By definition of
\omega_{0}
, this implies
\omega(c,d)=0
for all
d\in C
. In particular, this implies
c\in C^{\omega}
and hence
c\in C\cap C^{\omega}
. Therefore,
[c]=0
and and so
\omega_{0}^{\flat}
is injective hence a linear isomorphism.

∎

Definition 3.13

(Linear Reduced Space)
.

Given a subspace
C\subseteq V
, call
V_{0}=C/(C\cap C^{\omega})
the
linear reduction
of
V
at
C
and call
\pi_{0}:C\to V_{0}
the
reduction map.

In the next chapter, we will discuss what other structures can be “reduced” in a symplectic vector space. In particular, we will study when Lagrangian subspaces can be reduced.

7
Symplectic and Poisson Manifolds

We are now ready to define symplectic manifolds. As is quite unorthodox, we will pretty quickly move into even greater generality of Poisson manifolds. This will be done as many features of symplectic manifolds which may appear somewhat mysterious become very natural in the more general Poisson picture. Furthermore, in the singular setting, the Poisson bracket is the more natural global object and so it is worthwhile spending time on both concepts at the same time.

Definition 3.14

(Symplectic Manifold)
.

A symplectic manifold is a pair
(M,\omega)
where
M
is a manifold and
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
is a
2
-form satisfying the following two conditions.

(1)

(Closed)
d\omega=0
.

(2)

(Non-Degenerate) The map

\omega^{\flat}:TM\to T^{*}M;\quad v\mapsto\omega(v,\cdot)

is an isomorphism of vector bundles.

If
\omega
satisfies the above two conditions, we call
\omega
a
symplectic form
.

Example 3.15
.

Of course any symplectic vector space
(V,\omega)
will be a symplectic manifold. Turning now to standard symplectic
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
, if we write
\mathbb{R}^{2n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and write
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
for the coordinates of the first and second copies of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, respectively, then

\omega_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}dx_{i}\wedge dy_{i}

which is transparently closed. A simple application of Corollary
3.5
then immediately shows all symplectic vector spaces are symplectic manifolds.

Example 3.16
.

As a generalization of the above, consider now cotangent bundles. Fix a manifold
Q
and let
\tau_{Q}:T^{*}Q\to Q
be the cotangent bundle of
Q
. Define the
Liouville
1
-form

\theta_{Q}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}Q)
by

(\theta_{Q})_{\alpha}(v)=\langle\alpha,(\tau_{Q})_{*}v\rangle

(30)

for
\alpha\in T^{*}Q
and
v\in T_{\alpha}(T^{*}Q)
. Choosing local coordinates
(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})
on
Q
and letting
(p^{1},\dots,p^{n})
be the induced cotangent coordinates, it immediately follows that in these coordinates

\theta_{Q}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}dq_{i}

(31)

and hence in these coordinates

d\theta_{Q}=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}dq_{i}\wedge dp_{i}

(32)

Therefore,

\omega_{Q}=-d\theta_{Q}\in\Omega^{2}(T^{*}Q)

(33)

is a symplectic form.

Notice how both the symplectic vector space and the cotangent bundle examples were able to be written in the same local form. This is a more general phenomenon of symplectic manifolds.

Theorem 3.17
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold with
\dim(M)=2n
and
x\in M
. Then we can find a neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
of
x
, a neighbourhood
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2n}
of
0
, and a diffeomorphism

\phi:U\to V

such that
\phi^{*}\omega_{0}=\omega|_{U}
, where
\omega_{0}
is the standard symplectic form.

In particular, there are no local invariants of symplectic manifolds. This makes symplectic structures a much more rigid structure than say Riemannian structures. Before we continue on with more basic definitions of symplectic geometry, let us now discuss another family of examples of non-trivial symplectic manifolds, namely coadjoint orbits.

Example 3.18
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, and let
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
denote its dual. Recall the coadjoint action introduced in Example
2.2

G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*};\quad(g,q)\mapsto\text{Ad}_{g}^{*}(q).

We will now discuss a canonical symplectic structure on the coadjoint orbits, called the Kostant-Kirillov-Souriau symplectic structure. Let us now fix
p\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and write

\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p):=\{\text{Ad}_{g}^{*}(p)\ |\ g\in G\}

for the coadjoint orbit through
p
. Equip
\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p)
with a smooth manifold structure via the bijection

G/G_{p}\to\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p).

Since
\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p)
is an orbit of a group action, the its tangent spaces are spanned by fundamental vector fields. In particular, at
p
, define

\omega_{KKS}(\xi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}(p),\eta_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}(p)):=\langle p,[\xi,\eta]\rangle.

(34)

\omega_{KKS}
can then be extended by the
G
-action to all of
\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p)
to define a
2
-form
\omega_{KKS}\in\Omega^{2}(\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p))
. I claim now that
\omega_{KKS}
is a symplectic form.

Let us assume that
G_{p}\neq G
otherwise
\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p)
is just a point and we will agree that points are trivial symplectic manifolds. Now, to show
\omega_{KKS}
is closed, first identify
T_{q}^{*}\mathfrak{g}^{*}=\mathfrak{g}
. In this case, we can then easily show that if
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
and
q\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, then the fundamental vector field
\xi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}(q)
at
q
has the form

\xi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}(q)=-\text{ad}_{\xi}^{*}(q),

(35)

where
\text{ad}_{\xi}^{*}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is defined by

\langle\text{ad}_{\xi}^{*}(q),\eta\rangle=-\langle q,[\xi,\eta]\rangle

Making use of the identity

[\text{ad}_{\xi}^{*},\text{ad}_{\eta}^{*}]=\text{ad}_{[\xi,\eta]}^{*}

then implies
d\omega_{KKS}=0
. Non-degeneracy is a consequence of the fact that
G_{p}\neq G
.

Given now a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
, by definition each tangent space
T_{x}M
is a symplectic vector space. Using this, we can now “globalize” the linear definitions of distinguished subspaces of a symplectic vector space to submanifolds of a symplectic manifold.

Definition 3.19

(Isotropic/Coisotropic/Lagrangian/Symplectic Submanifolds)
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
C\subseteq M
a submanifold. For each property
P\in\{\text{isotropic},\ \text{coisotropic},\ \text{Lagrangian},\ \text{symplectic}\}
, say
C
is a
P
submanifold if
T_{x}C\subseteq T_{x}M
is a
P
subspace for each
x\in C
.

Now symplectic manifolds have a distinguished class of vector fields called Hamiltonian vector fields. These are obtained by the identification of vector fields and
1
-forms induced by the isomorphism
\omega^{\flat}:TM\to T^{*}M
. In particular, the Hamiltonian vector fields correspond to the exact
1
-forms. However, before we discuss this any further, it will be most natural for when we get into the singular setting to introduce a more general structure than symplectic forms. Namely, Poisson structures.

Definition 3.20

(Poisson Structure)
.

A
Poisson manifold
is a pair
(M,\pi)
, where
M
is a manifold and
\pi\in\bigwedge^{2}(TM)
is a bivector so that the pairing

\{\cdot,\cdot\}:C^{\infty}(M)\times C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(M);\quad(f,g)\mapsto\{f,g\}:=\pi(df,dg)

satisfies the Jacobi identity. That is, for all
f,g,h\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we have

\{f,\{g,h\}\}+\{g,\{h,f\}\}+\{h,\{f,g\}\}=0.

We call
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
a
Poisson bracket
.

Remark 3.21
.

Given that the Poisson brackets
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
arise from biderivations, it follows that they are bilinear and antisymmetric. In particular, this implies a Poisson bracket is a Lie bracket.

Definition 3.22

(Poisson Map)
.

Let
(M_{1},\pi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\pi_{2})
be two Poisson manifolds. A smooth map
\phi:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is said to be
Poisson
if for all
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M_{2})
we have

\{\phi^{*}f,\phi^{*}g\}_{1}=\phi^{*}\{f,g\}_{2}..

Now, if we are given a Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
, there is a very natural way to obtain vector fields from functions. Given a function
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, observe that since
\pi
is a bivector, the map

\{f,\cdot\}:C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(M)

is a derivation. As we will be exploring in great detail in Chapter
6
, derivations of the algebra of functions is one way of characterizing vector fields. This then allows us to give the following definition.

Definition 3.23

(Hamiltonian Vector Field)
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
a function. Define the
Hamiltonian vector field
of
f
, denote
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, by

V_{f}(g)=\{f,g\}

for all
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold. Then the map

C^{\infty}(M)\to\mathfrak{X}(M);\quad f\mapsto V_{f}

is a Lie algebra homomorphism.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the Jacobi identity. Given
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
and a test function
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we have

\displaystyle[V_{f},V_{g}]h

\displaystyle=V_{f}(V_{g}h)-V_{g}(V_{f}h)

\displaystyle=\{f,\{g,h\}\}-\{g,\{f,h\}\}

\displaystyle=\{f,\{g,h\}\}+\{g,\{h,f\}\}

\displaystyle=-\{h,\{f,g\}\}

\displaystyle=\{\{f,g\},h\}

\displaystyle=V_{\{f,g\}}h,

where on the third and fifth lines we used antisymmetry of the Poisson bracket and on line four we used the Jacobi identity.
∎

Returning back to the symplectic case, suppose we have a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
. By definition,
\omega
defines an isomorphism of vector bundles

\omega^{\flat}:TM\to T^{*}M

Write
\omega^{\#}=(\omega^{\flat})^{-1}
. Thus, given two functions
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we can define

\{f,g\}:=\omega^{\#}(df)(g)

(36)

It’s then easy to see that since
\omega
is closed, that
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
defines a Lie bracket. We can summarize our discussion as follows.

Proposition 3.25
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold.

(1)

The pairing

\{\cdot,\cdot\}:C^{\infty}(M)\times C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(M);\quad(f,g)\mapsto\{f,g\}

defined by Equation (
36
) defines a Poisson structure on
M
.

(2)

Given any function
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, the Hamiltonian vector field
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
defined by Definition
3.23
is the unique vector field satisfying

df=\omega(V_{f},\cdot).

(3)

For any two functions
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we have

\omega(V_{f},V_{g})=-\{f,g\}.

Example 3.26
.

Recall that thanks to Theorem
3.17
all symplectic manifolds are locally symplectomorphic to standard symplectic
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
for some
n
. Thus, to understand what Hamiltonian vector fields look like locally, it suffices to look at standard symplectic
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. In which case, fix standard coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
so that

\omega_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}dx_{i}\wedge dy_{i}.

If we let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
be a function, then

df=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}dx_{i}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}dy_{i}\bigg).

It’s easy to see that

\displaystyle\omega_{0}^{\flat}\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=dy_{i}

\displaystyle\omega_{0}^{\flat}\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\bigg)

\displaystyle-dx_{i}

Hence, the unique vector field
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
satisfying

df=\omega^{\flat}(V_{f})

will have the form

V_{f}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\bigg)

This also allows us to easily determine the local form of the Poisson bracket on a symplectic manifold. Given functions
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
, the standard Poisson bracket on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
has the form

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{0}:=V_{f}(g)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial x_{i}}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial y_{i}}\bigg)

In terms of a bi-vector
\pi_{0}\in\Gamma(\bigwedge^{2}TM)
, we can easily deduce

\pi_{0}=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}.

Example 3.27
.

Not all Poisson manifolds are symplectic. For instance, the bivector
\pi\in\Gamma(\bigwedge^{2}T\mathbb{R}^{2})
given by

\pi=r^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y},

where
r=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
. It’s easily verified that
\pi
is a Poisson bivector, however it cannot come from a symplectic form
\pi=0
at
(x,y)=(0,0)
which clearly cannot happen given the local form a symplectic Poisson bivector given in Example
3.26
. However, provided we agree that points are trivial symplectic manifolds, we do have a canonical partition into submanifolds

\mathbb{R}^{2}=\{0\}\bigsqcup(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\})

where the restriction of the Poisson bivector is symplectic. For instance, writing
S=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
, it’s easy to see that
\pi
is tangent to
S
and that the induced map

\pi^{\#}:T^{*}S\to TS;\quad\alpha\mapsto\pi(\alpha,\cdot)

is an isomorphism of vector bundles. Writing
\pi^{\flat}
for the inverse, map it’s easy to see that the induced
2
-form
\omega_{S}\in\Omega^{2}(S)
given by

\omega_{S}(v,w):=\pi(\pi^{\flat}(v),\pi^{\flat}(w))

is symplectic. In fact, in coordinates

\omega_{S}=-\frac{dx\wedge dy}{r^{2}}.

Definition 3.28

(Symplectic Submanifold of Poisson Structure)
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold. A submanifold
S\subseteq M
is said to be
symplectic
if

(1)

\pi
is tangent to
S
, i.e.
\pi|_{S}\in\Gamma(\bigwedge^{2}TS)

(2)

The restriction of the map

\pi^{\#}:T^{*}M\to TM;\quad\alpha\mapsto\pi(\alpha,\cdot)

to
T^{*}S
is defines an isomorphism of vector bundles

\pi^{\#}|_{T^{*}S}:T^{*}S\to TS

This property that a Poisson manifold decomposes into a disjoint union of symplectic submanifolds is a general feature of Poisson structures.

Theorem 3.29

(
[
Wei83
]
)
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold. Then for any point
x\in M
, there exists unique connected symplectic submanifold
S\subseteq M
containing
x
with the following properties.

(1)

If
R\subseteq M
is another connected symplectic submanifold containing
x
, then
R\subseteq S
as an open subset.

(2)

T_{x}S
is spanned by Hamiltonian vector fields
V_{f}(x)
for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

We call
S
the
symplectic leaf containing
x
.

Remark 3.30
.

Of course if
(M,\omega)
is a symplectic manifold, then the symplectic leaves are simply the connected components of
M
.

Example 3.31
.

As an interesting and non-trivial example, let’s return to coadjoint orbits of a connected Lie group. Fix a connected Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and dual
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. There is a canonical Poisson structure on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
called the Kostant-Kirillov-Souriau (KKS) Poisson structure, denoted
\pi_{KKS}
. To define
\pi_{KKS}
, identify
T^{*}\mathfrak{g}^{*}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times\mathfrak{g}
. Using this identification, we can define a bracket on
\Omega^{1}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
given by

[\alpha,\beta](p):=[\alpha(p),\beta(p)]_{\mathfrak{g}},\quad\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{1}(\mathfrak{g}*),\ p\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}

where
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{g}}
is the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}
. Using this, we now define

\pi_{KKS}(\alpha,\beta)(p):=\langle p,[\alpha(p),\beta(p)]\rangle,\quad\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{1}(\mathfrak{g}^{*}),\ p\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}.

(37)

The fact that
\pi_{KKS}
satisfies the axioms of a Lie bracket on functions is a consequence of the fact that
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{g}}
is a Lie bracket. Now, let us see why the coadjoint orbits are the symplectic leaves for this Poisson structure.

A choice of a basis
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}
defines a coordinate system on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Using this and Equation (
35
), we have for any
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
and
p\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
that

V_{f}(p)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\xi_{i}}\text{ad}_{\xi_{i}}^{*}(p).

This allows us to conclude that (1) the Hamiltonian vector fields are tangent to the coadjoint orbits and (2) the set of Hamiltonian vector fields is spanned by the fundamental vector fields. Putting these two facts together, we deduce that the coadjoint orbits are precisely the symplectic leaves of
\pi_{KKS}
.

Example 3.32
.

More generally, give a Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},[\cdot,\cdot])
we can define a
linear Poisson structure
on its dual
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
given as follows. For
f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
, we identify
df,dg:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}
. We then define

\pi_{x}(d_{x}f,d_{y}g):=\langle x,[d_{x}f,d_{x}g]\rangle

This endows
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
with a Poisson structure called a
linear Poisson structure
.

8
Hamiltonian Spaces and Reduction

We now begin to combine the equivariant geometry of Chapter
2
with the symplectic geometry developed thus far. This leads to Hamiltonian spaces. These can be motivated either from a purely symplectic point of view or from a physics point of view via Noether’s Theorem. For sake of space, I will confine myself to the symplectic picture. The idea is to suppose we have a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
and a Lie group
G
acting on
M
symplectically. That is, for each
g\in G
, we have

g^{*}\omega=\omega.

Then, as we saw in Chapter
2
, each Lie algebra element
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}=\text{Lie}(G)
induces a vector field
\xi_{M}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
. Since the action of
G
preserves the symplectic form, it follows that the flow of
\xi_{M}
preserves
\omega
. That is,

L_{\xi_{M}}\omega=0,

where
L
denotes the Lie derivative operator. Another class of vector field which preserves the symplectic form are the Hamiltonian vector fields. Thus, the most natural kind of action is one where the fundamental vector fields are also Hamiltonian, i.e. for each
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
there exists a function
J^{\xi}\in C^{\infty}(M)
so that
V_{J^{\xi}}=\xi_{M}
. Combining these functions together and imposing further conditions leads to the following definition.

Definition 3.33

(Hamiltonian
G
-space)
.

A
Hamiltonian
G
-space
consists of a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
and a map
J:M\to\mathfrak{g}
, called the
momentum map
, such that the following holds.

(1)

M
is a
G
-space and the
G
-action preserves
\omega
.

(2)

J
is
G
-equivariant with respect to the coadjoint action of
G
on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

(3)

(Hamiltonian Condition) If
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
and we write
\xi_{M}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
for the induced fundamental vector field and

J^{\xi}:M\to\mathbb{R};\quad x\mapsto\langle J(x),\xi\rangle,

then

dJ^{\xi}-\omega(\xi_{M},\cdot)=0.

We will write
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
for a Hamiltonian
G
-space. If
G
acts properly on
M
, say
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a
proper Hamiltonian
G
-space
.

Definition 3.34

(Isomorphism of Hamiltonian
G
-Spaces)
.

Let
J_{1}:(M_{1},\omega_{1})\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
J_{2}:(M_{2},\omega_{2})\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be two Hamiltonian
G
-spaces. An
isomorphism
between them is a
G
-equivariant symplectomorphism
\Phi:M_{1}\to M_{2}
such that the diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{2}}

Example 3.35
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
p\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Then the inclusion

\iota:\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{*}

of the coadjoint orbit through
p
is a momentum map with respect to the KKS symplectic structure
\omega_{KKS}\in\Omega^{2}(\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p))
from Example
3.18
. By definition,
\omega_{KKS}
is preserved by the
G
-action on
\text{Ad}_{G}^{*}(p)
, so we only have to show
\iota
is momentum map. Fixing
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, recall that

\xi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}(q)=-\text{ad}_{\xi}^{*}(q).

In particular,
\xi_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
is linear in
q
. Hence,

Example 3.36
.

Let
G
be a Lie group,
\mathfrak{g}=\text{Lie}(G)
its Lie algebra, and
Q
a proper
G
-space. The cotangent bundle
T^{*}Q
then has a canonical Hamiltonian
G
-space structure

J:(T^{*}Q,\omega_{can})\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}

which we describe now. Write
\tau_{Q}:T^{*}Q\to Q
for the bundle map,
\theta_{Q}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}Q)
for the Liouville
1
-form and
\omega=-d\theta_{Q}
for the canonical symplectic form as in Example
3.16
.

First, recall from Example
2.25
that
T^{*}Q
has a canonical proper
G
-equivariant vector bundle structure given by

G\times T^{*}Q\to T^{*}Q;\quad(g,\alpha)\mapsto g\cdot\alpha

where for
\alpha\in T_{q}^{*}Q
and
v\in T_{g\cdot q}Q

\langle g\cdot\alpha,v\rangle=\langle\alpha,T_{q}g^{-1}(v)\rangle.

This action preserves the Lioville
1
-form. Indeed, given
q\in Q
,
\alpha\in T_{q}^{*}Q
,
v\in T_{\alpha}(T^{*}Q)
, and
g\in G
, we have

\displaystyle(g^{*}\theta)_{\alpha}(v)

\displaystyle=\theta_{g\cdot\alpha}(T_{\alpha}g(v))

\displaystyle=\langle g\cdot\alpha,T_{g\cdot\alpha}\tau\circ T_{\alpha}g(v)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle g\cdot\alpha,T_{q}g\circ T_{\alpha}\tau(v)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha,T_{\alpha}\tau(v)\rangle

\displaystyle=\theta_{\alpha}(v).

Thus, the action by
G
also preserves the symplectic form
\omega=-d\theta
.

Now to describe the momentum map. Fix
\alpha\in T^{*}Q
and define
J(\alpha)\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by

\langle J(\alpha),\xi\rangle=\langle\alpha,\xi_{Q}(\tau(\alpha))\rangle,

(38)

where
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\xi_{Q}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
is the associated fundamental vector field on
Q
. To see that
J
is a momentum map, fix
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. By definition of the Liouville
1
-form, we have

J^{\xi}=\theta(\xi_{T^{*}Q})

where
\xi_{T^{*}Q}\in\mathfrak{X}(T^{*}Q)
is the fundamental vector field associated to
\xi
. Note that by construction,
\theta
is an invariant
1
-form and thus,

L_{\xi_{T^{*}Q}}\theta=0

where
L_{\xi_{T^{*}Q}}
is the Lie derivative. By Cartan’s magic formula (see for instance
[
Lee13
, Theorem 14.35]
)

0=d\theta(\xi_{T^{*}Q},\cdot)+d(\theta(\xi_{T^{*}Q})

and hence

dJ^{\xi}=d(\theta(\xi_{T^{*}Q}))=\omega(\xi_{T^{*}Q},\cdot).

Thus,
J
is a momentum map.

As we will be studying cotangent bundles in great detail, let’s give a name to the above construction.

Definition 3.37

(Hamiltonian Lift)
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
Q
a proper
G
-space. Call the proper Hamiltonian
G
-space

J:(T^{*}Q,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}

constructed in the above example the
Hamiltonian lift
of the action on
Q
.

9
Linear Symplectic Representations

To understand the local structure of Hamiltonian spaces, it will be necessary to move to describe the infinitesimal structure associated to symplectic representations.

Definition 3.38

(Symplectic Representation)
.

Let
G
be a Lie group. A
symplectic representation
is a symplectic vector space
(V,\omega)
together with a
G
-representation structure on
V
which preserves the symplectic form.

We will be making good use of a modified version of the Slice Theorem (i.e Theorem
2.13
) for proper Hamiltonian spaces, and hence our interest will primarily lie with symplectic representations of compact Lie groups. Now let us establish a foundational property of symplectic representations that will be quite prominent when we discuss symplectic reduction.

Proposition 3.39
.

Let
K
be a compact Lie group and
(V,\omega)
a symplectic
K
-representation. Then the fixed point set
V^{K}
is a symplectic subspace.

Proof.

Since the action by
K
on
V
is linear, it immediately follows that
V^{K}
is a linear subspace. By assumption,
\omega
is
K
-invariant. In particular, this means it defines an isomorphism of
K
-representations

\omega^{\#}:V\to V^{*};\quad v\mapsto\omega(v,\cdot)

and hence a linear isomorphism

\omega^{\#}:V^{K}\to(V^{*})^{K}.

Using averaging, we can easily show that the natural map

(V^{*})^{K}\to(V^{K})^{*}

induced by restricting the dual of the inclusion
V^{K}\hookrightarrow V
is a linear isomorphism. In particular, if
v\in V
we can then find
\lambda\in(V^{*})^{K}
so that
\lambda(v)\neq 0
. Using the isomorphism
\omega^{\#}
,
\lambda
corresponds to some
w\in V^{K}
. By definition this then means that

\omega(v,w)=-\lambda(v)\neq 0.

Thus,
V^{K}
is symplectic.
∎

Continuing on with the theme of reduction, let’s now apply our results about linear reduction to invariant subspaces.

Proposition 3.40
.

Let
K
be a compact Lie group,
(V,\omega)
a finite dimensional symplectic representations, and
C\subseteq V
a sub
K
-representation. Then the following holds.

(1)

The symplectic complement
C^{\omega}
is a sub
K
-representation.

(2)

The linear reduction
V_{0}=C/(C\cap C^{\omega})
has a canonical symplectic representation structure.

Proof.

(1)

This follows from the fact that
K
preserves the symplectic form. If
v\in C^{\omega}
and
k\in K
, then for any
c\in C
we have

\omega(k\cdot v,c)=\omega(v,k\cdot c)=0

since
C
is closed under the action of
K
. Hence,
k\cdot v\in C^{\omega}
and so
C^{\omega}
is a sub-representation.

(2)

Since both
C
and
C^{\omega}
are sub-representations, so is the intersection
C\cap C^{\omega}
. And thus, we have a canonical action on the reduced space
V_{0}
given by

K\times V_{0}\to V_{0};\quad(k,[c])\mapsto[k\cdot c].

To see that this action preserves the reduced symplectic form
\omega_{0}
, let
\pi_{0}:C\to V_{0}
be the quotient map. Clearly
\pi_{0}
is equivariant, thus if
k\in K
, then

\pi_{0}^{*}k^{*}\omega_{0}=k^{*}\pi_{0}^{*}\omega_{0}=k^{*}(\omega|_{C})=\omega|_{C}

Thus, by definition of
\omega_{0}
we have
k^{*}\omega_{0}=\omega_{0}
. Hence
(V_{0},\omega_{0})
is a symplectic
K
-representation.

∎

Corollary 3.41
.

Let
K
be a compact Lie group and
(V,\omega)
a symplectic
K
-representation. Then the symplectic complement
(V^{K})^{\omega}
is a symplectic sub-representation of
V
.

Proof.

Since
V^{K}
is symplectic, then by Proposition
3.40
, so is the complement
(V^{K})^{\omega}
. Furthermore, by item (1) of Proposition
3.40

(V^{K})^{\omega}
is a sub-representation.
∎

As it turns out, symplectic representations have a canonical momentum map called the
quadratic momentum map
. To set this up, suppose
K
is a compact Lie group and
(V,\omega)
a symplectic representation. Since
V
is a vector space, we have a canonical identification
TV=V\times V
. Hence, if
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
is a Lie algebra element and
\xi_{V}\in\mathfrak{X}(V)
the associated fundamental vector field, then for each
v\in V
we can identify
\xi_{V}(v)\in V
. This now allows us to show the following.

Lemma 3.42
.

Let
K
be a compact Lie group and
(V,\omega)
a symplectic representation. Then the map
J_{V}:V\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
defined by

\langle J_{V}(v),\xi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\omega(\xi_{V}(v),v),\quad\xi\in\mathfrak{k},\ v\in V

(39)

is a momentum map for the
K
-action. We call
J
the
quadratic momentum map

Proof.

To see that this is a momentum map, let
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
and
v\in V
. We show

dJ_{V}^{\xi}+\omega(\xi_{V},\cdot)=0.

Indeed, fix
v\in V
and identify
w\in V
with an element of
T_{v}V
by the action

w(f)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}f(v+tw),

where
f\in C^{\infty}(V)
. Then on one hand we have

d_{v}J_{V}^{\xi}(w)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}J_{V}^{\xi}(v+tw)=\frac{\omega(\xi_{V}(v),w)+\omega(\xi_{V}(w),v)}{2},

where we used the linearity of
\xi_{V}
. Since the action of
K
on
(V,\omega)
is symplectic, we have

\omega(\xi_{V}(w),v)=-\omega(w,\xi_{V}(v))

which, together with the antisymmetry of
\omega
returns

d_{v}J_{V}^{\xi}(w)=\frac{\omega(\xi_{V}(v),w)+\omega(\xi_{V}(v),w)}{2}=\omega(\xi_{V}(v),w).

Hence

dJ_{V}-\omega(\xi_{V},\cdot)=0.

We also need to show
J_{V}
is equivariant, but this is a consequence of the fact that for any
k\in K
,
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
, and
v\in V
we have

k\cdot\xi_{V}\cdot k^{-1}\cdot v=(\text{Ad}_{k}\xi)\cdot v.

From this and the
K
-invariance of
J_{V}
, it immediately follows that

J_{V}(k\cdot v)=\text{Ad}^{*}_{k}J_{V}(v).

∎

Let us now record some facts about linear symplectic representations that will come in handy later on.

Lemma 3.43
.

Let
K
be a compact Lie group and
(V,\omega)
a symplectic
K
-representation.

(1)

If
J_{V}:V\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
is the quadratic momentum map, then
J_{V}^{-1}(0)^{K}=V^{K}
.

(2)

If
J_{W}:W\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
denotes induced quadratic momentum map, then

J_{V}^{-1}(0)=J_{W}^{-1}(0)+V^{K}.

Proof.

(1)

First, the statement makes sense since
J_{V}
is equivariant. Hence, since
G
fixes
0\in\mathfrak{k}^{*}
, it follows that
K
acts on
J_{V}^{-1}(0)
. In particular, we clearly then have
J_{V}^{-1}(0)^{K}\subseteq V^{K}
. On the otherhand, if
v\in V^{K}
then we see for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
that

\xi_{V}(v)=0.

In particular, this implies that

\langle J_{V}(v),\xi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\omega(\xi_{V}(v),v)=\frac{1}{2}\omega(0,v)=0.

Since
v
and
\xi
we arbitrary, we conclude that
V^{K}\subseteq J_{V}^{-1}(0)
.

(2)

Since
W
and
V^{K}
are both symplectic spaces and are are symplectic complements, then they are also linear complements. That is,
V=V^{K}+W
and
V^{K}\cap W=\{0\}
. Hence, for every
v\in V
there are unique
v^{K}\in V^{K}
and
w\in W
so that

v=v^{K}+w.

Now, observe that since
K
is a sub-representation, if
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
, then

\xi_{V}|_{W}=\xi_{W}.

With this, we see that if
v\in V^{K}
and
w\in W
, then for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
, we have

\langle J_{V}(v+w),\xi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\omega(\xi_{V}(v+w),v+w)=\frac{1}{2}\omega(\xi_{W}(w),w)=\langle J_{W}(w),\xi\rangle.

In particular, we see that if
v\in V^{K}
and
w\in W
, then
J_{V}(v+w)=0
if and only if
J_{W}(w)=0
. The result then follows.

∎

10
Reduction and Local Normal Forms

Our main interest for Hamiltonian
G
-spaces is going to be performing symplectic reduction. We now introduce this foundational concept below.

Definition 3.44

(Symplectic Reduction)
.

Let
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
we define the
symplectic reduction
of
M
at level
0
of the momentum map to be the quotient

M_{0}:=J^{-1}(0)/G.

We will write
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
for the quotient map. Call
\pi_{0}
the
reduction map

Example 3.45
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
Q
a proper
G
-space on which
G
acts freely, and let
J:T^{*}Q\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be the Hamiltonian lift as in Definition
3.37
. Let’s first determine
J^{-1}(0)
. Given
\alpha\in T^{*}Q
by definition
\alpha\in J^{-1}(0)
if and only if
\langle J(\alpha),\xi_{Q}\rangle=0
for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, where
\xi_{Q}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
is the associated fundamental vector field. Recall from Example
2.26
that since
G
acts freely, the fundamental vector fields span a
G
-invariant subbundle
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}\subseteq TQ
. Thus, we have shown that
J^{-1}(0)
is the annihilator of
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}
, that is

J^{-1}(0)=\mathfrak{g}_{Q}^{\circ}\subseteq T^{*}Q,

Since
G
acts freely on
Q
, we also have that the reduced space

(T^{*}Q)_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G=\mathfrak{g}_{Q}^{\circ}/G

is a vector bundle over
Q/G
. Which vector bundle? Well as we will show in more generality later on, it is precisely
T^{*}(Q/G)
.

In the case where a compact Lie group is acting freely, the so-called “miracle of reduction” occurs. Let
K
be a compact Lie group and
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
a Hamiltonian
K
-space. Suppose
0
is a regular value of
J
and that
K
acts freely on
J^{-1}(0)
. Then it is a straightforward calculation to show for any
x\in J^{-1}(0)
that

(T_{x}J^{-1}(0))^{\omega_{x}}=T_{x}(K\cdot x).

This is the “miracle of reduction” for the following reasons. First,
J^{-1}(0)
is a coisotropic submanifold and for each
x\in J^{-1}(0)
, the tangent space of the quotient space has the form

T_{[x]}(J^{-1}(0)/K)=T_{x}J^{-1}(0)/T_{x}(K\cdot x)

which is precisely the linear reduction of
T_{x}J^{-1}(0)
. Thus, we have mostly shown the following classic result.

Theorem 3.46

(Marsden-Weinstein-Meyer Reduction
[
MW74
,
Mey73
]
)
.

Let
K
be a compact Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
and
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
a Hamiltonian
K
-space. Suppose
0
is a regular value of
J
and that
K
acts freely on
J^{-1}(0)
. Then the following holds.

(1)

J^{-1}(0)\subseteq M
is an embedded coisotropic submanifold.

(2)

The reduction
M_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/K
has a canonical symplectic form
\omega_{0}\in\Omega^{2}(M_{0})
such that

\pi_{0}^{*}\omega_{0}=\omega|_{J^{-1}(0)},

where
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
is the reduction map.

Now we are going to be dealing with proper actions by groups which need not act freely. Thus, we will need a local normal form to properly discuss this more general setting. An application of Theorem
3.46
together with Reduction in Stages (see for instance
[
MMO+07
]
for a comprehensive source) allows us to show the following.

Lemma 3.47

(Local Model of Proper Hamiltonian Space
[
SL91
]
)
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
K\leq G
a compact subgroup. Write
\mathfrak{k}
for the Lie algebra of
K
and

\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}=\{p\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\ |\ \langle p,\xi\rangle=0\text{ for all }\xi\in\mathfrak{k}\}

for the annihilator of
\mathfrak{k}
. Suppose we have the following data
Suppose we have the following data.

(i)

A linear symplectic representation
(V,\omega_{V})
of
K
.

(ii)

A
K
-equivariant splitting
\mathfrak{p}:\mathfrak{k}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
of the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{k}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Write
\pi:G\times\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times V\to G\times_{K}(\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times V)
for the quotient map and
\omega_{T^{*}G}\in\Omega^{2}(G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})
for the canonical symplectic form.

(1)

G\times_{K}(\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times V)
is canonically a symplectic manifold with unique
G
-invariant symplectic form
\omega
satisfying

\pi^{*}\omega=\omega_{T^{*}G}\times\omega_{V}|_{G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times V}.

(2)

Writing
J_{V}:V\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
for the quadratic momentum map from Example
3.42
, the map

J:G\times_{K}(\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times V)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*};\quad[g,q,v]\mapsto\text{Ad}_{g}^{*}(q+\mathfrak{p}\circ J_{V}(v))

is a momentum map.

Now that we have our local model, let’s now discuss the local normal form and how to obtain it. Fix a Hamiltonian
G
-space
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and let
x\in M
. Then the tangent space
T_{x}M
has a natural symplectic
G_{x}
representation structure. Clearly this action preserves the tangent space to the orbit
T_{x}(G\cdot x)
. Thus, by Proposition
3.40
the reduction of the complement
T_{x}(G\cdot x)^{\omega_{x}}
has a canonical symplectic action by
G_{x}
as well. This allows us to give the following definition.

Definition 3.48

(Symplectic Normal Space)
.

Let
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a Hamiltonian
G
-space and
x\in M
. Define the
symplectic normal space
to the orbit through
x
, denoted
S\nu_{x}(M,G)
, to be the linear reduction of
T_{x}(G\cdot x)^{\omega_{x}}
, that is

S\nu_{x}(M,G):=\frac{T_{x}(G\cdot x)^{\omega_{x}}}{T_{x}(G\cdot x)^{\omega_{x}}\cap T_{x}(G\cdot x)}

(40)

Write
J_{x}:S\nu_{x}(M,G)\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}
for the induced quadratic momentum map as in Lemma
3.42

We now have enough set-up to state a version of the slice theorem for Hamiltonian spaces.

Theorem 3.49

(Local Normal Form for Momentum Map Along Zero Level-Set
[
SL91
]
)
.

Let
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space and
x\in J^{-1}(0)
. Write
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
for the Lie algebra of the stabilizer group
G_{x}
and suppose
\mathfrak{p}:\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a splitting as in Lemma
3.47
. Then there exists the following data.

(1)

G
-invariant neighbourhoods
U\subseteq M
of
x
and
V\subseteq G\times_{G_{x}}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G))
of
[e,0,0]

(ii)

An isomorphism of Hamiltonian
G
-space
\Phi:U\to V
with respect to the momentum map

M\times_{G_{x}}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times V)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*};\quad[g,q,v]\mapsto\text{Ad}_{g}^{*}(q+\mathfrak{p}\circ J_{x}(v)),

where
J_{x}:S\nu_{x}(M,G)\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}
is the quadratic momentum map, such that
\Phi(x)=[e,0,0]
.

Definition 3.50

(Symplectic Slice Neighbourhood)
.

Let
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space and
x\in M
. Call neighbourhoods
U\subseteq M
and
V\subseteq G\times_{G_{x}}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G))
together with the isomorphism of Hamiltonian
G
-spaces
\Phi:U\to V
a
symplectic slice neighbourhood
about
x
.

As a consequence of this, we get the following extension of Marsden-Weinstein-Meyer reduction.

Corollary 3.51
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space. Suppose
J^{-1}(0)\neq\emptyset
and any two points in
J^{-1}(0)
have conjugate stabilizers. Then the following holds.

(1)

J^{-1}(0)
is an embedded submanifold.

(2)

The reduction
M_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G
is a canonically a symplectic manifold with symplectic structure
\omega_{0}\in\Omega^{2}(M_{0})
satisfying

\pi_{0}^{*}\omega_{0}=\omega|_{J^{-1}(0)},

where
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
is the reduction map.

Proof.

Since both statements are local in nature, by Theorem
3.49
we may assume that we are in a local model of a Hamiltonian
G
-space. That is,
M=G\times_{K}(\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times V)
for a compact subgroup
K\leq G
and symplectic
K
-representation
(V,\omega)
with
J:M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
having the form

J:G\times_{K}(\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times V)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*};\quad[g,q,v]\mapsto\text{Ad}_{g}^{*}(q+\mathfrak{p}\circ J_{V}(v)),

where
\mathfrak{p}:\mathfrak{k}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a splitting and
J_{V}:V\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
is the quadratic momentum map.

(1)

Since the image of
\mathfrak{p}
lies in the kernel of the natural projection
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
, it follows that

J^{-1}(0)=G\times_{K}(\{0\}\times J_{V}^{-1}(0)).

Since
[e,0,0]\in J^{-1}(0)
, by assumption we must have that
J^{-1}(0)\subseteq M_{(K)}
. As we saw in Proposition
2.11
,

G\times_{K}(\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times V)_{(K)}=G\times_{K}((\mathfrak{k}^{\circ})^{K}\times V^{K})

and hence by Lemma
3.43
,

J^{-1}(0)=G\times_{K}(\{0\}\times J_{V}^{-1}(0)^{K})=G\times_{K}(\{0\}\times V^{K}).

(41)

This is trivially equivariantly diffeomorphic to
(G/K)\times V^{K}
and hence
J^{-1}(0)
is an embedded submanifold.

(2)

Using Equation (
41
), we obtain that

M_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G\cong V^{K}.

This shows both that the reduced space
M_{0}
has a natural smooth manifold structure. Furthermore, since
V
is symplectic, we have by Proposition
3.39
that
V^{K}
is a symplectic subspace and thus
M_{0}
has a natural symplectic structure.

∎

As a final consequence of the the normal form theorem, we can give a infinitesimal description to the tangent space of a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space which will come in handy when we pass to the singular setting.

Proposition 3.52
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space, and
x\in J^{-1}(0)
. Then a symplectic slice neighbourhood about
x
induces a
G_{x}
-equivariant isomorphism of linear symplectic
G_{x}
-representations

\phi:(T_{x}M,\omega_{x})\to(T^{*}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})\times S\nu_{x}(M,G),\omega_{0})

where
S\nu_{x}(M,G)
is the symplectic normal space to the orbit through
x
as in Definition
3.48
and
\omega_{0}=\omega_{T^{*}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})}\times\omega_{S\nu_{x}(M,G)}
.

Proof.

Fixing
x\in J^{-1}(0)
, let

\Phi:U\subseteq M\to V\subseteq G\times_{G_{x}}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G))

be a symplectic slice neighbourhood around
x
. In particular, we obtain a
G_{x}
-equivariant linear symplectomorphism

T_{x}\Phi:T_{x}M\to T_{[e,0,0]}G\times_{G_{x}}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G)).

Since
G_{x}
acts freely on
G\times\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G)
, we have

\displaystyle T_{[e,0,0]}G\times_{G_{x}}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G))

\displaystyle\cong T_{(e,0,0)}(G\times\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G))/T_{(e,0,0)}(G_{x}\cdot(e,0,0))

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})\times\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G).

The dual of the projection
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x}
provides a
G_{x}
-equivariant isomorphism
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}
and hence
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})\times\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\cong T^{*}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})
. Since the symplectic form on the local normal form is given by the reduction of the product of the symplectic form on
T^{*}G
and
S\nu_{x}(M,G)
, the result then follows.
∎

11
Non-Trivial Examples of Reduction

11.1
Toric Manifolds and the Delzant Construction

A beautiful application of the theory of reduction is to the theory of toric manifolds. These are a particular kind of Hamiltonian space and, as we shall see, they can be reconstructed from the image of the momentum map. Unless otherwise stated, everything below can be found in Delzant’s original paper
[
Del88
]
. For an excellent overview in English, see Chapters 1-3 in
[
Gui94
]
.

Definition 3.53

(Toric Manifold)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a torus, i.e. a compact connected abelian Lie group which is isomorphic to
(S^{1})^{n}
for some
n
. Write
\mathfrak{t}
for the Lie algebra of
\mathbb{T}
. A
toric
\mathbb{T}
-manifold
is a Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}
-space
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{t}
such that

(1)

\mathbb{T}
acts effectively on
M
, i.e.

\bigcap_{x\in M}\mathbb{T}_{x}=\{e\}

and

(2)

\dim(M)=2\dim(\mathbb{T})
.

Delzant’s construction takes as input a particular kind of polytope and produces a toric manifold. In order to set up this construction, we first give a definition of this special class of polytope.

Definition 3.54

(Delzant Polytope)
.

Consider a subset
\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We say
\Delta
is a
Delzant polytope
if the following holds.

(1)

There exists
inward normals

v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
so that

\Delta=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\ |\ \langle x,v_{i}\rangle\geq\lambda_{i}\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,N\}

for some
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

The map linear map
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined by
\pi(e_{i})=v_{i}
, where
e_{1},\dots,e_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is the standard basis, is a surjective linear map such that

\pi(\mathbb{Z}^{N})=\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Remark 3.55
.

Given a Delzant polytope

\Delta=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\ |\ \langle x,v_{i}\rangle\geq\lambda_{i}\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,N\},

we can use the normals to cut out the
facets
. Namely, given any set of indices
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
, we define

F_{I}:=\{x\in\Delta\ |\ \langle x,v_{i}\rangle=\lambda_{i}\text{ for all }i\in I\}.

If non-empty, this is again another polytope inside of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Furthermore, it’s easy to see that if
I_{1}\subseteq I_{2}
are two sets of indices, then

F_{I_{2}}\subseteq F_{I_{1}}.

The vertices can be identified as the minimal facets with respect to inclusion. Finally, as it will be helpful later on, let us define the
open facets
of
\Delta
to be subsets of the form

\mathring{F}_{I}:=F_{I}\setminus\bigcup_{I\subsetneq J}F_{J},

i.e. what remains from a facet after removing smaller facets with respect to inclusion. These are submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Let’s now fix a Delzant polytope
\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with normals
v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
so that

\Delta=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ \langle x,v_{i}\rangle\geq\lambda_{i}\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,N\}

(42)

for some
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
. Delzant’s construction for a toric manifold
M_{\Delta}
is as follows. Let
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the linear map defined by

\pi(e_{i})=v_{i}\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,N,

where
e_{1},\dots,e_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is the standard basis. Furthermore, let
\mathfrak{k}=\ker(\pi)
. Then we get an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(43)

Since
\pi(\mathbb{Z}^{N})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, it follows that
\pi
induces a group homomorphism

\pi:T^{N}=\mathbb{R}^{N}/\mathbb{Z}^{N}\to T^{n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}/\mathbb{Z}^{n}

and, furthermore, the kernel
K
is a subtorus of
T^{N}
and its Lie algebra can be canonically identified with
\mathfrak{k}
. Hence, we get a exact sequence of Lie groups.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(44)

Identify
T^{N}=(S^{1})^{N}\subseteq(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{N}
, and let
\omega
be the canonical symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. The action

T^{N}\times\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{C}^{N};\quad((t_{1},\dots,t_{N}),(z_{1},\dots,z_{N}))\mapsto(t_{1}z_{1},\dots,t_{N}z_{N})

has a canonical momentum map

\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N};\quad(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(|z_{1}|^{2},\dots,|z_{N}|^{2})

(45)

Since
T^{N}
is abelian, we can shift
J
by a constant in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and still have a momentum map. For this reason, we will define

J:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N};\quad(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(|z_{1}|^{2},\dots,|z_{N}|^{2})-\lambda.

(46)

As a final step towards the construction, let
\iota:\mathfrak{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
be the inclusion and let
\iota^{*}:(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{*}\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
be its dual. Then the map

\iota^{*}\circ J:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}

(47)

is a momentum map for the induced
K
-action.

Lemma 3.56

(
[
Del88
]
)
.

K
acts freely on
(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)
.

Proof.

This amounts to showing that if
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\in(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)
, then
z_{j}\neq 0
for all
j
. A nice proof of this fact can be found in
[
Gui94
, Theorem 1.6]

∎

Consequently, using Marsden-Weinstein-Meyer reduction (Theorem
3.46
),
\phi^{-1}(0)/K
is canonically a symplectic manifold. Furthermore, we see that
(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)
is precisely the set of points in
\mathbb{C}^{N}
mapped to
\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{*}
, the annhilator of
\mathfrak{k}
. This can canonically be identified with
(\mathbb{R}^{N}/\mathfrak{k})^{*}\cong(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 3.57

(Toric Manifold from a Delzant Polytope)
.

Given a Delzant polytope
\Delta
, let
(M_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})
denote the symplectic reduction of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
along
\phi^{-1}(0)
. Furthermore, we let

J_{\Delta}:M_{\Delta}\to\mathbb{R}^{n};\quad[x]\mapsto J(x)

(48)

be the induced map.

Delzant’s construction turns out to produce all toric manifolds.

Theorem 3.58

(
[
Del88
]
)
.

J_{\Delta}:(M_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a toric
T^{n}
-manifold. If
\Delta
is compact, then so is
M_{\Delta}
. Furthermore, if
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is any toric
T^{n}
-manifold, then
\Delta=J(M)
is a Delzant polytope and as Hamiltonian
T^{n}
-spaces,
(M,\omega)\cong(M_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})
.

11.2
Reduction of Cotangent Bundles

As was hinted at in Example
3.45
, the symplectic reduction of a cotangent bundle where the underlying group is acting both properly and freely produces the cotangent bundle of the quotient space. It turns out we can push this much further as we shall see.

For the rest of this section, we will let
G
be a connected Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
,
Q
a proper
G
-space with the property that
Q=Q_{(K)}
for some compact
K\leq G
,
J:T^{*}Q\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the canonical Hamiltonian
G
-space structure on the cotangent bundle, and we will let

\mathfrak{g}_{Q}:=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\xi_{Q}\ |\ \xi\in\mathfrak{g}\}\subseteq TQ

(49)

be the invariant subbundle spanned by the fundamental vector fields from Example
2.26
.

Lemma 3.59

(
[
ER90
]
)
.

Suppose the action of
G
on
Q
is regular. That is,
Q=Q_{(K)}
for some compact subgroup
K\leq G
.

(i)

J^{-1}(0)=J^{-1}(0)_{(K)}

(ii)

(T^{*}Q)_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G
is a smooth manifold and there exists unique symplectic form
\omega_{0}\in\Omega^{2}((T^{*}Q)_{0})
such that

\pi_{0}^{*}\omega_{0}=\omega|_{J^{-1}(0)},

where
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}
is the quotient map.

(iii)

The restriction of the Liouville
1
-form
\theta_{Q}|_{J^{-1}(0)}
is basic and hence induces a
1
-form
\theta_{0}\in\Omega^{1}((T^{*}Q)_{0})
such that

\pi_{0}^{*}\theta_{0}=\theta_{Q}|_{J^{-1}(0)}.

(50)

and
d\theta_{0}=\omega_{0}
.

Proof.

(i)

Suppose
\alpha\in J^{-1}(0)
and let
g\in G_{\alpha}
. Writing
x=\tau_{Q}(\alpha)
, we see that

g\cdot x=g\cdot\tau_{Q}(\alpha)=\tau_{Q}(g\cdot\alpha)=\tau_{Q}(\alpha)=x.

Hence,
G_{\alpha}\subseteq G_{\tau_{Q}(\alpha)}\sim K
. Thus, we may assume after conjugation that
G_{\alpha}=K_{\alpha}\subseteq K
.

To show that
K\subseteq K_{\alpha}
, choose a
G
-invariant metric on
Q
so that we have a splitting

T_{x}Q=T_{x}(G\cdot x)\oplus\nu_{x}(Q,G)

Since there is only one orbit-type on
Q
, it immediately follows that
K
acts trivially on
\nu_{x}(Q,G)
. Now fix
v\in T_{x}Q
and
k\in K
. Since
K
stabilizes
x=\tau_{Q}(\alpha)
, it follows that
k\cdot\alpha\in T_{x}^{*}Q
. Hence, we may pair
k\cdot\alpha
and
v
, yielding

\langle k\cdot\alpha,v\rangle=\langle\alpha,k^{-1}\cdot v\rangle

Writing
v=\xi_{Q}(x)+w
for some
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
and
w\in\nu_{x}(Q,G)
, we have

k^{-1}\cdot v=(\text{Ad}_{k^{-1}}\xi)_{Q}(x)+w

Thus,

\langle\alpha,k^{-1}\cdot v\rangle=\langle\alpha,w\rangle

Similarly,

\langle\alpha,v\rangle=\langle\alpha,w\rangle.

Thus,
k\cdot\alpha=\alpha

(ii)

Since the orbit foliation on
Q
is regular,
J^{-1}(0)
is an embedded submanifold of
T^{*}Q
. Furthermore,
J^{-1}(0)
has only one orbit-type by Lemma
3.59
, hence the quotient
J^{-1}(0)/G
is a manifold. Therefore, by Marsden-Weinstein reduction,
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
inherits a canonical symplectic form
\omega_{0}
with

\pi_{0}^{*}\omega_{0}=\omega_{Q}|_{J^{-1}(0)}.

(iii)

This immediately follows from the fact that
\theta_{Q}
is
G
-invariant and from the uniqueness of
\omega_{0}
.

∎

Recall that
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}\subseteq TQ
from Equation (
49
) is defined to be the span of the fundamental vector fields. This is the tangent bundle of foliation by
G
-orbits on
Q
. Hence, if we write
\pi:Q\to Q/G
for the projection map, we have a short exact sequence of vector bundles over
Q

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TQ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{-1}T(Q/G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi}

where
\pi^{-1}T(Q/G)\subseteq Q\times T(Q/G)
is the pull-back bundle. Dualizing the short exact sequence, we get a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\xi_{Q})^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi}

where

\phi:\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)\to T^{*}Q

is defined by

\langle\phi(q,\beta),v\rangle=\langle\beta,T_{q}\pi(v)\rangle

for
(q,\beta)\in\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)
and
v\in T_{q}Q
. Working point-wise, we see that the image of
(\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G))_{q}
by
\phi
is precisely
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}(q)^{\circ}=J^{-1}(0)_{q}
for all
q\in Q
. Thus, we have an isomorphism of vector bundles over
Q

\phi:\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)\to J^{-1}(0).

(51)

Now to set up the next statement, write
\tau_{Q/G}:T^{*}(Q/G)\to Q/G
for the bundle projection map. By definition,

\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)=\{(q,\beta)\in Q\times T^{*}(Q/G)\ |\ \pi(q)=\tau_{Q/G}(\beta)\}

where
\pi:Q\to Q/G
is the quotient map. Hence, we have two natural maps

pr_{1}:\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)\to Q;\quad(q,\beta)\mapsto q

and

pr_{2}:\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)\to T^{*}(Q/G);\quad(q,\beta)\mapsto\beta.

Lemma 3.60
.

In the setting where
Q
has only one orbit-type, let
\theta_{Q/G}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}(Q/G))
denote the Liouville
1
-form on
T^{*}(Q/G)
. Then,

\phi^{*}\theta_{Q}=pr_{2}^{*}\theta_{Q/G}.

Proof.

Let us fix
(q,\beta)\in\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)
and
v\in T_{(q,\beta)}(\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G))
. We first have

(\phi^{*}\theta_{Q})_{(q,\beta)}(v)=(\theta_{Q})_{\phi(q,\beta)}(\phi v)=\langle\phi(q,\beta),(\tau_{Q})_{*}\phi_{*}v\rangle=\langle\beta,(\pi\circ\tau_{Q}\circ\phi)_{*}v\rangle

After a diagram chase, we have

\pi\circ\tau_{Q}\circ\phi=\tau_{Q/G}\circ pr_{2}

Hence,

(\phi^{*}\theta_{Q})_{(q,\beta)}(v)=\langle\beta,(\tau_{Q/G}\circ pr_{2})_{*}v\rangle=(\theta_{Q/G})\beta((pr_{2})_{*}v\rangle=(pr_{2}^{*}\theta_{Q})_{(q,\beta)}(v).

∎

With this fact established, we can now prove the following.

Theorem 3.61
.

Suppose
Q
has only one orbit-type. Then the map
\phi:\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)\to J^{-1}(0)
from Equation (
51
) induces a canonical symplectomorphism

\widetilde{\phi}:T^{*}(Q/G)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}

(52)

Proof.

Write
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}
for the quotient map. Since

\phi:\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)\to J^{-1}(0)

is an isomorphism of
G
-equivariant vector bundles,
\phi
induces an isomorphism between the quotients

\widetilde{\phi}:T^{*}(Q/G)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}

By Lemma
3.59
, the symplectic form on
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
has a canonical primitive
\theta_{0}
. By Lemma
3.60
, we have

\widetilde{\phi}^{*}\theta_{0}=\theta_{Q/G}

and hence
\widetilde{\phi}
is a symplectomorphism.
∎

†
†
margin:

4

Polarizations

Given the description of symplectic manifolds at the beginning of Chapter
3
as generalizations of phase space from classical physics, we may wonder what other physics concepts can be generalized and ported over to the symplectic world. Polarizations represent the generalization of momentum, or rather, momentum directions to symplectic manifolds. Indeed, an arbitrary symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
may be describable locally as a phase-space thanks to Darboux’s Theorem, but this does not mean the local notion of momentum from this description is globally coherent. The key here is that in phase space, the momentum variables carve out Lagrangian submanifolds. Then thanks to Frobenius’ Theorem, the tangent space of this foliation by momentum submanifolds gives an involutive subbundle
\mathcal{P}\subseteq TM
by Lagrangian subspaces. This in turn can be generalized further by demanding not that the polarization lie in the tangent bundle, but rather in the complexified tangent bundle
T^{\mathbb{C}}M
, giving us a notion of “complex momentum”. As we shall see in the next chapter, this concept will be key in formulating geometric quantization.

Our goal in this chapter is to not only lay out an elementary theory of polarizations, but also to develop the non-singular theory of their reduction. This is an essential idea for fusing Hamiltonian geometry with quantization.

12
Lagrangian Subspaces and Their Reductions

Before we can work out the case of general reduction of polarizations, it will be helpful to have some preliminary facts handy.

For all that follows, we will be assuming
(V,\omega)
is a finite dimensional real symplectic vector space,
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
will be the
\mathbb{C}
-linear extension of
\omega
to
V_{\mathbb{C}}:=V\otimes\mathbb{C}
, and
L\subseteq V_{\mathbb{C}}
will be a complex Lagrangian subspace. The main question we want to answer in this section is the following: given a subspace
C\subseteq V
and its reduction
\pi_{0}:C\to V_{0}
, where
V_{0}=C/(C\cap C^{\omega})
, when is

L_{0}=\pi_{0}(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})\subset(V_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}

a Lagrangian subspace? In the case where
C
is coisotropic, i.e.
C^{\omega}\subseteq C
, the answer is always yes.

Proposition 4.1
.

Suppose
C\subseteq V
is a coisotropic subspace and let
V_{0}=C/C^{\omega}
and
\pi:C\to V_{0}
be the reduction map. Then,

L_{0}:=\pi_{0}(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})\subseteq(V_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}

is a complex Lagrangian subspace.

Proof.

L_{0}
being isotropic is immediate by the definition of the reduced symplectic form
\omega_{0}
. So we just need to show
L_{0}
is coisotropic. Let
c\in C
so that
\pi_{0}(c)\in L_{0}^{\omega_{0}}
. Then, for any
\ell\in L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}}
, we have

0=\omega_{0}(\pi_{0}(c),\pi_{0}(\ell))=\omega_{0}(c,\ell).

Hence,

c\in(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})^{\omega}=L^{\omega}+C_{\mathbb{C}}^{\omega}=L+C^{\omega}_{\mathbb{C}}

Writing
c=a+b
,
a\in L
and
b\in C^{\omega}_{\mathbb{C}}
, we have that
a\in L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}}
since
C^{\omega}\subseteq C
. Thus,

\pi_{0}(c)=\pi_{0}(a)\in L_{0}.

∎

A question worth investigating here, is what happens to the type after we do reduction along a coisotropic subspace.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
C\subseteq V
be a coisotropic subspace,
L\subseteq V_{\mathbb{C}}
a complex Lagrangian subspace, and let
\pi_{0}:C\to V_{0}
be as in Proposition
4.1
.

(1)

If
L
is totally complex, i.e.
L\cap\overline{L}=\{0\}
, then
L_{0}
is also totally complex, i.e.
L_{0}\cap\overline{L_{0}}=\{0\}
.

(2)

If
L
is real, i.e.
L=\overline{L}
, then
L_{0}
is also real.

Proof.

Both (1) and (2) are consequences of the fact that
L_{0}
is obtained by intersecting a complex subspace with the complexification of a real subspace.
∎

Example 4.3
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a
4
dimensional symplectic vector space and
\{e_{1},e_{2},f_{1},f_{2}\}
a Darboux basis for
\omega
, i.e

\omega=e_{1}^{*}\wedge f_{1}^{*}+e_{2}^{*}\wedge f_{2}^{*},

where
\{e_{i}^{*},f_{i}^{*}\}
is the dual basis. Consider now the coisotropic subspace

C=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{1},e_{2},f_{1}\}.

C
is indeed coisotropic with

C^{\omega}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{2}\}

and hence the reduced space
V_{0}=C/C^{\omega}
has a canonical Darboux basis which can be identified with
\{e_{1},f_{1}\}
. Write
\pi_{0}:C\to V_{0}
for the reduction map. Now, consider the following three Lagrangian subspaces,

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{1},e_{2}\}

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{1}+if_{1},e_{2}+if_{2}\}

\displaystyle L_{3}

\displaystyle=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{1}+if_{1},e_{2}\}

\displaystyle L_{4}

\displaystyle=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{1},e_{2}+if_{2}\}

Note that
L_{1}
is real,
L_{2}
is totally complex, and
L_{3}
and
L_{4}
are mixed. Indeed, as we suspect, we see that the reduction of
L_{1}
remains real

(L_{1})_{0}=\pi_{0}(L_{1}\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{1}\},

the reduction of
L_{2}
remains complex

(L_{2})_{0}=\pi_{0}(L_{2}\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{1}+if_{1}\}.

Whereas we see that the reduction of
L_{3}

(L_{3})_{0}=\pi_{0}(L_{3}\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{1}+if_{1}\}

becomes totally complex and the reduction of
L_{4}

(L_{4})_{0}=\pi_{0}(L_{4}\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{1}\}

becomes real. So the only stable types under reduction are real and totally complex.

Before we move on to the general case, I would like to recall a result of Guillemin and Sternberg. First, a definition.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a real symplectic vector space and
L\subseteq V^{\mathbb{C}}
a complex Lagrangian subspace. Define inner-product

V^{\mathbb{C}}\times V^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C};\quad(v,w)\mapsto i\omega(v,\overline{w}),

(53)

where
\overline{w}
denotes the complex-conjugate of
w
. If the inner-product in Equation (
53
) is positive-definite when restricted to
L
, then say
L
is
positive
.

Proposition 4.5

(
[
GS82
]
)
.

Suppose
L\subseteq V\otimes\mathbb{C}
is a positive Lagrangian subspace and
C\subseteq V
a coisotropic subspace. Then,

L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}}^{\omega}=\{0\}.

Proof.

This is near verbatim the proof found in
[
GS82
]
. Let
c_{1},c_{2}\in C^{\omega}
so that
\ell=c_{1}+ic_{2}\in L
. Then,

i\omega(\ell,\overline{\ell})=2\omega(c_{1},c_{2})=0

since
C^{\omega}
is isotropic. Since
L
is assumed to be positive, it then follows that
\ell=0
.
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

Suppose
L\subseteq V\otimes\mathbb{C}
is a positive Lagrangian subspace and
C\subseteq V
is coisotropic. Then the reduced Lagrangian
L_{0}\subseteq(V_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}=(C/C^{\omega})\otimes\mathbb{C}
is a positive polarization.

Proof.

Write
\pi_{0}:C\to V_{0}
for the projection. Recall that

L_{0}=\pi_{0}(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}}).

Write
\omega_{0}
for the reduced form on
V_{0}
. Then, if
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}}
we have

i\omega_{0}(\pi_{0}(\ell_{1}),\overline{\pi_{0}(\ell_{2})})=i\omega(\ell_{1},\overline{\ell_{2}})

Suppose now that
\pi_{0}(\ell_{1})=\pi_{0}(\ell_{2})\neq 0
. Then there exists
c\in C^{\omega}_{\mathbb{C}}
with

\ell_{2}=\ell_{1}+c.

Thus,

i\omega_{0}(\pi_{0}(\ell_{1}),\pi_{0}(\ell_{2}))=i\omega(\ell_{1},\overline{\ell_{1}}+\overline{c})=i\omega(\ell_{1},\overline{\ell}_{1})>0

where we used the fact that
L
is positive and that
C^{\omega}_{\mathbb{C}}
is closed under complex conjugation.
∎

In the case of non-singular reduction, this will immediately imply that a polarization induces a reduced polarization on the reduced space, provided certain regularity conditions hold. However, it will not suffice on its own in the case of singular reduction. For that, we need a few auxiliary results.

Proposition 4.7
.

Suppose
S\subseteq V
is a symplectic subspace and suppose that

L=L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}+L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}^{\omega}.

(54)

Then
L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}
is a complex Lagrangian subspace of
S_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

Let
\omega_{S}:=\omega|_{S}
. Clearly,

L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq(L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}})^{\omega_{S}}

so
L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}
is isotropic. To show coisotropic, let
x\in(L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}})^{\omega_{S}}
and let
\ell\in L
. By the decomposition in Equation (
54
), we can write

\ell=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},

where
\ell_{1}\in L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\ell_{2}\in L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}^{\omega}
. Observe that

\omega(x,\ell)=\omega(x,\ell_{1})+\omega(x,\ell_{2}).

Since
x\in S_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\ell_{2}\in S_{\mathbb{C}}^{\omega}
, the second summand vanishes and thus

\omega(x,\ell)=\omega(x,\ell_{1})=\omega_{S}(x,\ell_{1})=0.

Therefore,
x\in L^{\omega}=L
and hence
x\in L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

Recall now that if
S\subseteq V
is a symplectic subspace, then so is its complement
S^{\omega}
. Furthermore, we have a canonical internal direct sum
V=S\oplus S^{\omega}
. Thus, we have a natural projection
P_{S}:V\to S
along the complement of
S
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Suppose
L\subseteq V_{\mathbb{C}}
a complex Lagrangian subspace and
C\subseteq V
a subspace. Suppose there is a symplectic subspace
S\subseteq V
such that

(1)

C=C\cap S+C\cap S^{\omega}

(2)

L=L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}+L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}^{\omega}

(3)

C\cap S
is coisotropic in
S
.

(4)

c-P_{S}(c)\in C^{\omega}
for all
c\in C
.

Then the following holds.

(i)

The symplectic complements of
C\cap S
in
S
is given by

(C\cap S)^{\omega|_{S}}=C\cap C^{\omega}\cap S

and the image of
C\cap C^{\omega}
under the symplectic projection
P_{S}:V\to S
is given by

P_{S}(C\cap C^{\omega})=(C\cap S)^{\omega|_{S}}.

(ii)

Define

V_{0}=\frac{C}{C\cap C^{\omega}}\quad\text{and}\quad S_{0}=\frac{C\cap S}{(C\cap S)^{\omega|_{S}}}

and let
\pi_{C}:C\to V_{0}
and
\pi_{S}:C\cap S\to S_{0}
be the respective projections. Then the inclusion
\iota:C\cap S\hookrightarrow C
defines a symplectomorphism
\phi:S_{0}\to V_{0}
making the diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\cap S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(iii)

L_{0}=\pi_{C}(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})
is a Lagrangian subspace of
(V_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

(i)

First, observe that

C\cap C^{\omega}\cap S=(C\cap S)^{\omega|_{S}}

Indeed, since

C=C\cap S+C\cap S^{\omega},

it is straightforward to see that

(C\cap S)^{\omega|_{S}}=C^{\omega}\cap S.

But we are assuming
C\cap S
is coisotropic, thus

C^{\omega}\cap S\subseteq C\cap S

and hence

C^{\omega}\cap S=(C^{\omega}\cap S)\cap(C\cap S)=C\cap C^{\omega}\cap S.

Now to show that

P_{S}(C\cap C^{\omega})=C\cap C^{\omega}\cap S.

Let
c\in C\cap C^{\omega}
. Using the identity

(C+C^{\omega})^{\omega}=C\cap C^{\omega},

we show
P_{S}(c)
lies in the symplectic complement of
C+C^{\omega}
. To that end, let
x\in C
and
y\in C^{\omega}
. Using the fact that
P_{S}(C)=C\cap S
, we obtain

\omega(P_{S}(c),x+y)=\omega(P_{S}(c),x)=\omega(P_{S}(c),P_{S}(x))

Now, since
c-P_{S}(c)\in S^{\omega}
, we obtain

\omega(P_{S}(c),P_{S}(x))=\omega(P_{S}(c)+c-P_{S}(c),P_{S}(x))=\omega(c,P_{S}(x)).

Once again using the fact that
P_{S}(C)=C\cap S
and
c\in C^{\omega}
, we then obtain

\omega(c,P_{S}(x))=0

hence the claim.

(ii)

To define
\phi
let
c\in C\cap S
and let

\phi(\pi_{S}(c))=\pi_{C}(c).

This is well-defined since if
c-c^{\prime}\in C\cap C^{\omega}\cap S
, then clearly
\pi_{C}(c-c^{\prime})=0
. By construction, it makes the diagram commute. Writing
\omega_{0}
for the symplectic form on
V_{0}
and
(\omega_{S})_{0}
for the symplectic form on
S_{0}
, we observe that

\pi_{S}^{*}\phi^{*}\omega_{0}=\iota^{*}\pi_{C}^{*}\omega_{0}=\iota^{*}(\omega|_{C})=\omega|_{S\cap C}=(\omega_{S})|_{C\cap S}

Since
(\omega_{0})_{S}
is the unique two form on
S_{0}
satisfying
\pi_{S}^{*}(\omega_{S})_{0}=\omega_{S}|_{C\cap S}
, it follows that

\phi^{*}\omega_{0}=(\omega_{S})_{0}.

It remains to show that
\phi
is a linear isomorphism. Since
\phi
pulls back a non-degenerate
2
-form to a non-degenerate
2
-form, it immediately follows that
\phi
is injective. For surjectivity, observe that if
c\in C
, then since

c-P_{S}(c)\in C^{\omega},

we immediately conclude that

\phi(\pi_{S}(P_{S}(c))=\pi_{C}(c).

Hence,
\phi
is a symplectomorphism.

(iii)

Using the commuting diagram in (ii), we obtain that

\pi_{C}(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})=\phi(\pi_{S}(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}}\cap S_{\mathbb{C}})).

Since
C\cap S
is coisotropic and by Proposition
4.7
,
L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}
is a complex Lagrangian subspace of
S_{\mathbb{C}}
, it follows that
\pi_{S}(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}}\cap S_{\mathbb{C}})
is a complex Lagrangian subspace of
(S_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}
. Hence, by Proposition
4.7
, it follows that
\pi_{S}(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}}\cap S_{\mathbb{C}})\subseteq(S_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}
is a complex Lagrangian and since
\phi
is a symplectomorphism, so is its image.

∎

It might seem unlikely that we should ever be in the presence of a symplectic subspace as in Proposition
4.8
. However, in the presence of a linear action of a compact group, much of the above becomes automatic. So henceforth, let us fix a compact (not necessarily connected) Lie group
K
, and suppose
(V,\omega)
is a symplectic
K
-representation. We will also be assuming that the Lagrangian subspace
L\subseteq V_{\mathbb{C}}
is a complex
K
-representation.

Proposition 4.9
.

(1)

L=L\cap V^{K}_{\mathbb{C}}+L\cap(V^{K})^{\omega}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and

(2)

L^{K}
is a Lagrangian subspace of
V^{K}
.

Proof.

(1)

Recall from Proposition
3.39
that
V^{K}
is a symplectic subspace. Hence, we have a natural surjective projection along
(V^{K})^{\omega}

P_{V^{K}}:V\to V^{K}\subseteq V.

Restricting, we get a map
P_{V^{K}}|_{L}:L\to L^{K}\subseteq L
and hence we also get a canonical direct sum

L=L^{K}+\ker(P_{V^{K}}|_{L}).

It’s then automatic that
L^{K}=L\cap V^{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\ker(P_{V^{K}}|_{L})=L\cap(V^{K})^{\omega}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

(2)

In light of Proposition
4.7
,
L\cap V^{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
is a complex Lagrangian subspace of
V^{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Furthermore, we have

L^{K}=L\cap V^{K}_{\mathbb{C}}.

The result then follows.

∎

With this, we can now do a linear version of singular reduction.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
V
and
W
be two finite dimensional symplectic
K
-representations and let
F\subseteq V
be a
K
-invariant real Lagrangian subspace and define

C=F\oplus W^{K}

and let

(V\oplus W)_{0}:=\frac{F\oplus W^{K}}{(F\oplus W^{K})\cap(F\oplus W^{K})^{\omega}}

and write
\pi:C\to V_{0}
for the projection. If
L\subseteq V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus W_{\mathbb{C}}
is a
K
-invariant complex Lagrangian subspace, then

L_{0}=\pi(L\cap C_{\mathbb{C}})\subseteq(V\oplus W)_{0}\otimes\mathbb{C}

is a complex Lagrangian subspace of
(V\oplus W)_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
S=V^{K}\oplus W^{K}=(V\oplus W)^{K}
.
S
is a symplectic subspace and the projection map

P_{S}:V\oplus W\to V^{K}\oplus W^{K};\quad(v,w)\mapsto(v^{K},w^{K})

is given by averaging over
K
. By Proposition
4.9
,

L=L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}+L\cap S_{\mathbb{C}}^{\omega}.

Since
C
is closed under the
K
-action, we also have

C=C\cap S+C\cap S^{\omega}.

Next,

C\cap S=F^{K}\oplus W^{K}

which is clearly co-isotropic in
V^{K}\oplus W^{K}
. Finally, for any
(f,w)\in F\oplus W^{K}
, we have

(f,w)-P_{S}(f,w)=(f,w)-(f^{K},w)=(f-f^{K},0)

which lies in
C^{\omega}
since
F
is Lagrangian in
V
. Thus, we can apply Proposition
4.8
and the result then follows.
∎

13
Polarizations

Now that the linear theory of Lagrangian subspaces is complete, we can now go to the global theory of polarizations.

Definition 4.11

(Polarization)
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold. A
polarization
of
(M,\omega)
is a complex distribution
P\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
satisfying the following properties.

•

P
is involutive. That is, if
V,W\in\Gamma(P)
, then
[V,W]\in\Gamma(P)
.

•

P
is Lagrangian. That is, for all
x\in M
, the fibre
P_{x}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}M
is a complex Lagrangian subspace with respect to the complexification
\omega_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}
of the symplectic form
\omega_{x}
.

Given a polarization
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
, define the polarized functions by

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M):=\{f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})\ |\ v(f)=0\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{P}\}.

We will also write
C_{\mathcal{P}}^{\infty}(M)
for real-valued functions in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
.

We are to think of a polarization as representing the “momentum directions” of a symplectic manifold. Locally, this represents half the variables on which a wave-function could depend, so we will only take wave-functions which are constant in those directions. Before we make this notion precise, let us discuss some examples of polarizations and some of their features.

Example 4.12
.

Let
(M,\omega)=(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{can})
be standard symplectic
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with Darboux coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
. This symplectic manifold has a large number of natural polarizations. Viewing the
y
coordinates as momentum coordinates, we can produce the momentum polarization

\mathcal{P}_{momentum}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigg\{\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\ \bigg|\ i=1,\dots,n\bigg\}

and, viewing the
x
coordinates as the position varibles, we get the position polarization

\mathcal{P}_{position}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigg\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\ \bigg|\ i=1,\dots,n\bigg\}.

Both of these polarizations are meaningful for quantization. Note that they are also the complexifications of real distributions and hence are foliations. In both cases, the polarized functions
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{momentum}}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{position}}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
are equal to the functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
which are constant along the leaves and hence can be identified with
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{C})
.

We can get other kinds of polarizations via complex structures. Let
I
denote the standard complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
identifying it with
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Now consider the holomorphic and anti-holomorphic tangent bundles
T^{0,1}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and
T^{0,1}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. These are both involutive Lagrangian subbundles of
T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and hence are polarizations. In this case, the polarized function
\mathcal{O}_{T^{1,0}\mathbb{R}^{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and
\mathcal{O}_{T^{0,1}\mathbb{R}^{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
are equal to the anti-holomorphic and holomorphic functions, respectively.

Example 4.13
.

As a generalization of the above, cotangent bundles are also extremely natural places for polarizations to arise. Let
Q
be a manifold and
\tau:T^{*}Q\to Q
the cotangent bundle of
Q
. Since
\tau
is a surjective submersion with fibres the cotangent fibres, the complexification of the kernel

\mathcal{P}=\ker(T\tau)\otimes\mathbb{C}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}(T^{*}Q)

is an involutive complex distribution. Furthermore, since for any
\alpha\in T^{*}Q
we have

\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}=T_{\alpha}(T^{*}_{\tau(\alpha)}Q)\otimes\mathbb{C},

it immediately follows from the definition of the Liouville form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}Q)
that
\mathcal{P}
is Lagrangian. Working in cotangent coordinates, we can immediately deduce that the polarized function
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(T^{*}Q)
are the complex-valued smooth functions which are constant along the cotangent fibres. Put another way,

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(T^{*}Q)=\tau^{*}C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C}).

(55)

Example 4.14
.

Suppose
T^{n}
is an
n
-dimensional torus and
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
is a toric
T^{n}
space with
T^{n}
acting freely. Then by Example
2.26
, the subset
\mathfrak{t}_{M}\subseteq TM
defined by

(\mathfrak{t}_{M})_{x}=\{\xi_{M}(x)\ |\ \xi\in\mathfrak{t}\},\quad x\in M

is a
T^{n}
-invariant subbundle. I claim
\mathcal{P}=\mathfrak{t}_{M}\otimes\mathbb{C}
is a polarization. Involutivity follows immediately from
\mathfrak{t}_{M}
being the tangent bundle to a foliation. As for Lagrangian, since
T^{n}
is abelian we have for any
\xi,\eta\in\mathfrak{t}
that

\omega(\xi_{M},\eta_{M})=\eta_{M}(J^{\xi})=0

since
J^{\xi}:M\to\mathbb{R}
is invariant for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}
. Thus, the rank of
\mathfrak{t}_{M}
is half the dimension of
M
, it follows that
\mathcal{P}
is fibre-wise Lagrangian and hence a polarization.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a polarization.

(1)

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
. Then
f\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
if and only if
V_{f}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
.

(2)

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
is a Poisson subalgebra of
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

(1)

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
. Then
f\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
if and only if
d_{x}f
lies in the annihilator of
\mathcal{P}_{x}
for all
x
. Since
df=\omega(V_{f},\cdot)
, this occurs if and only if
V_{f}(x)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\omega}=\mathcal{P}_{x}
for all
x
. In turn, this occurs if and only if
V_{f}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
.

(2)

Let
f,g\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
. Then,

V_{\{f,g\}}=[V_{f},V_{g}].

By (1),
V_{f},V_{g}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
and so by involutivity, we have
V_{\{f,g\}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
which implies
\{f,g\}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
by (1).

∎

There are two archetypal (and most extensively studied) kinds of polarizations: real and Kähler. Fix a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
for the following discussion. Now suppose we have a (non-singular) foliation
\mathcal{F}
of
M
by Lagrangian submanifolds. Then, by Frobenius’ Theorem, the tangent bundle of the foliation
T\mathcal{F}
is involutive, and clearly
T_{x}\mathcal{F}\subseteq T_{x}M
is Lagrangian. Hence, the complexification

T\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{C}\subseteq TM\otimes\mathbb{C}

is a polarization.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
denote a Lagrangian foliation and let
\mathcal{P}=T\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{C}
. Then the polarized functions
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
are precisely the complex functions which are constant along the leaves of
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

By definition,
f\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
if and only if
df|_{L}=0
for every leaf
L
. Since the leaves are connected and
df|_{L}=d(f|_{L})
, this implies the result.
∎

Let us now discard the Lagrangian foliation
\mathcal{F}
and consider the opposite “extreme” (in a sense to made precise later): a Kähler structure. Let
I
be an integrable complex structure on
M
which is compatible with
\omega
in the sense that

g(X,Y):=\omega(X,I(Y))

defines a Riemannian metric on
(M,\omega)
.
I
naturally extends to an endomorphism

I:TM\otimes\mathbb{C}\to TM\otimes\mathbb{C}

which allows us to decompose
TM\otimes\mathbb{C}
into its
i
and
-i
eigenbundles, respectively

TM\otimes\mathbb{C}=T_{J}^{0,1}M\oplus T_{J}^{1,0}M.

It immediately follows that each of
T_{I}^{0,1}M,T_{I}^{1,0}M\subseteq TM\otimes\mathbb{C}
are Lagrangian subbundles and they are both involutive by Newlander-Nirenberg
[
NN57
]
. Hence, we get two polarizations from
I
: the holomorphic tangent bundle
T_{I}^{0,1}M
and the antiholomorphic tangent bundle
T_{I}^{1,0}M
.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
(M,\omega,I)
be a Kähler manifold. Then the complex polarized functions with respect to the holomorphic tangent bundle
T^{1,0}_{I}M
