are the antiholomorphic functions. Conversely, the complex polarized functions with respect to the antiholomorphic tangent bundle
T^{0,1}_{I}M
are given by the holomorphic functions on
M
.

Proof.

This is merely the definition of holomorphic and antiholomorphic functions.
∎

I said that this example coming from Kähler structures was opposite to that of a Lagrangian foliation. This is in the sense of complex conjugation. Notice that if
\mathcal{F}
is a Lagrangian foliation, then
T\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{C}
is equal to its fibre-wise complex conjugate

\overline{T\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{C}}=T\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{C},

while for a Kähler structure
I
, the holomorphic and anti-holomorphic tangent bundles
T_{I}^{1,0}M
and
T_{I}^{0,1}M
intersect trivially and are permuted by complex conjugation. That is,

\overline{T^{0,1}_{I}M}\cap T_{I}^{0,1}M=\{0\}.

This leads naturally to the notion of types of polarizations.

Definition 4.18

(Polarization Types)
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq TM\otimes\mathbb{C}
a polarization.

(1)

\mathcal{P}
is called
real
if
\mathcal{P}
is equal to its fibre-wise complex conjugate, that is

\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{P}.

(2)

\mathcal{P}
is called
totally complex
if
\mathcal{P}
trivially intersects its fibre-wise complex conjugate, that is

\overline{\mathcal{P}}\cap\mathcal{P}=\{0\}.

(3)

If
\mathcal{P}
is neither real nor totally complex, it is said to be of
mixed
type.

Remark 4.19
.

In general, totally complex polarizations need not correspond to a Kähler structure. Indeed, rather they correspond to pseudo-Kähler structures which means the induced metric
g
is a pseudo-Riemannian metric. That is, the induced metric
g
is not demanded to be positive-definite. Indeed, given a totally complex polarization
\mathcal{P}
we have an internal direct sum

TM\otimes\mathbb{C}=\mathcal{P}\oplus\overline{\mathcal{P}}.

Define then

I:\mathcal{P}\oplus\overline{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{P}\oplus\overline{\mathcal{P}};\quad v+w\mapsto iv-iw.

If we let
v\in TM
, then we can write
v=p+\overline{p}
for unique
p\in\mathcal{P}
. By definition,

I(v)=ip-i\overline{p}

Observe that
\overline{I(v)}=I(v)
which implies that
I(v)
lies in
TM
. Hence, we get a map

I:TM\to TM

Trivially
I^{2}=-\text{id }_{TM}
and
I
is integrable by Newlander-Nirenberg. Now define symmetric
2
-tensor on
TM
by

g(v,w)=\omega(v,I(w)).

g
need not be positive definite, but it will be non-degenerate, hence defining a pseudo-Riemannian metric.

For instance endow
\mathbb{C}^{2}
with complex coordinates
(z_{1},z_{2})
and let

\omega=\frac{i}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}dz_{j}\wedge d\overline{z_{j}}

be the canonical symplectic form. Now define

\mathcal{P}=\bigg\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}},\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z_{2}}}\bigg\rangle.

It’s then easy to see that
\mathcal{P}
is involutive and Lagrangian, hence a polarization. Furthermore, we trivially have
\mathcal{P}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}=0
. Thus,
\mathcal{P}
is totally complex. In this setting, we see that the induced symmetric
2
-tensor has the form

g=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{2}}

where concatenation indicates symmetric product, and the
x_{j},y_{j}
are defined by

x_{j}=\frac{z_{j}+\overline{z_{j}}}{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad y_{j}=\frac{i}{2}(z_{j}-\overline{z_{j}}).

In particular, the induced metric has mixed signature
(++--)
and thus is pseudo-Riemannian.

Example 4.20
.

Let
(M_{1},\omega_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2})
be two symplectic manifolds,
\mathcal{F}
a Lagrangian foliation of
M_{1}
and
J
a Kähler structure on
M_{2}
. Then, the external direct sum

\mathcal{P}=(T\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{C})\oplus T_{J}^{0,1}M_{2}\subseteq T(M_{1}\times M_{2})\otimes\mathbb{C}

is a polarization of mixed type.

As a last property of polarizations, I would like to discuss positivity. Observe that for each
x\in M
, we can define a symmetric sesquilinear form on
\mathcal{P}_{x}
as follows:

\langle v,w\rangle:=i\omega_{x}(v,\overline{w}),

(56)

where
\overline{w}
denotes the complex-conjugate of
w
.

Definition 4.21
.

A polarization
\mathcal{P}
is said to be positive if the pairing defined in Equation (
56
) is positive-definite.

As we shall see below, a polarization being positive is equivalent to being Kähler. The point of providing this alternate characterization is that positivity is an easy condition to verify in the context of taking quotients.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
a totally complex polarization. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

\mathcal{P}
is positive.

(2)

There exists Kähler structure
I
so that
\mathcal{P}=T_{I}^{0,1}M
.

Proof.

First, let us suppose
\mathcal{P}=T_{I}^{0,1}M
for some Kähler structure
I
. Write
g
for the induced Riemannian metric given by

g(v,w)=\omega(v,I(w)).

Fixing
x\in M
and
v\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and plugging
v
into the inner-product from Equation (
56
),

\langle v,v\rangle=i\omega_{x}(v,\overline{v})

Since
v
lies in the complexificaiton of
TM
, there exists
w_{1},w_{2}\in T_{x}M
so that
v=w_{1}+iw_{2}
. From here, it then follows that

\langle v,v\rangle=2\omega_{x}(w_{1},w_{2}).

Now, since
I(v)=-iv
, it follows that
I(w_{1})=w_{2}
and
I(w_{2})=-w_{1}
. In particular,

\langle v,v\rangle=2\omega_{x}(w_{1},I(w_{1}))=2g_{x}(w_{1},w_{1}).

In particular, if
w_{1}\neq 0
, we have
\langle v,v\rangle>0
and hence
\mathcal{P}
is positive.

For the converse, let us suppose that
\mathcal{P}
is positive. First I claim that
\mathcal{P}
is totally complex. Indeed, if
v\in\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}_{x}}
for some
x\in M
, then it follows that
\overline{v}\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
. Hence,

\langle v,v\rangle=i\omega_{x}(v,\overline{v})=0

since
\mathcal{P}_{x}
is Lagrangian. Since
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is assumed to be positive-definite, it follows that
v=0
. Thus,
\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}_{x}}=0
and so
\mathcal{P}
is totally complex.

Thus, by the discussion in Remark
4.19
, there exists an integrable complex structure
I:TM\to TM
such that
\mathcal{P}=T^{0,1}_{I}M
. To finish, we need to show that

g(v,w):=\omega(v,I(w))

is positive definite. This is straightforward as for any
v\in TM
first see that

I(v+iI(v))=I(v)-iv=-i(v+iI(v))

and hence
v+iI(v)\in\mathcal{P}
and furthermore

g(v,v)=\omega(v,I(v))=\frac{i}{2}\omega(v+iI(v),v-iI(v))=\frac{\langle v+iI(v),v+iI(v)\rangle}{2}

If
v\neq 0
, it then immediately follows from positivity that
g(v,v)>0
and thus
I
is a Kähler structure.
∎

14
Images of Vector Bundles and Complex Distributions

In the previous section, we derived the infinitesimal picture needed for singular reduction. Now we will need to piece everything together making use of integrability. The main ideas here are coming from vector bundle and complex distribution theory.

First, we need to ask a somewhat simple question. When is the image of a vector bundle again a vector bundle? In particular, if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a vector bundle morphism with
f
a surjective submersion, when is
p(A)\subseteq B
a vector subbundle over
N
? Unless
f
is a diffeomorphism, it does not suffice to just assume that
p
is constant rank.

Example 4.23
.

To demonstrate that a vector bundle morphism with constant rank need not have a vector bundle as its image, consider the universal covering map

f:\mathbb{R}\to S^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{C};\quad\theta\mapsto e^{i\theta}

Let
B=S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the trivial rank
2
bundle over
S^{1}
and define
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
as follows. For each
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, let
R_{\theta}\in\text{SO}(2)
be the counter-clockwise rotation by
\theta
. Choosing a line

L=\{(x,0)\ |\ x\in\mathbb{R}\}.

Now define
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

A_{\theta}:=R_{\theta/4}(L).

The restriction of the projection
pr_{1}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
onto the first factor defines a vector bundle structure

\pi_{A}:A\to\mathbb{R}.

Write
\pi_{B}:S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to S^{1}
for the projection on the first factor as well. Since
A_{\theta}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
for each
\theta
, we get a canonical vector bundle morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

given by fibre-wise inclusion. Then, we see
p(A)
is not a vector subbundle of
B
. Letting

X=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ x=0\text{ or }y=0\},

we see that

p(A)\cap(\{(1,0)\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})=\{(1,0)\}\times X

and over every other point in
S^{1}
will be a rotated copy of
X
. Hence,
p(A)
is not a vector subbundle.

Constant rank does not suffice to ensure the image of a vector bundle morphism is a vector bundle, so we need to impose a further condition.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

be a vector bundle morphism with
f
a surjective submersion. Then
p(A)\subseteq B
is a vector subbundle of
B
with

p(A)_{f(x)}=p(A_{x})

for any
x\in M
if and only if

(1)

The map

M\to\mathbb{Z};\quad x\mapsto\dim p(A_{x})

is constant.

(2)

If
f(x)=f(y)
, then
p(A_{x})=p(A_{y})
.

Proof.

First, suppose
p(A)\subset B
is a vector subbundle with

p(A_{x})=p(A)_{f(x)}

for all
x\in M
. Then clearly the dimension of
p(A_{x})
must be constant and if
x,y\in M
satisfy
f(x)=f(y)
, then
p(A_{x})=p(A_{y})
.

So let us now assume the converse. That is, assume
p(A_{x})
is constant in
x
and
p(A_{x})=p(A_{y})
for any
x,y\in M
with
f(x)=f(y)
. To show that
p(A)
is a vector subbundle, it suffices to show that around any point
n\in N
, we can find a neighbourhood
U\subseteq N
of
n
and point-wise linearly independent sections
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{k}\in\Gamma(U,B)
which span
p(A)|_{U}
.

Thus, making use of the fact that
f
is a submersion, we can pass to local coordinates and assume
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
N=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the projection onto the first factor. Note that since
p
is assumed constant rank, the kernel
\ker(p)\subseteq A
is a vector subbundle. Thus, we can find sections

\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k},\sigma_{\ell+1},\dots,\sigma_{r}\in\Gamma(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},A)

so that
\sigma_{\ell+1},\dots,\sigma_{r}
is a frame for
\ker(p)
. For each
i=1,\dots,k
, define

\tau_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to B;\quad x\mapsto p\circ\sigma_{i}(x,0)

By construction,
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{k}
span
p(A)
and are point-wise linearly independent. Hence the claim.
∎

Definition 4.25
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

be a vector bundle morphism. If
p(A_{x})=p(A_{y})
for any
x,y\in M
with
f(x)=f(y)
, say
p
is
constant along the fibres of
f
.

Remark 4.26
.

Returning to the vector bundle morphism from Example
4.23

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\subseteq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
f(\theta)=e^{i\theta}
is the universal covering map and
A
is defined by

A_{\theta}:=R_{\theta/4}(L)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}

where
L=\{(x,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ x\in\mathbb{R}\}
and
R_{\theta/4}\in\text{SO}(2)
is the counter-clockwise rotation around the origin by
\theta/4
.

The reason why
p(A)
was not a vector subbundle of
S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
was precisely because
p
violates hypothesis (ii) in Lemma
4.24
. Indeed, consider the fibres
A_{0}
and
A_{2\pi}
. We have

p(A_{0})=\{f(0)\}\times L=\{1\}\times L

whereas

p(A_{2\pi})=\{f(2\pi)\}\times R_{\pi/2}(L)=\{1\}\times R_{\pi/2}(L).

Clearly
p(A_{0})\neq p(A_{2\pi})
even though
f(0)=f(2\pi)
.

Example 4.27
.

If we do not demand
p
be constant along the fibres of the base map, we can also obtain examples where
p(A)
is a vector bundle. Indeed, consider now
f:S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
given by projection onto the second factor. Let
B=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the trivial rank-2 vector bundle and let us define
A\subseteq(\text{SO}(2)\times\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
as follows. Let

L=\{(x,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ x\in\mathbb{R}\}

and for each
(R,t)\in\text{SO}(2)\times\mathbb{R}
, define

A_{(R,t)}:=R(L)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}.

These fibres then piece together to a real line bundle
A\to\text{SO}(2)\times\mathbb{R}
. Letting
p:A\to B
be given by fibre-wise inclusion, we see that
p
is constant rank, but if
R,R^{\prime}\in\text{SO}(2)
are distinct elements and are not inverses, then for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
the two lines
R(L)
and
R^{\prime}(L)
are not equal and thus

A_{(R,t)}\neq A_{(R^{\prime},t)}

and hence their images are not equal either under
p
. However, it’s easy to see that

p(A)=B

and hence is a vector subbundle. Notice that in this case,
p(A_{(R,t)})\neq p(A)_{t}
for any
(R,t)\in\text{SO}(2)\times\mathbb{R}
.

Corollary 4.28
.

Suppose
p:A\to B
is a vector bundle morphism which is constant along the fibres of
f
. Then any choice of splitting

A=\ker(p)\oplus A^{\prime}

defines an injective map

p^{!}:\Gamma(p(A))\to\Gamma(A^{\prime});\quad\sigma\mapsto p^{!}\sigma

such that for any
\sigma\in\Gamma(p(A))
, the diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{!}\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

Proof.

Clearly
p
induces an isomorphism of vector bundles over
M
between the pullback bundle
f^{-1}(p(A))
and
A^{\prime}
,

f^{-1}(p(A))\cong A^{\prime}

(57)

Thus, if
\sigma\in\Gamma(p(A))
is a section, we get an induced pull-back section
f^{-1}\sigma\in\Gamma(f^{-1}p(A))
which gets identified with a section
p^{!}\sigma\in\Gamma(A^{\prime})
via the isomorphism in Equation (
57
).
∎

The immediate consequence of our discussion of general vector bundles is that we can now push-forward distributions by surjective submersions provided certain conditions are met.

Lemma 4.29
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are smooth manifold,
P\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
is a complex involutive subbundle, and
\pi:M\to N
a surjective submersion. Write
V^{\mathbb{C}}M:=\ker(T^{\mathbb{C}}\pi)
. Suppose the following conditions hold.

(i)

T^{\mathbb{C}}\pi|_{P}:P\to T^{\mathbb{C}}N
is constant along the fibres of
\pi
and is constant rank.

(ii)

For any sections
X\in\Gamma(V^{\mathbb{C}}M)
and
Y\in\Gamma(P)
we have

[X,Y]\in\Gamma(P).

Then

\pi_{*}P:=T\pi(P)\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}N

is an involutive complex distribution on
N
.

Proof.

The map

T\pi|_{P}:P\to T^{\mathbb{C}}N

is a constant rank vector bundle morphism which is constant along fibres over a surjective submersion. By Lemma
4.24
, its image
\pi_{*}P
is a complex subbundle of
T^{\mathbb{C}}N
. Now to show
\pi_{*}P
is involutive.

Let
X,Y\in\Gamma(\pi_{*}P)
be two sections and let
X^{L},Y^{L}\in\Gamma(P)
be two lifts as in Corollary
4.28
. Then
[X^{L},Y^{L}]\in\Gamma(P)
since
P
is involutive. Since
X^{L}\sim_{\pi}X
and
Y^{L}\sim_{\pi}Y
, we have
[X^{L},Y^{L}]\sim_{\pi}[X,Y]
. Thus,
[X,Y]\in\Gamma(\pi_{*}P)
as well.
∎

15
The Non-Singular Reduction of Polarizations

For all that follows, let
G
be a connected Lie group,
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space, and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq TM\otimes\mathbb{C}
a
G
-invariant polarization.

Theorem 4.30
.

Suppose
0
is a regular value of
J
,
J^{-1}(0)\neq\emptyset
, and so that
G
acts freely on
J^{-1}(0)
. Let
M_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G
be the reduced space and
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
the quotient map. If

\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}:=\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}J^{-1}(0)\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M

is an embedded submanifold of
T^{\mathbb{C}}M
, then

\mathcal{P}_{0}:=(\pi_{0})_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)})

is a polarization of
M_{0}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}
is an embedded submanifold of
T^{\mathbb{C}}M
, it immediately follows that
\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}
is a complex vector subbundle of
T^{\mathbb{C}}J^{-1}(0)
. Furthermore, since
\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}
is given by the intersection of two subbundles which are closed under Lie brackets,
\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}
is a complex distribution of
J^{-1}(0)
.

We are assuming that
\mathcal{P}
is
G
-invariant, and so it follows immediately that the vector bundle morphism
T\pi_{0}:\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}\to T^{\mathbb{C}}M_{0}
as in the below diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\mathbb{C}}M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{-1}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}

(58)

is constant along the fibres of
\pi_{0}
in the sense of Definition
4.25
. Furthermore, since
J^{-1}(0)
is a coisotropic submanifold and

T_{x}(G\cdot x)=(T_{x}J^{-1}(0))^{\omega_{x}}

for all
x
, it follows from the linear reduction statement in Proposition
4.1
that
T_{x}\pi_{0}((\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)})_{x})\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}_{\pi_{0}(x)}M_{0}
is a Lagrangian subspace of
T^{\mathbb{C}}_{\pi_{0}(x)}M_{0}
for any
x
. From this, we obtain two facts at once. First, the vector bundle morphism in Equation (
58
) is constant rank. Making use of Lemma
4.29
, we deduce that
(\pi_{0})_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)})=\mathcal{P}_{0}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M_{0}
is a complex distribution on
M_{0}
. Second, since
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is fibre-wise Lagrangian, it must be the case that
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is a polarization.

∎

Definition 4.31
.

Given a
G
-invariant polarization
\mathcal{P}
, say
\mathcal{P}
is
reducible

\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}:=\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}J^{-1}(0)
is an embedded submanifold of
T^{\mathbb{C}}M
.

In light of this theorem, from now on we shall be assuming that
0
is a regular value of
J
and that
G
acts freely on
J^{-1}(0)
. The main question we will be investigating further is when
\mathcal{P}
is reducible and the implications therein.

Proposition 4.32
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{J^{-1}(0)}\subseteq TJ^{-1}(0)
denote the tangent bundle of the foliation of
J^{-1}(0)
by orbits of the free
G
-action. If
\mathcal{P}
is fibre-wise positive as in Definition
4.21
, then

\mathcal{P}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{J^{-1}(0)}=\{0\}.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that for each
x\in J^{-1}(0)
, we have

(T_{x}J^{-1}(0))^{\omega_{x}}=T_{x}(G\cdot x)=(\mathfrak{g}_{J^{-1}(0)})_{x}.

So, by Proposition
4.5
,
\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}(G\cdot x)=\{0\}
since
\mathcal{P}
is positive.
∎

Corollary 4.33

(
[
GS82
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{P}
is a positive polarization, then it is reducible. Furthermore, the reduced polarization

\mathcal{P}_{0}=(\pi_{0})_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)})

is a positive polarization on
M_{0}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.32
, the sum

\mathcal{P}|_{J^{-1}(0)}+\mathfrak{g}_{J^{-1}(0)}\otimes\mathbb{C}

defines a vector subbundle of
T^{\mathbb{C}}M|_{J^{-1}(0)}
. Working point-wise, we see the symplectic dual is given by

(\mathcal{P}|_{J^{-1}(0)}+\mathfrak{g}_{J^{-1}(0)}\otimes\mathbb{C})^{\omega}=\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}J^{-1}(0)=\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}.

Hence,
\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}
is a subbundle and thus
\mathcal{P}
is reducible. The reduced polarization
\mathcal{P}_{0}
being positive-definite is a consequence of Corollary
4.6
.
∎

Applying this result together with Corollary
4.2
, allows us to conclude the following.

Corollary 4.34
.

If
I
is a Kähler structure, then it canonically induces a Kähler structure on
M_{0}
.

Example 4.35
.

It follows from both Theorem
3.58
and Corollary
4.34
that all toric manifolds are Kähler manifolds and, in fact, come equipped with an invariant Kähler structure. Let
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
be a toric
\mathbb{T}
-manifold with
n=\dim(\mathbb{T})
. Then by Theorem
3.58

J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
is
\mathbb{T}
-equivariantly diffeomorphic to the reduction of standard symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{N}
with respect to the action of some subtorus
K\leq(S^{1})^{N}
.

Now let
I_{can}
be the standard complex structure on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Clearly
I_{can}
is invariant under the full
(S^{1})^{N}
action, hence also under the action of the subtorus
K
. Therefore by Corollary
4.34
,
I_{can}
induces a Kähler structure
I_{\Delta}
on
M_{\Delta}
. Note that via Delzant’s construction, the action of
\mathbb{T}
on
M
can be identified with the action of
(S^{1})^{N}/K
on the reduced space. In particular, it then follows that
I_{\Delta}
must be invariant under the action of
\mathbb{T}
as well.

On the other extreme is of course real polarizations. These are most definitely not positive polarizations, so we will need to approach the question of when they are reducible in a slightly different fashion. Before we give the statement, let us recall a standard definition (for instance,
[
Hud24
, Example 2.7, (c)]
.)

Definition 4.36
.

Let
M
be a manifold and
A,B\subseteq M
two submanifolds. We say
A
and
B

intersect cleanly
if

(i)

A\cap B
is a submanifold of
M
.

(ii)

T_{x}(A\cap B)=T_{x}A\cap T_{x}B
for all
x\in A\cap B
.

Proposition 4.37
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}=\{L_{x}\}_{x\in M}
is a Lagrangian foliation of
M
. If the leaves of
\mathcal{F}
intersect
J^{-1}(0)
cleanly and with constant rank, then the partition

\mathcal{F}_{J^{-1}(0)}=\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{F}}\pi_{0}(L\cap J^{-1}(0))

is a regular foliation of
J^{-1}(0)
and

T\mathcal{F}\cap TJ^{-1}(0)=T\mathcal{F}_{J^{-1}(0)}

Proof.

By construction,
T\mathcal{F}_{J^{-1}(0)}
is an involutive subbundle of
TJ^{-1}(0)
. Thus, by Frobenius’ Theorem (for instance, see
[
Lee13
, Theorem 19.12]
),
T\mathcal{F}_{J^{-1}(0)}
is the tangent bundle of a regular foliation on
J^{-1}(0)
.
∎

Corollary 4.38
.

If
\mathcal{P}
is a real polarization and the Lagrangian leaves of
\mathcal{P}
intersect
J^{-1}(0)
cleanly with constant rank, then
\mathcal{P}
is reducible and its reduction
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is also real. Furthermore, if
x\in J^{-1}(0)
,
L_{x}\in\mathcal{F}
the Lagrangian leaf through
x
, and
\widetilde{L}_{[x]}\subseteq M_{0}
the leaf through
[x]\in M_{0}
, then

\pi_{0}^{-1}(\widetilde{L}_{[x]})=L_{x}\cap J^{-1}(0).

16
Reduction of Polarization on Cotangent Bundles

Our other natural family of examples we’ve been examining in this thesis are cotangent bundles as they are equipped with natural real polarizations. Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
Q
a proper
G
-space, and
\tau_{Q}:T^{*}Q\to Q
the cotangent bundle. As we saw in Example
4.13
,
T^{*}Q
has a natural polarization
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\subseteq T(T^{*}Q)\otimes\mathbb{C}
given by the complexification of the kernel of the derivative of the bundle map
\tau_{Q}
. Recall that in the case that
Q
has only one orbit-type, we showed in Theorem
3.61
that the reduction
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
is naturally symplectomorphic to the cotangent bundle
T^{*}(Q/G)
. This of course also has a natural polarization
\mathcal{P}_{Q/G}
given by the complexification of the kernel of the bundle map
\tau_{Q/G}:T^{*}(Q/G)\to Q/G
.

Proposition 4.39
.

Suppose
Q
has only one orbit-type and let

\phi:T^{*}(Q/G)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}

be the symplectomorphism from Theorem
3.61
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
is reducible in the sense of Definition
4.31
. Furthermore, the induced polarization
\mathcal{P}_{0}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}(T^{*}Q)_{0}
satisfies

T^{\mathbb{C}}\widetilde{\phi}(\mathcal{P}_{Q/G})=\mathcal{P}_{0}.

Proof.

Clearly

\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}J^{-1}(0)=\ker(T\tau_{Q}|_{J^{-1}(0)})\otimes\mathbb{C}

Hence,
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
is reducible and

\mathcal{P}_{0}=T^{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{Q}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}J^{-1}(0))

is a real polarization on
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
. Writing

\overline{\tau_{Q}}:(T^{*}Q)_{0}\to Q/G

for the induced projection,
(T^{*}Q)_{0}\to Q/G
is a vector bundle over
Q/G
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}
coincides with the canonical foliation of
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
by the fibres of
\overline{\tau_{Q}}
. Furthermore, the map

\widetilde{\phi}:T^{*}(Q/G)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}

is also an isomorphism of vector bundles. Thus,
\widetilde{\phi}
maps
\mathcal{P}_{Q/G}
to
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.
∎

†
†
margin:

5

Geometric Quantization

Quantization is a term that refers to a wide variety of procedures and techniques that take mathematical objects corresponding to classical physics, i.e. the physics of Newton, Hooke, Lagrange, etc., and produce a quantum analogue. This is quite a vague introduction, but it must be by necessity given the great depth of different versions of “quantization” which tend to try and reproduce different aspects of quantum mechanical theory in vastly different fashions. We will now get more specific with a discussion of geometric quantization, at least its simplest form.

The general idea is that classical physical systems are to be represented by symplectic manifolds
(M,\omega)
. The manifold
M
itself is to be thought of as a “phase-space” for some system, i.e. the set of all permissible positions and momenta that a system can take on. The observables are then modelled by the smooth functions
C^{\infty}(M)
, where a particular function
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
is to be thought of as representing a some quantity that can be measured directly via experiment. The symplectic form then induces an algebraic structure of a Poisson algebra
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on
C^{\infty}(M)
. Provided we are given a distinguished function
H\in C^{\infty}(M)
corresponding to the energy of the system, we can then determine how any observable
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
evolves using the Hamilton-Jacobi equation:

\frac{d}{dt}(f\circ\phi^{H}(t))=\{H,f\}(\phi^{H}(t)),

where
\phi^{H}(t)
denotes the Hamiltonian flow of
H
.

On the quantum side, we make use of Hilbert spaces and self-adjoint operators instead. Here we have a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with elements of the projectivization
\mathbb{\mathcal{H}}
corresponding to “wave-function” which are to be thought of as the states of a quantum mechanical system. The observables in this context are then the self-adjoint operators
S(\mathcal{H})
(or, more generally the essentially self-adjoint operators
[
Hal13
]
) with the possible values an operator
A\in S(\mathcal{H})
represent being given by elements of the spectrum. Similar to the case, the quantum observables
S(\mathcal{H})
also have a natural Lie algebraic structure, that of the commutator. Given
A,B\in S(\mathcal{H})
and
\hbar>0
, define

[A,B]_{\hbar}:=\frac{i}{\hbar}[A,B],

where
[\cdot,\cdot]
is the commutator of operators. In this case, if we have a distinguished observable
H\in S(\mathcal{H})
corresponding to the energy called the
Hamiltonian
, we can also track how another observable
A\in S(\mathcal{H})
changes over time by Schrodinger’s equation

\frac{d}{dt}A(t)=[H,A(t)]_{\hbar},

where

A(t)=e^{itH/\hbar}Ae^{-itH/\hbar}

See Hall
[
Hal13
]
for more information about this formalism.

We can see there are some major parallels between the symplectic formalism of classical mechanics and the Hilbert space formalism of quantum mechanics. Namely both have a state space, a set of observables with a real Lie algebra structure, and the necessity of a choice of a distinguished operator corresponding to energy which determines how an observable changes through the Lie bracket. This leads to the following “definition” of quantization.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold. A quantization of
(M,\omega)
consists of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and a Lie algebra homomorphism

\phi:C^{\infty}(M)\to S(\mathcal{H})

such that
\phi(1)=\text{id }_{\mathcal{H}}
. Furthermore, the pair
(\mathcal{H},\phi)
should be “minimal”

Now, what exactly is meant by minimal will depend on who you talk to. There is the classical definition of Dirac
[
Dir82
]
which further imposes that a complete set of functions on
(M,\omega)
should be mapped to a complete set of self-adjoint operators on
\mathcal{H}
. As was shown by Groenewold and van Hove
[
Gro46
]
, this is impossible for Euclidean spaces. However, Dirac quantizations do exist for tori as shown by Gotay
[
Got95
]
, so it’s not an entirely impossible dream. This thesis is not concerned with Dirac quantization, so we shall leave it at that.

Rather, let us impose a different version of “minimality”.

Definition 5.1

(Quantization Procedure)
.

A quantization procedure for a collection
\mathcal{C}
of symplectic manifolds consists of the following data.

(i)

A map

\mathcal{Q}:\mathcal{C}\to\text{Hilbert Spaces};\quad(M,\omega)\mapsto\mathcal{Q}(M,\omega)

assigning a Hilbert space to each symplectic manifold in the collection
\mathcal{C}
.

(ii)

To each symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)\in\mathcal{C}
there is a Lie subalgebra
\mathcal{A}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M)
called the
quantizable functions
.

(iii)

To each symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
there is a Lie algebra homomorphism

\mathcal{Q}:\mathcal{A}\to S(\mathcal{Q}(M,\omega)).

This data must satisfy the following axioms

(1)

If
1_{M}\in C^{\infty}(M)
denotes the constant
1
function, then
1_{M}\in\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{Q}(1_{M})=\text{id }_{\mathcal{Q}(M,\omega)}
.

(2)

Standard symplectic
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})\in\mathcal{C}
and

\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

I will now outline a particular quantization procedure called “Geometric Quantization” which ultimately goes back to the work of Kostant
[
Kos09
]
, Kirillov
[
Kir62
]
, and Souriau
[
Sou67
]
.

Remark 5.2
.

As a final note before we move on, we will be working in so-called “natural units” where we set
\hbar=1
as I don’t want to have to keep track of unnecessary constants as we go along. In particular, the Lie bracket on self-adjoint operators will now have the form

[A,B]_{1}=i[A,B].

I leave it as an exercise to the reader to insert
\hbar
in the correct positions in all the definitions to follow.

17
Prequantum Line Bundles

Prequantum line bundles first emerged from the work of Kirillov
[
Kir62
]
as a way of obtaining unitary representations of Lie groups via their coadjoint orbits. In the subsequent work of Kostant
[
Kos09
]
and Souriau
[
Sou67
]
, they were extended to more general symplectic manifolds as a potential first step for achieving a systematic framework for quantization. Let us now see how examine how one could derive such objects before giving the formal definition.

Suppose for simplicity that we have a compact symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
. We have a canonical Hilbert space associated to
(M,\omega)
, namely the space of compactly supported complex-valued functions
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
with the inner product

\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{M}f\overline{g}\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!},

where
\overline{g}
denotes the pointwise complex conjugate and
2n=\dim(M)
. The symplectic structure gives us a way of mapping functions to operators, namely Hamiltonian vector fields. Observe that if
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
g_{1},g_{2}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
, then

\displaystyle\langle V_{f}g_{1},g_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(V_{f}g_{1})\overline{g_{2}}\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(V_{f}(g_{1}\overline{g_{2}})-g_{2}\overline{V_{f}g_{2}})\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}V_{f}(g_{1}\overline{g_{2}})\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}-\langle g_{1},V_{f}g_{2}\rangle

Using the fact that
L_{V_{f}}\omega=0
and Stokes’ Theorem, we see that

0=\int_{M}V_{f}\bigg(g_{1}\overline{g_{2}}\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}\bigg)=\int_{M}V_{f}(g_{1}\overline{g_{2}})\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}.

Thus, the Hamiltonian vector field
V_{f}
is a skew-adjoint operator on
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
. Given that mapping a function to its Hamiltonian vector field is a Lie algebra homomorphism, we then obtain our first guess for a quantization map, namely

C^{\infty}(M)\to S(C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}));\quad f\mapsto-iV_{f},

(59)

This already gets us part of the way to a quantization scheme as in Definition
5.1
, however we quickly run into a problem here. Namely, if
1_{M}\in C^{\infty}(M)
is the constant
1
function, then
V_{1_{M}}=0
, which violates Axiom (1) in Definition
5.1
. This can easily be remedied by adding another term to the map in Equation (
59
). For any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, define the pointwise multiplication operator

m_{f}:C^{\infty}(M)\to S(C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}));\quad g\mapsto m_{f}g,

where
m_{f}g(x)=f(x)g(x)
for all
x\in M
and
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
. We then get a new map to the self-adjoint operators

C^{\infty}(M)\to S(C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}));\quad f\mapsto-iV_{f}+m_{f}

(60)

which now maps
1_{M}
to the identity. However, the map in Equation (
60
) is not a Lie algebra homomorphism! Indeed, given
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we have

\displaystyle i[-iV_{f}+m_{f},-iV_{g}+m_{g}]

\displaystyle=-i[V_{f},V_{g}]+[V_{f},m_{g}]+[m_{f},V_{g}]+i[m_{f},m_{g}]

\displaystyle=-iV_{\{f,g\}}+[V_{f},m_{g}]+[m_{f},V_{g}]+i[m_{f},m_{g}]

By acting against a test function
f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
, we obtain that

\displaystyle[V_{f},m_{g}]

\displaystyle=m_{\{f,g\}}

\displaystyle[m_{f},m_{g}]

\displaystyle=0.

Thus,

\displaystyle i[-iV_{f}+m_{f},-iV_{g}+m_{g}]=-iV_{\{f,g\}}+2m_{\{f,g\}}\neq-iV_{\{f,g\}}+m_{\{f,g\}}.

To remedy this situation, suppose that
\omega
is exact, that is
\omega=d\theta
for some
1
-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. Then for any function
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
we can add a correction term to Equation (
60
), namely
\theta(V_{f})
. This provides for us a third potential map

\mathcal{Q}_{pre}:C^{\infty}(M)\to S(C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}));\quad f\mapsto-iV_{f}+m_{\theta(V_{f})}+m_{f}

(61)

can easily be verified to satisfy Axiom (1) and is a Lie algebra homomorphism. Indeed, we first see that

\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(1_{M})=iV_{1_{M}}+m_{\theta(1_{M})}+m_{1_{M}}

As we’ve already seen,
V_{1_{M}}=0
and so

\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(1_{M})=i\cdot 0+m_{\theta(0)}+m_{1_{M}}=m_{1_{M}}=\text{id }_{C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})}

As for being a Lie algebra homomorphism, for any
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
, making use of our previous computation, we obtain

\displaystyle i[\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f),\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(g)]

\displaystyle=i[-iV_{f}+m_{\theta(V_{f})}+m_{f},-iV_{g}+m_{\theta(V_{g})}+m_{g}]

\displaystyle=-iV_{\{f,g\}}+2m_{\{f,g\}}+[V_{f},m_{\theta(V_{g})}]+[m_{\theta(V_{f})},V_{g}].

Again, acting against a test function, we obtain

[V_{f},m_{\theta(V_{g})}]+[m_{\theta(V_{f})},V_{g}]=m_{V_{f}\theta(V_{g})-V_{g}\theta(V_{f})}.

Note that

\omega(V_{f},V_{g})=V_{f}\theta(V_{g})-V_{g}\theta(V_{f})-\theta([V_{f},V_{g}]).

Thus, since
-\{f,g\}=\omega(V_{f},V_{g})
and
[V_{f},V_{g}]=V_{\{f,g\}}
, we conclude that

[V_{f},m_{\theta(V_{g})}]+[m_{\theta(V_{f})},V_{g}]=-m_{\{f,g\}}+m_{\theta(V_{\{f,g\}})}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle i[\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f),\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(g)]

\displaystyle=-iV_{\{f,g\}}+2m_{\{f,g\}}-m_{\{f,g\}}+m_{\theta(V_{\{f,g\}})}

\displaystyle=-iV_{\{f,g\}}++m_{\theta(V_{\{f,g\}})}+m_{\{f,g\}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(\{f,g\}).

Of course a general symplectic manifold will not be exact in the sense that the symplectic form admits a global primitive. And so, in principle, we would need to pass to an open cover of
M
where primitives of
\omega
exist, then glue these together to obtain a well-defined global operator. The cost of doing this is that our pre-Hilbert space is no longer
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
, but rather the sections of a complex line bundle. This now leads us to the definition of a prequantum line bundle.

Definition 5.3

(Prequantum Line Bundle)
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold. A prequantum line bundle is a Hermitian complex line bundle over
M

\mathcal{L}\to M
together with a compatible connection

\nabla:\mathfrak{X}(M)\otimes\Gamma(\mathcal{L})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L});\quad X\otimes\sigma\mapsto\nabla_{X}\sigma

such that if
V,W\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
are vector fields and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
is a section, then

\nabla_{V}(\nabla_{W}\sigma)-\nabla_{Y}(\nabla_{V}\sigma)-\nabla_{[V,W]}\sigma=i\omega(V,W)

(62)

We call
\nabla
the prequantum connection and write
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
for the data of a prequantum line bundle.

Example 5.4
.

Our discussion leading up to the formal definition of a prequantum line bundle showed that any exact symplectic manifold admits a prequantum line bundle. More formally, suppose
(M,\omega)
is a symplectic manifold with
\omega=d\theta
for some
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. Let

\mathcal{L}=M\times\mathbb{C}

with the standard Hermitian structure on
\mathbb{C}
. Identifying
\Gamma(\mathcal{L})=C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
, define

\nabla:\mathfrak{X}(M)\otimes C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})\to C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C});\quad V\otimes f\mapsto L_{V}f+i\theta(V)f,

where
L_{V}f
is the Lie derivative of
f
along
V
. Doing pretty well the exact same computation as we did for showing the map in Equation (
61
), we can easily show that

[\nabla_{V},\nabla_{W}]-\nabla_{[V,W]}=id\theta(V,W)=i\omega(V,W).

Hence,
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
is a prequantum line bundle.

As was suggested above, the exact case corresponds to the local picture of prequantum line bundles. If
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
is a prequantum line bundle, then on a trivializing neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
so that
\mathcal{L}|_{U}\cong U\times\mathbb{C}
as complex line bundles, we can identify
\Gamma(\mathcal{L}|_{U})
with
C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{C})
. Under this identification, we get a canonical complex-valued
1
-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U,\mathbb{C})
defined by

\alpha(X)=\nabla_{X}(1_{U}),

where
1_{U}\in C^{\infty}(U)
is the constant
1
-function. Using Equation (
62
) one can verify that
\alpha
is actually imaginary, i.e
\alpha=i\theta
for a real-valued
1
-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(U)
and that this real-valued
1
-form
\theta
is a primitive of
\omega
, i.e.
d\theta=\omega
. With this discussion, we have outlined a proof of the following.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
be a prequantum line bundle,
U\subseteq M
an open subset, and

\phi:\mathcal{L}|_{U}\to U\times\mathbb{C}

a trivialization. Then, identifying the sections of
\mathcal{L}|_{U}\to U
with complex-valued smooth functions
C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{C})
, there exists a
1
-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(U)
such that
d\theta=\omega|_{U}
and so that
\nabla
can be identified with

\nabla_{V}f=L_{V}f+i\theta(V)f,

where
L_{V}
denotes the Lie derivative of
f
along
V
.

This then allows us to translate the the map defined by Equation (
61
) into the language of connections to obtain the following definition.

Definition 5.6

(Prequantum Operator)
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
be a prequantum line bundle and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Define the
prequantum operator
of
f
to be the linear operator on sections

\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f):\Gamma(\mathcal{L})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L})

given by

\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f)=-i\nabla_{V_{f}}+m_{f},

where
m_{f}
is the pointwise multiplication operator by
f
.

As is expected from the local description given above, prequantum operators satisfy Axiom (2) of Definition
5.1
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
, then

i[\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f),\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(g)]=\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(\{f,g\}).

Furthermore,
\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(1)=\text{id }_{\Gamma(\mathcal{L})}
.

Proof.

Fix
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Then, as operators on
\Gamma(\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle i[\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f),\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(g)]

\displaystyle=i[-i\nabla_{V_{f}}+m_{f},-i\nabla_{V_{g}}+m_{g}]

\displaystyle=-i[\nabla_{V_{f}},\nabla_{V_{g}}]+[\nabla_{V_{f}},m_{g}]+[m_{f},\nabla_{V_{g}}]+i[m_{f},m_{g}].

First note that clearly
[m_{f},m_{g}]=0
as both
m_{f}
and
m_{g}
are just pointwise multiplication by real-valued functions. Next, since
\nabla
is a connection we have for any
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})

[\nabla_{V_{f}},m_{g}]\sigma=\nabla_{V_{f}}(g\sigma)-g\nabla_{V_{f}}\sigma=(V_{f}g)\sigma=\{f,g\}\sigma.

Thus, swapping
f
and
g
in the above we obtain

[\nabla_{V_{f}},m_{g}]+[m_{f},\nabla_{V_{g}}]=2m_{\{f,g\}}.

Finally, the prequantum condition on
\nabla
provides

\displaystyle[\nabla_{V_{f}},\nabla_{V_{g}}]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{[V_{f},V_{g}]}+i\omega(V_{f},V_{g})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{V_{\{f,g\}}}-im_{\{f,g\}},

where we used the facts that
[V_{f},V_{g}]=V_{\{f,g\}}
and
\omega(V_{f},V_{g})=-\{f,g\}
via Proposition
3.25
. Therefore,

\displaystyle i[\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f),\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(g)]

\displaystyle=-i(\nabla_{V_{\{f,g\}}}-im_{\{f,g\}})+2m_{\{f,g\}}

\displaystyle=-i\nabla_{V_{\{f,g\}}}+m_{\{f,g\}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(\{f,g\})

as desired.
\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(1_{M})=\text{id }_{\Gamma(\mathcal{L})}
follows from the fact that
V_{1_{M}}=0
.
∎

As was the case for an exact compact symplectic manifold, if
M
is compact then we can make
\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
into a Hilbert space via

\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle=\int_{M}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}

where
2n=\dim(M)
and
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the Hermitian structure on
\mathcal{L}
. In this case,
\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f)\in S(\Gamma(\mathcal{L}))
for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. If
M
is not compact, then we need to take the Hilbert space completion of the space of compactly supported section
\Gamma_{0}(\mathcal{L})
, in which case
\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f)
will only be essentially self-adjoint.

So prequantum line bundles seem like quite the promising direction towards quantization of symplectic manifolds. However we must now ask which symplectic manifolds admit prequantum line bundles. Given Proposition
5.5
, it follows that a prequantum line bundle
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)
can be obtained by gluing together local trivializations of neighbourhoods where the symplectic form
\omega
is exact. This procedure can only work out if
\omega
satisfies an integrality condition. In particular, using a Cech cohomology argument, one can prove the following.

Theorem 5.8

(
[
Kos09
]
)
.

A symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
admits a prequantum line bundle if and only if the cohomology class
\frac{1}{\hbar}[\omega]\in H^{2}(M)
lies in the image of

H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}(M).

This is quite nice as it suggests the discrete properties associated to quantization, like, for instance, the discrete orbits an electron can inhabit in an atom. However, as this next example shows, prequantum line bundles cannot be the end of the story as far as quantization goes.

Example 5.9
.

Let
(M,\omega)=(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{can})
be standard symplectic
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. That is, writing
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
for coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, we have

\omega_{can}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}dx_{i}\wedge dy_{i}.

Using the primitive

\theta=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}dx_{i}

of
\omega
and making use of the construction in Example
5.4
, the Hilbert space the prequantum operators act on is the Hilbert space completion of
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{C})
. This is easily computed to be
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, which is not the right Hilbert space!

As we can see from the above example, prequantum line bundles produce a Hilbert space which is “too big” in the sense that the Hilbert space depends on too many variables. This is a serious issue as important quantum mechanical properties like the uncertainty principle for position and momentum will not hold in the spaces produced by prequantum line bundles. So, we need to modify our procedure and cut down on the variables. This is achieved by polarizations.

Let us now record some elementary properties of prequantum line bundles which we will be using later on.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
be a prequantum line bundle,
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
, and
x\in M
. If
V,W\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and
V_{x}=W_{x}
, then

\nabla_{V}\sigma(x)=\nabla_{W}\sigma(x).

Proof.

Fix
x\in M
, a section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
, and vector fields
V,W\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
with
V_{x}=W_{x}
. Let
U\subseteq M
be a trivializing neighbourhood of
\mathcal{L}
. Then, by Proposition
5.5
, after identifying the sections
\Gamma(\mathcal{L}|_{U})
with complex-valued functions
C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{C})
we can identify the connection
\nabla
with the operator

\nabla=L+i\theta,

where
L
is the Lie derivative operator for a vector field and
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(U)
is a local primitive of
\omega
. Via the trivialization, we can identify
\sigma|_{U}
with a function
h\in C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{C})
. Then,

\nabla_{V}h(x)=V_{x}h+i\theta(V_{x})h(x)=W_{x}h+i\theta(W_{x})h(x)=\nabla_{W}h(x).

∎

Corollary 5.11
.

For each
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
and
v\in T^{\mathbb{C}}M
, define

\nabla_{v}\ \sigma:=\nabla_{V}\ \sigma,

(63)

where
V\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is any complex vector field with
V_{x}=v
. Then the map

\nabla\sigma:T^{\mathbb{C}}M\to L;\quad v\mapsto\nabla_{v}\ \sigma

(64)

is well-defined and a smooth map between complex vector bundles.

Proof.

Due to Proposition
5.10
, the map in Equation (
64
) is well-defined. To show smooth, fix
x\in M
and let
U\subseteq M
be an open neighbourhood which admits a frame
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathbb{C}}(U)
of
T^{\mathbb{C}}U
. Then the map

U\times\mathbb{C}^{k}\to T^{\mathbb{C}}U;\quad(y,\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{k})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}\lambda^{i}(V_{i})_{y}

is an isomorphism of vector bundles. Define now

\phi:U\times\mathbb{C}^{k}\to L|_{U};\quad(y,\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{k})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}\lambda^{i}\nabla_{V_{i}}\sigma(y)

Then
\nabla\sigma
fits into the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\mathbb{C}}U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L|_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\times\mathbb{C}^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Hence,
\nabla\sigma
is smooth.
∎

18
Geometric Quantization

Definition 5.12

(Quantization Data, Geometric Quantization)
.

By quantization data I will mean a triple
(M,\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
where
M
is a symplectic manifold,
\mathcal{P}
a polarization of
M
, and
\mathcal{L}
a prequantum line bundle over
M
. We then define the geometric quantization of the triple by

\mathcal{Q}(M,\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L}):=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})\ |\ \nabla_{\mathcal{P}}\sigma\equiv 0\}.

If
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{L}
are understood from context, we write

\mathcal{Q}(M)=\mathcal{Q}(M,\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L}).

Example 5.13
.

Going back to our example with symplectic
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. Defining

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{n}}\bigg)

then
\mathcal{P}
is a polarization of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. Using the same prequantum line bundle
\mathcal{L}=\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{C}
, we obtain

\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})=\{f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{C})\ |\ f(x,y)=g(x)\text{ for some }g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{C})\}

which can be identified with
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Note that is precisely the space of polarized functions
\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}}
. Of course we have another canonical polarization obtained by identifying
\mathbb{R}^{2n}=\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Letting
I_{can}
denote the canonical complex structure, let

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}=T^{0,1}\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

In this case, if we use the primitive

\theta=-\frac{i}{2}\sum\overline{z}_{j}dz_{j}

Then
\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})=\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}}
which is exactly the space of holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.

As we see from the above, geometric quantization need not output a Hilbert space. Indeed, further procedures like half-forms or metaplectic constructions provide the extra data needed. See Sniatycki
[
Śn75
]
for a good overview of these kinds of constructions. However, using a very similar idea to the proof above, we can prove the following about the geometric quantization of cotangent bundles.

Theorem 5.14
.

The quantization of
T^{*}Q
is equal to the polarized functions
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(T^{*}Q)
. In particular, we have an equality

\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)=\tau^{*}C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C}).

Proof.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}Q,\mathbb{C})
. Then
f\in\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)
if and only if for any
\alpha\in T^{*}Q
and
V\in T_{\alpha}(T^{*}_{\tau(\alpha)}Q)
we have

\nabla_{V}f=Vf+i\theta(V)f=0.

(65)

Note that by construction, we have
\theta(V)=0
if
V
is tangent to the cotangent fibres. Hence,
\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)
coincides with the polarized functions
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(T^{*}Q)
. By Equation (
65
), we have

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(T^{*}Q)=\tau_{Q}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C}).

∎

19
Quantization Commutes With Reduction

For all that follows,
G
will be a connected Lie group and
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space.

Definition 5.15

(Equivariant Prequantum Line Bundle)
.

A prequantum line bundle
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
is called equivariant if
L\to M
is equipped with the structure of a
G
-equivariant line bundle and if for each
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
we have

\xi\cdot\sigma=\nabla_{\xi_{M}}\sigma+iJ^{\xi}\sigma,

where
\xi\cdot\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
is defined by

(\xi\cdot\sigma)(x):=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\exp(t\xi)\sigma(\exp(-t\xi)\cdot x)

Example 5.16
.

Suppose
(M,\omega)
is a symplectic manifold with exact symplectic form
\omega
with a chosen primitive
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. Recall from Example
5.4
we have a canonical prequantum line bundle, the trivial bundle
\mathcal{L}=M\times\mathbb{C}
together with the connection

\nabla=L+i\theta.

Now suppose a connected Lie group
G
acts on
M
properly and in a Hamiltonian fashion

J:M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}.

If we equip
\mathcal{L}
with the trivial action on the second factor, then for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
and
f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
,

\xi\cdot f=\xi_{M}f.

Hence, the equivariance condition boils down to

\xi_{M}f=\xi_{M}f+i\theta(\xi_{M})f+iJ^{\xi}f

Hence,
\nabla
is equivariant if and only if

\theta(\xi_{M})=-J^{\xi}

(66)

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. More generally, if we also have a representation

\rho:G\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}

and use this to non-trivially act on the second factor of
\mathcal{L}
, then for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
and
f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C})
, we have

\xi\cdot f=\rho_{*}(\xi)f+\xi_{M}f,

where we are viewing
\rho_{*}(\xi)\in\mathbb{C}
. Hence,
\nabla
is equivariant if and only if

\theta(\xi_{M})=-J^{\xi}-i\rho_{*}(\xi)

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. Note that this only makes sense if
\rho_{*}(\xi)\in i\mathbb{R}
which is guaranteed to happen if
\rho
is a unitary representation, i.e. has image in
S^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

Example 5.17
.

We will see below examples of
1
-forms satisfying Equation (
66
) in Example
5.16
, but we can actually obtain a large number of examples from cotangent bundles. Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
Q
a proper
G
-space. Let
J:(T^{*}Q,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be the induced Hamiltonian
G
-space structure on
T^{*}Q
with respect to the canonical symplectic form where

\langle J(\alpha),\xi\rangle=\langle\alpha,\xi_{Q}\rangle

for
\alpha\in T^{*}Q
,
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, and
\xi_{Q}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
the associated fundamental vector field. The canonical symplectic form has of course a canonical primitive, namely the negative of the Liouville
1
-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(Q)
. Observe that if
\tau:T^{*}Q\to Q
is the bundle map,
\alpha\in T^{*}Q
, and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, then since
\tau
is equivariant we have

\theta_{\alpha}(\xi_{T^{*}Q}(\alpha))=\langle\alpha,T_{\alpha}\tau(\xi_{T^{*}Q}(\alpha))\rangle=\langle\alpha,\xi_{Q}(\tau(\alpha))\rangle=J^{\xi}(\alpha).

Thus,
-\theta
satisfies Equation (
66
) and so the trivial prequantum line bundle
\mathcal{L}=T^{*}Q\times\mathbb{C}
with the connection
\nabla=L-i\theta
is an equivariant prequantum line bundle.

Due to the equivariance of the momentum map, the level set
J^{-1}(0)
is closed under the action of
G
. Hence, so is the restriction
\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)}
for an equivariant prequantum line bundle. With this, we can now formally define the reduction of prequantum line bundles.

Definition 5.18

(Reduction of Prequantum Line Bundle)
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space, and
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
an equivariant prequantum line bundle. Define the
reduction of
\mathcal{L}
along the
0
-level set
by

\mathcal{L}_{0}:=(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/G.

(67)

For all that follows, we fix an equivariant prequantum line bundle
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
. An easy application of Theorem
2.35
and Proposition
2.39
allows us to conclude the following.

Lemma 5.19
.

Suppose
J^{-1}(0)\subseteq M
is an embedded submanifold and, furthermore, suppose
J^{-1}(0)
has only one orbit-type. Write
\pi_{M}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)}\to\mathcal{L}_{0}
for the quotient maps. If the map

J^{-1}(0)\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\mathcal{L};\quad(x,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(x)

(68)

is surjective, then the following holds.

(i)

The induced map
\mathcal{L}_{0}\to M_{0}
is a smooth complex line bundle over
M_{0}
.

(ii)

The projection
\pi_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)}\to\mathcal{L}_{0}
induces a bijection

\kappa:\Gamma(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})^{G}\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})

(iii)

The map

\kappa:\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0});\quad\sigma\mapsto\kappa(\sigma|_{J^{-1}(0)})

is a surjective map.

Remark 5.20
.

If
G
acts freely on
J^{-1}(0)
, then the assumption that the map

J^{-1}(0)\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)}

is automatic. However, if the action of
G
on
J^{-1}(0)
is not free, then

J^{-1}(0)\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)}

need not be surjective. The main consequence of this fact is that
\mathcal{L}_{0}=(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/G
need not be a manifold and hence need not be a line bundle. To see how this could be the case, consider the following example. Let
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
\mathcal{L}=M\times\mathbb{C}
be the trivial bundle. Equip
M
with the canonical
S^{1}
action

S^{1}\times M\to M;\quad(t,(x,y))\mapsto(t\cdot x,y),

where
t\cdot x
is the standard action of
S^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Consider also the standard representation
\rho:S^{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
given by the inclusion. It’s then an easy check to see that the momentum map is given by

J:M\to\mathbb{R};\quad(x,y)\mapsto\frac{\|x\|^{2}}{2}.

By Example
5.16
, an equivariant connection is specified by a primitive
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
of the symplectic form satisfying

\theta(\xi_{M})=J^{\xi}-i\rho_{*}(\xi)

(69)

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}=\mathbb{R}
. Let
\beta
be any primitive of the standard symplectic form on the second factor of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and consider the primitive

\theta=\frac{-x_{2}dx_{1}+x_{1}dx_{2}}{2}+pr_{2}^{*}\beta

on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. It easily follows that
\theta
satisfies Equation (
69
). Now, since we are acting by the canonical
S^{1}
action on
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, we see that over
J^{-1}(0)=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0),\mathbb{C})^{S^{1}}=\{0\}

Furthermore, we have

\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\mathbb{C}/S^{1})

which is not a line bundle over
J^{-1}(0)
.

To remedy the fact that the map

J^{-1}(0)\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\mathcal{L};\quad(x,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(x)

need not be surjective in general, one could extend this analysis to consider
orbibundles
as in done in
[
MS99
]
. However, for the purposes of this thesis we will content ourselves with a more restrictive equivariance condition.

As a special case of the above, we have the following Theorem of Guillemin and Sternberg.

Theorem 5.21

(
[
GS82
]
)
.

Suppose
0
is a regular value of
J
and
G
acts freely on
J^{-1}(0)
. Then
L_{0}
admits a unique prequantum connection
\nabla^{0}
which has the property that

\pi^{*}\nabla^{0}=\iota^{*}\nabla,

where
\pi:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
is the quotient map and
\iota:J^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow M
is the inclusion.

This is proven by passing to
G
-invariant trivializing neighbourhoods of points in
J^{-1}(0)
and showing that local primitives of the symplectic form are basic and hence descend to primitives of the reduced symplectic form. This approach requires that
G
act freely, and so we need to make an adjustment to extend to the more general (but still non-singular!) setting of Lemma
5.19
. To set up this more general approach, recall that by Corollary
2.40
if
J^{-1}(0)
is an embedded submanifold and has only one orbit-type, then the reduction map
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to J^{-1}(0)/G=M_{0}
induces a surjection

(\pi_{0})_{*}:\mathfrak{X}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}\to\mathfrak{X}(M_{0}).

Using this map, we can construct
\nabla^{0}
as follows. Letting
V\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{0})
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
, find lifts to
W\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and
\tau\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
with

(\pi_{0})_{*}(W|_{J^{-1}(0)})=V\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa(\tau)=\sigma.

Then define

\nabla^{0}_{V}\sigma:=\kappa(\nabla_{W}\tau).

(70)

By Corollary
2.40
and Lemma
5.19
, both
W
and
\tau
are guaranteed to exist. The issue now is to show that
\nabla^{0}
is well-defined, is smooth, and is a prequantum connection.

Theorem 5.22
.

Suppose
J^{-1}(0)\subseteq M
is an embedded submanifold with only one orbit type and suppose

J^{-1}(0)\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\mathcal{L};\quad(x,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(x)

is surjective. Then the following holds.

(i)

Suppose
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
and
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
satisfy

\sigma_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\sigma_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}

and

(\pi_{0})_{*}(V_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)})=(\pi_{0})_{*}(V_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}).

Then,

\nabla_{V_{1}}\sigma_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\nabla_{V_{2}}\sigma_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}.

(71)

(ii)

\mathcal{L}_{0}=(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/G
is canonically a prequantum line bundle over
M_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G
with prequantum connection
\nabla^{0}
given by

\nabla^{0}_{V}\sigma=\kappa(\nabla_{W}\tau),

where
W\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and
\tau\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
satisfy

(\pi_{0})_{*}W|_{J^{-1}(0)}=V\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa(\tau)=\sigma.

Proof.

(i)

Fix equivariant sections
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
and restrictable vector fields
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
satisfying
\sigma_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\sigma_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}
and
(\pi_{0})_{*}(V_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)})=(\pi_{0})_{*}(V_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)})
. Observe that

\nabla_{V_{1}}\sigma_{1}-\nabla_{V_{2}}\sigma_{2}=\nabla_{V_{1}}(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})+\nabla_{V_{1}-V_{2}}\sigma_{2}

I claim both terms vanish after restricting to
J^{-1}(0)
.

To show the first term vanishes, fix
x\in J^{-1}(0)
and let
U\subseteq M
be a trivialization neighbourhood for
\mathcal{L}
. Then, after choosing an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}|_{U}\to U\times\mathbb{C}
, we can identify
\Gamma(\mathcal{L}|_{U})
with
C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{C})
. Thus, since
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
over
J^{-1}(0)
, we can identify
\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}
with a function
f\in C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{C})
with
f|_{J^{-1}(0)}=0
. Furthermore, we can find a local primitive
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U)
of
\omega|_{U}
so that

\nabla=d+i\alpha.

Hence,

\nabla_{V_{1}}(f)=V_{1}f+i\alpha(V_{1})f.

Using the fact that
f(x)=0
, we get

\nabla_{V_{1}}f(x)=(V_{1})_{x}f.

Since
V_{1}
is tangent to
J^{-1}(0)
and
f
vanishes on
J^{-1}(0)
, we have
(V_{1})_{x}f=0
. Since this holds in any trivializing neighbourhood intersecting
J^{-1}(0)
, we obtain
\nabla_{V_{1}}(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})|_{J^{-1}(0)}=0
.

Now for the second term. Since
(\pi_{0})_{*}V_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=(\pi_{0})_{*}V_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}
, we have

(\pi_{0})_{*}((V_{1}-V_{2})|_{J^{-1}(0)})=0.

Since
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
is the quotient map by the
G
-action and the action of
G
on
J^{-1}(0)
is regular, there exists functions
h_{1},\dots,h_{M}\in C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))
and Lie algebra elements
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{M}\in\mathfrak{g}
so that

(V_{1}-V_{2})|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}h_{i}(\xi_{i})_{J^{-1}(0)},

where
(\xi_{i})_{J^{-1}(0)}\in\mathfrak{X}(J^{-1}(0))
is the fundamental vector field associated to
\xi_{i}
. Thus,

\nabla_{V_{1}-V_{2}}\sigma_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}h_{i}\nabla_{(\xi_{i})_{M}}\sigma_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}.

By the equivariance of
\nabla
and
\sigma_{2}
, we have

\nabla_{(\xi_{i})_{M}}\sigma_{2}=\xi_{i}\cdot\sigma+iJ^{\xi_{i}}\sigma_{2}=+iJ^{\xi_{i}}\sigma_{2}

Thus, restricting to
J^{-1}(0)
we have

\nabla_{V_{1}-V_{2}}\sigma_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=0.

(ii)

\mathcal{L}_{0}
is a complex line bundle is statement (i) from Lemma
5.19
. Now let us show
\nabla^{0}
is well-defined. Fix
V\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{0})
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
and suppose
W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
are two lifts of
V
and
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
are two lifts of
\sigma
. Then, by construction we have

(\pi_{0})_{*}W_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=(\pi_{0})_{*}W_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=V

and

\kappa(\tau_{1})=\kappa(\tau_{2})=\sigma.

Hence, by (i)

\nabla_{W_{1}}\tau_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\nabla_{W_{2}}\tau_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}.

Since both
\nabla_{W_{1}}\tau_{1}
and
\nabla_{W_{2}}\tau_{2}
are equivariant sections of
\mathcal{L}
, we conclude by (iii) of Lemma
5.19
that

\kappa(\nabla_{W_{1}}\tau_{1})=\kappa(\nabla_{W_{2}}\tau_{2}).

Hence,
\nabla^{0}
is well-defined.
\nabla^{0}
is also clearly smooth since the image of
\kappa
is the smooth sections of
\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
. Finally, we show
\nabla^{0}
is prequantum. Fix
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{0})
with lifts
W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and a section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
with a lift
\tau\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
. Unfolding definitions shows

[\nabla^{0}_{V_{1}},\nabla^{0}_{V_{2}}]\sigma=\kappa([\nabla_{W_{1}},\nabla_{W_{2}}]\tau)

and since the map
(\pi_{0})_{*}:\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}\to\mathfrak{X}(M_{0})
is a Lie algebra homomorphism,

\nabla^{0}_{[V_{1},V_{2}]}\sigma=\kappa(\nabla_{[W_{1},W_{2}]}\tau).

Hence,

\displaystyle[\nabla^{0}_{V_{1}},\nabla^{0}_{V_{2}}]\sigma-\nabla^{0}_{[V_{1},V_{2}]}\sigma

\displaystyle=\kappa([\nabla_{W_{1}},\nabla_{W_{2}}]\tau-\nabla_{[W_{1},W_{2}]}\tau)

\displaystyle=\kappa(-i\omega(W_{1},W_{2})\tau)

\displaystyle=-i\omega_{0}(V_{1},V_{2})\sigma.

where we used the fact that
\nabla
is prequantum and that
\omega|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\pi_{0}^{*}\omega_{0}
. Thus,
\nabla^{0}
is prequantum.

∎

Now let us fix a
G
-invariant polarization
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
. If
\mathcal{P}
is reducible as in Definition
4.31
, then we can reduce the polarization to get a new polarization
\mathcal{P}_{0}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M_{0}
. The question now is what is the relationship between the quantization of
M
with respect to
\mathcal{P}
and
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
and that of the reduced space? Guillemin and Sternberg showed that provided a strong relationship in the compact and free case.

Theorem 5.23

(
[
GS82
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
be an equivariant prequantum line bundle and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
a
G
-invariant positive polarization. Suppose further both
M
and
G
are compact and
G
acts freely on
M
. Then the map
\kappa:\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\Gamma(L_{0})
from Lemma
5.19
restricts to a bijection

\kappa:\mathcal{Q}(M)^{G}\to\mathcal{Q}(M_{0}).

This Theorem is the archetypal
[Q,R]=0
result. We can provide a partial generalization for arbitrary proper actions and polarizations.

Theorem 5.24
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
be an equivariant prequantum line bundle and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
a
G
-invariant reducible polarization.

(1)

The quantization
Q(M)\subseteq\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
is a linear subrepresentation with respect to the canonical action

G\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L});\quad(g,\sigma)\mapsto g\cdot\sigma,

where

(g\cdot\sigma)(x)=g\cdot\sigma(g^{-1}\cdot x).

(2)

The map
\kappa:\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\Gamma(L_{0})
from Lemma
5.19
restricts to a map

\kappa:Q(M)^{G}\to Q(M_{0}).

(72)

Proof.

(1)

Since
G
is connected, it suffices to show
\xi\cdot\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(M)
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(M)
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. To that end, fix
\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(M)
,
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, and
V\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
. Then, using the equivariance property of
\nabla
, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla_{V}(\xi\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{V}(\nabla_{\xi_{M}}\sigma+iJ^{\xi}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{V}\nabla_{\xi_{M}}\sigma-i(VJ^{\xi})\sigma,

where we used the derivation property of the connection and the fact that
\nabla_{V}\sigma=0
. Observe that by definition

\nabla_{V}\nabla_{\xi_{M}}\sigma=\nabla_{\xi_{M}}\nabla_{V}\sigma+\nabla_{[V,\xi_{M}]}\sigma+i\omega(V,\xi_{M})\sigma

Since
\mathcal{P}
is invariant under the
G
-action, we have
[V,\xi_{M}]\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
and hence the terms
\nabla_{\xi_{M}}\nabla_{V}\sigma
and
\nabla_{[V,\xi_{M}]}\sigma
vanish. Furthermore, by definition of the momentum map, we have

\omega(V,\xi_{M})=-VJ^{\xi}\sigma

Thus,

\nabla_{V}(\xi\cdot\sigma)=-iVJ^{\xi}\sigma+iVJ^{\xi}\sigma=0.

Thus,
\xi\cdot\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(M)
.

(2)

Suppose
\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(M)^{G}
and let
\kappa(\sigma)\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
be the induced section on the reduced prequantum line bundle. To show
\kappa(\sigma)\in\mathcal{Q}(M_{0})
, let
x\in J^{-1}(0)
and
v\in(\mathcal{P}_{0})_{[x]}
be an element of the reduced polarization and choosing a lift
\widetilde{v}\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
. Then, by the definition of
\nabla^{0}
, we have

\nabla^{0}_{v}\kappa(\sigma)=\kappa(\nabla_{\widetilde{v}}\sigma)=\kappa(0)=0.

∎

20
Non-Singular [Q,R]=0 Examples

20.1
Cotangent Bundles

With this, we can now show that quantization commutes with reduction for cotangent bundles. Let
G
be a connected Lie group ,
Q
a proper
G
-space, and
J:T^{*}Q\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the Hamiltonian lift as in Definition
3.37
. Writing
\theta_{Q}\in\Omega^{1}(Q)
for the Liouville
1
-form, we get a canonical equivariant prequantum line bundle given by
\mathcal{L}_{Q}=T^{*}Q\times\mathbb{C}
and prequantum connection

\nabla^{Q}=L-i\theta_{Q},

where
L
denotes the Lie derivative operator. Via Theorem
3.61
, the reduction
(T^{*}Q)_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G
is canonically symplectomorphic to
T^{*}(Q/G)
. Letting
\pi:Q\to Q/G
be the quotient map, recall that the dual of the projection
T\pi:TQ\to\pi^{-1}T(Q/G)
,

\phi:\pi^{-1}T^{*}(Q/G)\to J^{-1}(0),

has image in
J^{-1}(0)
and induces a symplectomorphism
\widetilde{\phi}:T^{*}(Q/G)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}
. Not only that, but as we saw, the Liouville
1
-form
\theta_{Q}
induces a
1
-form
\theta_{0}
on
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
which pulls back to the Liouville
1
-form
\theta_{Q/G}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}(Q/G))
. Finally, writing
\mathcal{P}_{Q}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}(T^{*}Q)
and
\mathcal{P}_{Q/G}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}(T^{*}(Q/G))
for the canonical real polarizations, we saw in Proposition
4.39
that
\mathcal{P}_{Q}
is reducible and induces a polarization
\mathcal{P}_{0}
on
T^{*}(Q/G)
with the property that
T\widetilde{\phi}(\mathcal{P}_{Q/G})=\mathcal{P}_{0}
. With this, we are now ready to prove the following.

Theorem 5.25
.

Suppose
Q
has only one orbit-type.

(i)

(\mathcal{L}_{Q},\nabla^{Q})
is reducible. In particular,

(\mathcal{L}_{Q}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/G\cong(T^{*}Q)_{0}\times\mathbb{C}

and the induced prequantum connection
\nabla^{0}
has the form

\nabla^{0}=L-i\theta_{0},

where
\theta_{0}\in\Omega^{1}((T^{*}Q)_{0})
is as in Lemma
3.59
.

(ii)

The natural map

\kappa:\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)^{G}\to\mathcal{Q}((T^{*}Q)_{0})

is a bijection. In particular, there are two canonical linear isomorphisms

\mathcal{Q}((T^{*}Q)_{0})\to\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}(Q/G))

and

\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)^{G}\to\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}(Q/G))

which make the diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)^{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}((T^{*}Q)_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}(Q/G))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

Proof.

(i)

Have
\mathcal{L}_{Q}=T^{*}Q\times\mathbb{C}
. Hence,

\mathcal{L}_{Q}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=J^{-1}(0)\times\mathbb{C}.

Since
G
only acts on the first factor, the statement that
\mathcal{L}_{Q}|_{J^{-1}(0)}
is generated by
G
-invariant sections is equivalent to the statement that

Q\times C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0),\mathbb{C})^{G}\to Q\times\mathbb{C};\quad(q,f)\mapsto(q,f(q))

is surjective. Clearly this holds. Thus,
\mathcal{L}_{Q}
induces a prequantum line bundle
(\mathcal{L}_{0},\nabla^{0})\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}
. Clearly,

\mathcal{L}_{0}=(T^{*}Q)_{0}\times\mathbb{C}.

Now, let
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}
be the quotient map. The map

\pi_{0}^{*}:C^{\infty}((T^{*}Q)_{0},\mathbb{C})\to C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0),\mathbb{C})^{G}

is a bijection. Hence, the canonical map

\kappa:\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{Q})^{G}\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})

can be identified with the map

\kappa:C^{\infty}(T^{*}Q,\mathbb{C})^{G}\to C^{\infty}((T^{*}Q)_{0},\mathbb{C});\quad f\mapsto(\pi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(f|_{J^{-1}(0)}).

Now, suppose
V\in\mathfrak{X}((T^{*}Q)_{0})
and
f\in C^{\infty}((T^{*}Q)_{0},\mathbb{C})
. Choose lifts
W\in\mathfrak{X}(T^{*}Q,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and
h\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}Q,\mathbb{C})^{G}
. Then,

\nabla^{0}_{V}f=\kappa(\nabla_{W}h)=\kappa(Wh+i\theta_{Q}(W)h)=Vf+i\theta_{0}(V)f

Hence the claim.

(ii)

For all three quantizations, the prequantum connections have the form

\nabla=d+i\theta,

where
\theta
vanishes on the vector bundle fibres. Furthermore, the polarizations are given by vectors tangent to the vector bundle fibres. Hence, the quantizations all coincide with the polarized functions. Since

\widetilde{\phi}:T^{*}(Q/G)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}

is a symplectomorphism which preserves the polarizations, the pullback defines a bijection

\widetilde{\phi}^{*}:\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}((T^{*}Q)_{0})\to\tau^{*}_{Q/G}C^{\infty}(Q/G,\mathbb{C})

This gives us our first bijection. As for the second, note that
\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q,\mathbb{C})=\tau^{*}_{Q}C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C})
and
\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}(Q/G))=\tau_{Q/G}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q/G,\mathbb{C})
. Since
\tau_{Q}
is equivariant, we have

(\tau^{*}_{Q}C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C}))^{G}=\tau_{Q}^{*}(C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C})^{G})

The quotient map
\pi:Q\to Q/G
defines a bijection

\pi^{*}:C^{\infty}(Q/G,\mathbb{C})\to C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C})^{G}

Furthermore,
\tau_{Q}^{*}:C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C})\to\tau_{Q}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C})
and
\tau_{Q/G}^{*}:C^{\infty}(Q/G,\mathbb{C})\to\tau_{Q/G}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q/G,\mathbb{C})
are also bijections since
\tau_{Q}
and
\tau_{Q/G}
are surjective submersions. Thus, chaining this all together, we get a chain of bijections

\lambda=\tau_{Q/G}^{*}\circ(\pi^{*})^{-1}\circ(\tau_{Q}^{*})^{-1}:\tau_{Q}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C})^{G}\to\tau_{Q/G}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q/G,\mathbb{C}).

It is then a simple matter of a diagram chase to show

\widetilde{\phi}^{*}\circ\kappa=\lambda.

∎

We will discuss the case where
G
does not have a single orbit type when we get to singular spaces.

20.2
Geometric Quantization of Toric Manifolds

Recall Delzant’s construction of a toric manifold. Let

\Delta=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ \langle x,v_{i}\rangle\leq\lambda_{i},\ i=1,\dots,N\}

be a Delzant polytope with inward pointing normals
v_{i}
. For all that follows, we let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})
. Recall that as part of Delzant’s construction, we obtain a toric
T^{n}
manifold

J_{\Delta}:(M_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

via the symplectic reduction of standard symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{N}
with respect to the action of a subtorus
K\leq(S^{1})^{N}
. Specifically, letting
k\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
be the kernel of the map

\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{n};\quad e_{i}\mapsto v_{i}

and
K=\exp(k)\subseteq(S^{1})^{N}
, then

M_{\Delta}=(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)/K,

where

J:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N};\quad(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(|z_{1}|^{2},\dots,|z_{N}|^{2})-\lambda

and
\iota^{*}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
is the dual of the inclusion
\iota:\mathfrak{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
. We can then identify
(S^{1})^{N}/K=T^{n}
a
n
-dimensional torus.

Theorem 5.26
.

In the setup above,
J_{\Delta}:(M_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
admits a
T^{n}
-equivariant prequantum line bundle if both
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
and
\iota^{*}(\lambda)
lies in the integral lattice of
k
.

Proof.

Assume
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
and
\iota^{*}(\lambda)\in\mathfrak{k}^{*}
lie in the integral lattices. Writing
(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
for the complex coordinates of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{N})
for the real coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we have a canonical primitive for the canonical symplectic form

\alpha=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{2}d\theta_{j}.

With this, the trivial line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{can}=\mathbb{C}^{N}\times\mathbb{C}
with connection

\nabla^{can}=d+i\alpha

is a prequantum line bundle over
(\mathbb{C}^{N},\omega_{can})
. Since
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, we get a representation

\rho:T^{N}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times};\quad(e^{i\theta_{1}},\dots,e^{i\theta_{N}})\mapsto e^{-i\langle\lambda,(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{N})\rangle}.

It is then a straightforward matter to show for all
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{N},\mathbb{C})
and
j=1,\dots,N
that with respect to this representation,

e_{j}\cdot f=\partial_{\theta_{j}}f-i\lambda_{j}f

where
e_{1},\dots,e_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
are the standard basis. Hence, letting

J:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N};\quad(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(|z_{1}|^{2},\dots,|z_{N}|^{2})-\lambda

we obtain

\xi\cdot f=\nabla_{\xi_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}}f+iJ^{\xi}f

hence
(\mathcal{L}_{can},\nabla^{can})\to(\mathbb{C}^{N},\omega_{can})
is a
T^{N}
-equivariant prequantum line bundle. Since
\iota^{*}(\lambda)
is also assumed to be integral, we automatically obtain that
(\mathcal{L}_{can},\nabla^{can})\to(\mathbb{C}^{N},\omega_{can})
is also a
K
-equivariant prequantum line bundle. Since
K
acts freely on
J^{-1}(0)
, by Lemma
5.19

\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}:=(\mathcal{L}_{can}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/K=(J^{-1}(0)\times\mathbb{C})/K

is a complex line bundle over
M_{\Delta}=J^{-1}(0)/K
and comes equipped with a canonical prequantum connection
\nabla^{\Delta}
. Note that since the action of
K
is free on
J^{-1}(0)\times\mathbb{C}
, there is a natural action of
T^{n}\cong T^{N}/K
on
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
making

\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}\to M_{\Delta}

into an equivariant complex line bundle. I claim this is an equivariant prequantum connection. Indeed, recall the definition of
\nabla^{\Delta}
. Take
V\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{\Delta})
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})
. Then we can find lifts
\widetilde{V}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{C}^{N})^{K}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{can})^{K}
. We then set

\nabla^{\Delta}_{V}\sigma=\kappa(\nabla^{can}_{\widetilde{V}}\widetilde{\sigma}),

where

\kappa:\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{can})^{K}\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})

is the surjection as in Lemma
5.19
. In particular, taking
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})
, choose
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{N},\mathbb{C})^{K}
such that
\pi(\eta)=\xi
and so that
\kappa(f)=\sigma
. It’s then a simple matter to show

\kappa(\eta\cdot f)=\xi\cdot\sigma.

From here, it then follows that

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\sigma

\displaystyle=\kappa(\eta\cdot f)

\displaystyle=\kappa(\nabla^{can}_{\eta_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}}f+iJ^{\eta}f)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\Delta}_{\xi_{M}}f+iJ_{\Delta}^{\xi}\sigma.

Hence the conclusion.
∎

Now, let
I_{can}
be the canonical complex structure on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Since
I_{can}
is
K
-equivariant and the action of
K
on
J^{-1}(0)
is free, it induces a Kähler structure
I_{\Delta}
on
M_{\Delta}
. This induced Kähler structure is also
T^{n}
invariant. We will write
\mathcal{P}_{\Delta}=T^{0,1}_{I_{\Delta}}M_{\Delta}
.

Theorem 5.27

(
[
Ham08
]
)
.

For any element
a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}
, write

\psi_{a}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C};\quad(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\mapsto z_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots z_{n}^{a_{n}}.

Then there is a canonical isomorphism of
T^{n}
representations

\mathcal{Q}(M_{\Delta},\mathcal{P}_{\Delta},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})\cong\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\psi_{a}\ |\ a\in\Delta\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}\}.

Proof.

Let
T^{0,1}\mathbb{C}^{N}
denote the antiholomorphic tangent bundle of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
with respect to the canonical complex structure. Since
T^{0,1}\mathbb{C}^{N}
has a canonical trivialization, being spanned by the anti-holomorphic vector fields
\partial/\partial\overline{z_{i}}
, we see that a function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{N},\mathbb{C})
lies in the quantization
\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{C}^{N})
if and only if

\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z_{j}}}}f=0

for all
j=1,\dots,N
. Making use of the fact hat

\nabla^{can}=d+i\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{2}d\theta_{j}

we easily deduce that
f\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{C}^{N})
if and only if there exists holomorphic function
h\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that

f(z)=h(z)e^{-2\pi\|z\|^{2}},

where
\|\cdot\|
is the canonical
L^{2}
norm on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Note that the factor
e^{-2\pi\|z\|^{2}}
is invariant under the
T^{N}
action, hence as
T^{N}
spaces we have a canonical isomorphism between
\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{C}^{N})
and the space of holomorphic functions
h:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{C}
where we have equipped
\mathbb{C}
with the action induced by
\lambda
. In particular, we have

\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{C}^{N})^{K}\cong\{h\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{C}^{N})\ |\ h(t\cdot z)=\rho(t)h(z)\text{ for all }t\in K\}.

For any element
a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, write

z^{a}:=z_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots z_{N}^{a_{N}}.

Then, as is shown in
[
Ham08
]
, the space of equivariant holomorphic functions has basis

\{z^{a}\ |\ a\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}\cap\Delta\},

Since
K
acts freely on
J^{-1}(0)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\Delta}
is a Kähler structure, it follows then from Theorem
5.24
that

\mathcal{Q}(M_{\Delta})\cong\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{C}^{N})^{K}\cong\{\psi_{a}\ |\ a\in\Delta\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}\}.

∎

Part II   Differentiable Stratified Spaces

†
†
margin:

6

Subcartesian Spaces

Subcartesian structures are, along with diffeologies (e.g.
[
IZ13
]
) and stacks (e.g.
[
Vis05
]
) one the main ways to studying the differential geometry of singular spaces, i.e. spaces which are not manifolds. This approach dates back to the work of Aronszajn and Smith
[
AS61
]
in their study of Bessel potentials, and was subsequently generalized in the works of authors like Marshall
[
Mar75
]
to a extension of the theory of smooth manifolds.

The idea of subcartesian spaces is quite simple. Take a topological space
X
, locally embed it into some Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then restrict differential geometric constructions on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
to
X
suitably. This simple idea gets us a lot of mileage as we can get vector fields, tangent bundles, and even flows on singular spaces in this fashion (see
[
Śn13
]
or
[
KL23
]
for more information on flows). Of course it is not quite as simple as I have presented it, as we need an a priori notion of a what a “smooth function” means on the topological space
X
in order to make the local embeddings coherent. This is where the notion of a differential (also known as a Sikorski) structure comes into play.

Differential structures, as their alternate name suggests, are due to Sikorski
[
Sik67
]
. They provide an abstract framework for discussing “differentiable functions” on a topological space which allows for an easy generalization of constructions in smooth geometry which only depend on the smooth functions.
