, the connected components of
S\cap S(V)
lie in
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(S(V),\widehat{\mathscr{F}})
.

With this result in hand, we can now prove that the dimension-type partition truly is a locally finite singular foliation with embedded leaves, hence a stratification.

Theorem 7.36

(
[
MR25
]
)
.

The dimension-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is a locally finite singular foliation with embedded leaves.

Proof.

Let’s first prove
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is locally finite. This is an inductive argument based on the local normal form. First, it suffices to show the dimension-type partition of an infinitesimal foliation is finite. Indeed, if this is the case then around any point
x\in M
contained in dimension-type piece
S\in\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
there is a local foliate diffeomorphism between a neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
of
x
and
V\subseteq T_{x}M

\phi:U\subseteq M\to V\subseteq T_{x}S\oplus\nu_{x}(M,S)

(81)

Then since
\mathcal{S}_{\nu_{x}\mathscr{F}}(\nu_{x}(M,S))
is finite, it follows that only finitely many elements of
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
intersect
U
non-trivially.

Now let us fix an infinitesimal foliation
(V,\mathscr{F})
. We prove by induction on
\dim V
that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathscr{F}}(V)
is finite. First, if
\dim V=0
then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathscr{F}}(\{0\})
is a singleton, hence finite. Let us now assume the claim is true for infinitesimal foliations on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
for all
k<n
. Choosing a point
x
in the sphere
S(V)
of
V
and applying the local normal form in Theorem
7.33
, we get
(V,\mathscr{F})
is locally diffeomorphic to a regular foliation times an infinitesimal singular Riemannian foliation of strictly less dimension than
V
, hence is finite there. Since
S(V)
is compact, we can cover it by finitely many neighbourhoods where the intersection is finite. Using the homothety property (see Lemma
7.32
), we deduce that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathscr{F}}(V)
is finite.

Now to show that
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is a singular foliation. Due to the local normal form in Equation (
81
), it suffices to assume

(M,\mathscr{F})=(V_{1}\times V_{2},\mathscr{F}_{1}\times\mathscr{F}_{2})

where
V_{1},V_{2}
are vector spaces,
\mathscr{F}_{1}
is a regular foliation on
V_{1}
, and
\mathscr{F}_{2}
a singular foliation on
V_{2}
with
\{0\}
being the only leaf.

Claim 1:
If
W\in\mathfrak{X}(V_{1}\times V_{2})
is foliate, i.e. the flow
\phi_{t}^{W}
is a foliate diffeomorphism for all
t
, then
W\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathscr{F})
.

Fix a foliate vector field
W
. It suffices to show

W|_{V_{1}\times\{0\}}\in\mathfrak{X}(V_{1}\times\{0\}).

Indeed, fix a point
(x_{0},0)\in V_{1}\times\{0\}
and let
(x_{t},y_{t})
be an integral curve of
W
through
(x_{0},0)
. Note that since
W
is foliate, we have

\dim L_{(x_{0},0)}=\dim L_{(x_{t},y_{t})}

for all
t
. In particular, since
\{0\}
is the only
0
-dimensional leaf of
\mathscr{F}_{2}
and
\mathscr{F}_{1}
is regular, it follows that
y_{t}=0
for all
t
. In particular,

W_{(x_{0},0)}=(\dot{x}_{0},\dot{y}_{0})=(\dot{x}_{0},0)\in T_{(x_{0},0)}(V_{1}\times\{0\}).

Claim 2
: For all
v\in T_{(0,0)}V_{1}\times\{0\}
, we can find a foliate vector field
W\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathscr{F})
so that
W_{(0,0)}=(v,0)
.

Fix
v\in V_{1}
. Observe that the directional derivative along
v
, denoted
\frac{\partial}{\partial v}
, is foliate. Indeed, if
L^{\prime}_{v_{2}}
denotes the leaf of
v_{2}\in V_{2}
, then for every
(v_{1},v_{2})\in V_{1}\times V_{2}
,

L_{(v_{1},v_{2})}=(L+v_{1})\times L^{\prime}_{v_{2}}.

Then, for any
t
, if
\phi_{t}
denotes the flow of
\partial/\partial v
, we have

\phi_{t}(L_{(v_{1},v_{2})})=(L+v_{1}+tv)\times L^{\prime}_{v_{2}}=L_{\phi_{t}(v_{1},v_{2})}.

Clearly
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\bigg|_{(0,0)}=(v,0)
.

Using Claim 1 and Claim 2 together show that condition (S) in Definition
7.15
is satisfied locally by foliate vector fields. Using partitions of unity, we deduce that
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M)
is a singular foliation.
∎

Corollary 7.37
.

The dimension-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is a stratification by totally geodesic submanifolds.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is locally finite and the leaves are embedded submanifolds, the result follow immediately from Corollary
7.24
.
∎

Remark 7.38
.

(1)

Henceforth, we shall be calling the partition
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M)
of an SRF the
dimension-type stratification
.

(2)

Given any singular foliation
\mathscr{F}
of a manifold
M
, we can define a dimension-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
as in Definition
7.31
. However, in general these will not be stratifications. Indeed, consider the singular foliation
\mathscr{F}
on
\mathbb{R}
from Example
7.20
where the
0
-dimensional leaves are given by the points in the cantor set
C
and the
1
-dimensional leaves are given by the connected components of
\mathbb{R}\setminus C
. I claim
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is not locally finite. Indeed, observe that since
C
is totally disconnected, we have

\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})=\mathscr{F}

In which case, we see that if
x\in C
and
U
is any open subset of
\mathbb{R}
containing
x
, then
U\cap C
is infinite. Thus,
\mathscr{F}
is not locally finite which implies the dimension-type partition is not locally finite.

Although the dimension-type partition may not be a stratification, it is still a singular foliation! Perhaps a more general result could be proven that the dimension-type partition is always a singular foliation which could make use of foliate vector fields as in the proof of Theorem
7.36
.

(3)

As a final remark on the dimension-type stratification of a singular Riemannian foliation, it is also worth noting that while the dimension-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is a singular foliation, it will almost never be singular Riemannian. Indeed, consider the standard infinitesimal SRF
\mathscr{F}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with leaves given by the origin and concentric circles about the origin. The dimension-type stratification is quite easy to compute in this case, given by

\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})=\{\{(0,0)\},\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}\}.

Consider now the geodesic

\gamma(t)=(t,0),\quad t\in\mathbb{R}

Trivially,
\gamma
is orthogonal to the zero-dimensional leaf
\{(0,0)\}
, but it is not orthogonal to the dense leaf
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
. Thus,
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
fails condition (O) from Definition
7.29
.

28
Stratifications Arising From Proper Group Actions

Let us now discuss an interesting class of stratifications arising from proper group actions. Namely, the orbit-type, infinitesimal-type, and reduced orbit-type. To set up our analysis, we will need to first delve into the foliation by manifolds of symmetry.

For all that follows, we will be letting
G
be a connected Lie group,
\mathfrak{g}=\text{Lie}(G)
its Lie algebra, and
M
a proper
G
-space.

28.1
Orbit-Type Stratifications From Proper Group Actions

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
M
a proper
G
-space. Although the quotient space
M/G
need not be a manifold, it does have a canonical decomposition into manifolds called the
canonical stratification
. The main ingredient for this decomposition is the orbit-type partition discussed in Example
7.18
. Let us now give a formal definition to set up some notation.

Definition 7.39

(Orbit-Type and Canonical Partitions)
.

Define the orbit-type partition of
M
by

\mathcal{S}_{G}(M):=\bigcup_{H\leq G}\pi_{0}(M_{(H)}).

We then define the canonical partition of
M/G
by

\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G):=\{S/G\ |\ S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)\}.

We will now show that both
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
and
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
are differentiable stratifications of their respective subcartesian spaces.

Lemma 7.40
.

The pieces in the orbit-type
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
and canonical stratifications
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
are topological manifolds in the subspace topology. Furthermore, the induced subcartesian structures on the pieces are smooth manifold structures.

Proof.

As we’ve seen in Corollary
2.15
, the orbit-type pieces are embedded submanifolds. Furthermore, given a point
x\in M
we have a maximal slice neighbourhood
\phi:U\to G\times_{G_{x}}\nu_{x}(M,G)
in which the orbit-type piece
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
containing
x
will have the form

\phi(U\cap S)=G\times_{G_{x}}\nu_{x}(M,G)^{G_{x}}\cong(G/G_{x})\times\nu_{x}(M,G).

Thus, in the quotient
M/G
, the corresponding canonical piece
S/G
will locally have the form

(U\cap S)/G\cong((G/G_{x})\times\nu_{x}(M,G)^{G_{x}})/G\cong\nu_{x}(M,G)

and so the canonical pieces of
M/G
are also topological manifolds. Note that due to Corollary
6.33
, if we choose a Hilbert map
p:\nu_{x}(M,G)\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then the restriction defines an embedding
p:\nu_{x}(M,G)^{G_{x}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
of smooth manifolds. In particular, we see that if
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
is a canonical piece then
S
is a topological manifold in the subspace topology and the induced subcartesian structure
C^{\infty}(S)=\langle C^{\infty}(M/G)|_{S}\rangle
is a smooth manifold structure on
S
.
∎

To continue on our proof that both
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
and
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
are differentiable stratifications, we now need to show that they satisfy the following conditions.

•

Both are locally finite

•

Both satisfy the axiom of the frontier.

Since
\pi:M\to M/G
is a quotient map from a group action, it will suffice to simply show the orbit-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
satisfies these axioms.

Lemma 7.41
.

The orbit-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
is locally finite.

Proof.

Passing to slice coordinates as in the Slice Theorem (Theorem
2.13
), it suffices to show that if
K
is a compact group and
V
is a finite dimensional
K
-representation, then

\mathcal{S}_{K}(V)=\bigcup_{H\leq K}\pi_{0}(V_{(H)})

is finite. By Lemma 4.3.6 in
[
Pfl01
]
, there is a finite list
K_{1},\dots,K_{N}\leq K
of subgroups so that

V=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}V_{(K_{i})}.

Hence, it suffices to show
\pi_{0}(V_{(H)})
is finite for any compact subgroup
H\leq K
. This in turn was proven in Theorem 1.5 from
[
Sch80
]
.
∎

Now that local finiteness is established, we need to show the axiom of the frontier holds. For this, we will use our discsussion of the orbit-type decomposition from Example
7.18
. Namely, we gave an alternate proof to the following result of of Jotz-Ratiu-Sniatycki.

Theorem 7.42

(
[
JRS11
]
)
.

\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
is a singular foliation.

Now we are set up to show that the two partitions from Definition
7.39
are indeed stratifications.

Theorem 7.43
.

Both
(M,\mathcal{S}_{G}(M))
and
(M/G,\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G))
are differentiable stratified spaces and the quotient map
\pi:M\to M/G
is a differentiable stratified morphism.

Proof.

Applying Lemmas
7.40
and
7.41
, together with Theorem
7.42
, we obtain that the orbit-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
is a locally finite singular foliation with embedded leaves, hence by Corollary
7.24
is a differentiable stratification with respect to the smooth structure
C^{\infty}(M)
. Now to discuss the canonical partition
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
of the quotient space
M/G
.

Using Lemma
7.40
once again, we know that the pieces of
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
are topological manifolds. Since
\pi:M\to M/G
is an open quotient map, we easily deduce that
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
inherits local finiteness and the frontier condition from
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
. Hence, a final application of Lemma
7.40
demonstrates that
(M/G,\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G),C^{\infty}(M/G))
is a differentiable stratified space, where

C^{\infty}(M/G)=\{f:M/G\to\mathbb{R}\ |\ \pi^{*}f\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}\}.

∎

Before we end this discussion on orbit-type stratifications, I will record a final local normal form for proper
G
-spaces that will come in handy later when we discuss regularity conditions.

Lemma 7.44
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
K\leq G
a compact subgroup, and
V
a finite-dimensional
K
-representation. Choose a
K
-invariant metric on
\mathfrak{g}
and let
\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
be the orthogonal complement to
\mathfrak{k}=\text{Lie}(K)
. Then the map

\phi:\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}\times V\to G\times_{K}V;\quad(\xi,v)\mapsto[\exp(\xi),v]

has the following properties.

(1)

\phi
is a
K
-equivariant smooth map which defines a diffeomorphism in a neighbourhood of
(0,0)
.

(2)

If
U\subseteq\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}\times V
is a neighbourhood of
(0,0)
so that
\phi
is a diffeomorphism onto open
W=\phi(U)
, then for any closed subgroup
H\leq K
we have

\phi(U\cap(\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}\oplus V)_{(H)})=W\cap(G\times_{K}V)_{(H)}.

Proof.

(1)

Let
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}
and
v\in V
. Then for any
k\in K
we see that

k\cdot\phi(\xi,v)=\phi(\text{Ad}_{k}(\xi),k\cdot v)=[k\exp(\xi)k^{-1},k\cdot v].

Next, we see that if we identify
T_{[e,0]}G\times_{K}V=\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k}\oplus V
, then
T_{(0,0)}\phi
can be identified with the map

T_{(0,0)}\phi:\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}\oplus V\to(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})\oplus V;\quad(\xi,v)\mapsto(\xi+\mathfrak{k},v)

which is clearly an isomorphism. Hence,
\phi
defines a diffeomorphism in a neighbourhood of
(0,0)
.

(2)

Follows from equivariance.

∎

28.2
Reduced Orbit-Type and Infinitesimal Stratifications

We get another stratification from a proper group action, namely the one arising from the induced Lie algebra action. We call this the infinitesimal stratification. Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
\mathfrak{g}
its Lie algebra, and
M
a proper
G
-space. For any Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
, define

M_{(\mathfrak{h})}:=\{x\in M\ |\ \mathfrak{g}_{x}\text{ is conjugate to }\mathfrak{h}\}.

Using this, we define the infinitesimal-type partition of
M
in an analogous fashion as we did for the orbit-type stratification.

Definition 7.45

(Infinitesimal Partition)
.

Define the infinitesimal-type partiton of
M
by

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M):=\bigcup_{x\in M}\pi_{0}(M_{(\mathfrak{g}_{x})}).

Remark 7.46
.

Note that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
only depends on the induced Lie algebra action

\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{X}(M);\quad\xi\mapsto\xi_{M}

Since two distinct Lie groups
G_{1},G_{2}
could induce the same Lie algebra action, we should expect in general that the infinitesimal-type partition should be coarser than the orbit-type partition.

Before we can show that the infinitesimal-type partition is a stratification, we will need a local linear model for the infinitesimal-type pieces.

Proposition 7.47
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
K\leq G
a compact subgroup,
\mathfrak{k}=\text{Lie}(K)
its Lie algebra, and
V
a finite-dimensional
K
-representation. Let

V^{\mathfrak{k}}:=\bigcap_{\xi\in\mathfrak{k}}\ker(\xi_{V}).

Then,

(G\times_{K}V)_{(\mathfrak{k})}=G\times_{K}V^{\mathfrak{k}}.

Proof.

Let
[g,v]\in(G\times_{K}V)_{(\mathfrak{k})}
. Since
G_{[g,v]}
is conjugate to
G_{[e,v]}
and hence
\mathfrak{g}_{[g,v]}
is conjugate to
\mathfrak{g}_{[e,v]}
, we may assume
g=e
. Observe that
\mathfrak{g}_{[e,v]}=\mathfrak{k}_{v}\subseteq\mathfrak{k}
. Since
\mathfrak{k}_{v}
and
\mathfrak{k}
are conjugate and hence of the same dimension, it follows that
\mathfrak{k}_{v}=\mathfrak{k}
and hence
v\in V^{\mathfrak{k}}
.
∎

Proposition 7.48
.

Let
M
be a proper
G
-space.

(1)

Then the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
are embedded
G
-invariant submanifolds.

(2)

The orbit-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
is a refinement of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
. In general, a strict refinement.

Proof.

Applying the Slice Theorem and Proposition
7.47
, we recover (1) and the first statement in (2). To see that
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
is in general a strict refinement of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
, let
G=S^{1}
and
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Define an action of
S^{1}
on
M
by

S^{1}\times M\to M;\quad(z,x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto(zx_{1},z^{2}x_{2}).

Then we see
\mathcal{S}_{S^{1}}(M)
has three pieces,
\{(0,0)\}
,
\{(x_{1},x_{2})\ |\ x_{1}\neq 0\}
, and
\{(0,x_{2})\ |\ x_{2}\neq 0\}
. Conversely, if we let
i\mathbb{R}
denote the Lie algebra of
S^{1}
, then
\mathcal{S}_{i\mathbb{R}}(M)
consists of only two pieces,
\{(0,0)\}
and
M\setminus\{(0,0)\}
.
∎

Before we prove that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
is a stratification of
M
, let us discuss another partition which we get for free from the
G
-action. For any Lie group
G
, write
G^{\circ}
for the connected component containing the identity element. Equivalently, this is the maximal connected subgroup. Given a connected subgroup
K\leq G
, define

M_{(K)}^{\circ}:=\{x\in M\ |\ G_{x}^{\circ}\text{ is conjugate to }K\}.

Definition 7.49

(Reduced Orbit-Type)
.

Let
M
be a proper
G
-space. Define

\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)^{\circ}:=\bigcup_{x\in M}\pi_{0}(M_{(G_{x}^{\circ})}^{\circ}).

Proposition 7.50
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
K\leq G
a compact subgroup, and
V
a finite-dimensional
K
-representation. Then,

(G\times_{K}V)_{(K^{\circ})}=G\times_{K}V^{K^{\circ}}.

Proof.

First let us show that
G\times_{K}V^{K^{\circ}}\subseteq(G\times_{K}V)_{(K^{\circ})}
. Fixing
[g,v]\in G\times_{K}V^{K^{\circ}}
, we have

G_{[g,v]}=gK_{v}g^{-1}.

By definition,
K_{v}\subseteq K
and hence
K_{v}^{\circ}\subseteq K^{\circ}
. However, since we are assuming that
v\in V^{K^{\circ}}
, we also have
K^{\circ}\subseteq K_{v}
. Hence,
G_{[g,v]}^{\circ}=gK^{\circ}g^{-1}
and thus
[g,v]\in(G\times_{K}V)_{(K^{\circ})}
.

The reverse containment easily follows.
∎

Making use of slice coordinates from Theorem
2.13
together with Proposition
7.50
, we easily deduce the following Corollary.

Corollary 7.51
.

The reduced orbit-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)^{\circ}
is a partition of
M
into embedded
G
-invariant submanifolds.

So we now have two auxiliary partitions associated to a proper group action, the infinitesimal-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
and the reduced orbit-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)^{\circ}
. In turns out that we can use our results about singular Riemannian foliations to show that these partitions are the same and define a stratification of
M
.

Proposition 7.52

(
[
PTW17
]
)
.

For a proper
G
-space
M
, let
\mathscr{F}_{G}
denote the singular foliation by orbits. Then the infinitesimal partition
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
, the reduced orbit-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)^{\circ}
, and the dimension-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F}_{G})
are all equal. That is,

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)=\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)^{\circ}=\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F}_{G}).

Proof.

We first show
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)=\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)^{\circ}
. Indeed let
x,y\in M
and suppose
G_{x}^{\circ}
and
G_{y}^{\circ}
are conjugate. Since
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{y}
are the Lie algebras of
G_{x}^{\circ}
and
G_{y}^{\circ}
, it follows that
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{y}
are conjugate. Conversely, suppose
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{y}
are conjugate by
g\in G
. That is,
\text{Ad}_{g}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})=\mathfrak{g}_{y}
. Write

c_{g}:G\to G;\quad a\mapsto gag^{-1}

for conjugation by
g
. Since the exponential maps
\exp:\mathfrak{g}_{x}\to G_{x}^{\circ}
and
\exp:\mathfrak{g}_{y}\to G_{y}^{\circ}
have dense image and for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}_{x}
we have

\exp(\text{Ad}_{g}(\xi))=c_{g}(\exp(\xi)),

it follows that the diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{x}^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}_{y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{y}^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Ad}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}

Hence
G_{x}^{\circ}
and
G_{y}^{\circ}
are conjugate.

Now, we show
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F}_{G})=\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)^{\circ}
using the argument presented in the paper
[
PTW17
]
. To do this, observe that for any
x\in M
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dim(G\cdot x)&=\dim(G/G_{x})\\
&=\dim(G)-\dim(G_{x})\\
&=\dim(G)-\dim(G_{x}^{\circ}).\end{split}

(82)

In particular, if
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)^{\circ}
is the reduced orbit-type piece containing
x
and
R\in\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F}_{G})
is the dimension-type piece containing
x
, then
S\subseteq R
. To show
S=R
we show
S
is both closed and open in
R
. Since
R
is assumed to be connected, this will show
S=R
.

Indeed, let
y\in\overline{S}\cap R
lie in the relative closure of
S
in
R
. We choose a slice neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
around
y
which is equivariantly diffeomorphic to
G\times_{G_{y}}\nu_{y}(M,G)
and let
[g,v]\in G\times_{G_{y}}\nu_{y}(M,G)
correspond to
x\in S\cap U
. Then
G_{x}=G_{[g,v]}=g(G_{y})_{v}g^{-1}
. Due to Equation (
82
), we see that the dimension of the orbit through
x
is
\dim(G)-\dim((G_{y})_{v})
and the dimension of the orbit through
y
is
\dim(G)-\dim(G_{y})
. Since
x,y
lie in the same dimension-type this implies

\dim((G_{y})_{v})=\dim(G_{y}).

Since
(G_{y})_{v}^{\circ}\subseteq G_{y}^{\circ}
and
(G_{y})_{v}^{\circ}
is closed, we obtain
(G_{y})_{v}^{\circ}=G_{y}^{\circ}
and thus
G_{x}^{\circ}
is conjugate to
G_{y}^{\circ}
. Therefore,
y\in S
. Hence
\overline{S}\cap R=S\cap R
and
S
is closed in
R
.

To show
S
is open, we use the same argument.
∎

Corollary 7.53
.

The infinitesimal partition
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
is a singular foliation, hence a differentiable stratification of
M
.

Proof.

Since the action of
G
on
M
is proper, by a Theorem of Palais
[
Pal61
]
, there exists a
G
-invariant Riemannian metric on
M
making the orbit foliation
\mathscr{F}_{G}
into an SRF. By Theorem
7.36
, the dimension partition
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F}_{G})
is a singular foliation hence a stratification. Applying Proposition
7.52
, we obtain the desired conclusion.
∎

29
Stratified Tangent Vectors and Vector Fields

Just as we had tangent bundles for subcartesian spaces and tangent bundles for foliations, we can also obtain a notion of a tangent bundle and hence vector fields for differentiable stratified spaces. These will inherit many of the structures from Zariski tangent bundles. Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a weakly differentiable stratified space and
S\in\Sigma
a stratum. Differentiating the inclusion
S\hookrightarrow X
, we get a natural injection
TS\hookrightarrow TX
. This allows us to define the following.

Definition 7.54

(Stratified Tangent Bundle)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a weakly differentiable stratified space. Define the stratified tangent bundle by

T\Sigma:=\bigcup_{S\in\Sigma}TS

with canonical subcartesian structure
C^{\infty}(T\Sigma):=\langle C^{\infty}(TX)|_{T\Sigma}\rangle

Example 7.55
.

Given a smooth manifold
M
together with its canonical stratification
\pi_{0}(M)
by connected components, then the stratified tangent bundle

T\pi_{0}(M)=\bigcup_{S\in\pi_{0}(M)}TS

is exactly the same thing as the usual tangent bundle
TM
by Lemma
6.54
.

Example 7.56
.

If
(M,\mathscr{F})
is a foliated manifold with
\mathscr{F}
satisfying the hypotheses of Corollary
7.24
, then the foliation tangent bundle is the same as the stratified tangent bundle.

Definition 7.57
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a weakly differentiable stratified space.

(1)

Fix
S\in\Sigma
and
x\in S
. Then a stratified tangent vector at
x
is an element of
T_{x}S
. Write
T_{x}\Sigma
for the stratified tangent vectors at
x
.

(2)

A stratified vector field is a smooth section
V:X\to T\Sigma
of the canonical projection
\pi:T\Sigma\to X
. Write
\mathfrak{X}(X,\Sigma)
or, if context is clear,
\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
for the set of all stratified vector fields.

Proposition 7.58
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a weakly differentiable stratified space. Then
\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)\subseteq\mathfrak{X}(X)
with the inclusion being strict in general. Furthermore,

\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)=\bigcap_{S\in\Sigma}\mathfrak{X}(X,S),

(83)

where
\mathfrak{X}(X,S)
is as in Definition
6.57
.

Proof.

Since the differentiable structure on
T\Sigma
is the restriction of the differentiable structure on
TX
, it follows from Proposition
6.56
that
\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)\subseteq\mathfrak{X}(X)
.

In general, the above containment is strict. Indeed, consider
X=\mathbb{R}^{2}
together with the stratification

\Sigma=\{\{(0,0)\},\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}\}.

Any stratified vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
must vanish at the origin, which clearly is not true for a general vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

The identity in Equation (
83
) is a straightforward consequence of the definition of
\mathfrak{X}(X,S)
.
∎

Recall from Definition
6.49
that we have a natural Lie algebra structure on the set of Zariski vector fields, namely for
V,W\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
we define
[V,W]\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
by

[V,W](f)=V(W(f))-W(V(f)),\quad f\in C^{\infty}(X).

Using now Corollary
6.59
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 7.59
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a weakly differentiable stratified space and
V,W\in\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
. Then for every stratum
S\in\Sigma
, we have

[V|_{S},W|_{S}]=[V,W]|_{S}

As a final result, we record a hard Theorem of Schwarz that uses techniques that go beyond this thesis.

Theorem 7.60

(
[
Sch80
]
)
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
M
a proper
G
-space. Let
\pi:M\to M/G
be the quotient map.

(1)

If
V\in\mathfrak{X}(M)^{G}
is a
G
-invariant vector, then the fibre-wise application of the derivative map
T\pi:T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)\to T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
defines a stratified vector field
\pi_{*}V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G))
.

(2)

The map

\pi_{*}:\mathfrak{X}(M)^{G}\to\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G));\quad V\mapsto\pi_{*}V

is a surjection.

30
Regularity Conditions

Differentiable Stratified spaces are somewhat pathological in general and the kinds of spaces that appear usually are tamed. For this reason, we impose various regularity conditions on stratified spaces. The most famous of which being the Whitney conditions (see Definition
7.63
). We will also discuss a more useful kind of regularity assumption called Smoothly Locally Trivial With Conical Fibres introduced in
[
Zim24
]
.

30.1
Whitney Conditions

The Whitney conditions are local conditions that restrict how the strata in a stratification can fit together. They use the topology of Grassmannians in a non-trivial way, so let us first review this basic notion.

Definition 7.61

(Rank
k
Grassmannian)
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional vector space and
k\leq\dim(V)
. Define the rank
k
Grassmannian on
V
, denoted
\text{Gr}_{k}(V)
, to be the set of all
k
-dimensional subspaces of
V
. In symbols,

\text{Gr}_{k}(V)=\{W\subseteq V\ |\ W\text{ linear subspace and }\dim(W)=k\}.

Given a vector space
V
and an integer
k\leq\dim(V)
, we can endow
\text{Gr}_{k}(V)
with a topology by equipping
V
with an inner product. Then, given a subspace
W\in\text{Gr}_{k}(V)
let
P_{W}:V\to V
denote the orthogonal projection onto
W
. This defines an injective map

\text{Gr}_{k}(V)\hookrightarrow\text{End}(V);\quad W\mapsto P_{W}

(84)

and thus we can endow
\text{Gr}_{k}(V)
with the subspace topology inherited from the vector space
\text{End}(V)
. Now let

O(V)=\{T:V\to V\ |\ T\text{ is an isometry}\}

Then we have a natural action of
\text{O}(V)
on
\text{End}(V)
by conjugation

\text{O}(V)\times\text{End}(V)\to\text{End}(V);\quad(g,T)\mapsto g\circ T\circ g^{-1}.

One can easily show that if
W\in\text{Gr}_{k}(V)
and
g\in\text{O}(V)
is an isometry, then

g\circ P_{W}\circ g^{-1}=P_{g(W)}.

Clearly for any two
k
-dimensional subspaces
W,W^{\prime}\in\text{Gr}_{k}(V)
we can find an isometry
g
so that
g(W)=W^{\prime}
. Hence,
\text{Gr}_{k}(V)
is the orbit of the linear
\text{O}(V)
action and thus is naturally a compact connected smooth manifold. With this, we are now ready to discuss the Whitney conditions.

Definition 7.62
.

Let
R,S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two embedded submanifolds and
x\in S
. Say
(R,S)
is Whitney regular at
x
if the following two conditions hold.

(A)

If
\{x_{k}\}\subseteq R
is a sequence in
R
converging to
x
and
\tau\subseteq T_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a subspace such that
\{T_{x_{k}}R\}
converges to
\tau
in the
\dim(R)
Grassmannian of
T\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
T_{x}S\subseteq\tau
.

(B)

Suppose
\{y_{k}\}\subseteq S
is another sequence converging to
x
with
x_{k}\neq y_{k}
for all
k
and write
\ell_{k}
for the line connecting
x_{k}
to
y_{k}
. If the sequence
\{\ell_{k}\}
converges to a line
\ell
in the
1
-dimensional Grassmannian of
T\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
\ell\subseteq\tau
.

If
(R,S)
only satisfy (A) at
x
, say they are Whitney (A) at
x
.

Definition 7.63

(Whitney Regular Stratified Spaces)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space. Say
(X,\Sigma)
is Whitney (A) (resp. (B)) if for each pair of strata
(R,S)
with
S\subseteq\overline{R}
, any
x\in S
, and any chart
\phi:U\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
the pair
(\phi(S\cap U),\phi(R\cap U))
is Whitney (A) (resp. (B)) at
x
. If
(X,\Sigma)
satisfies both (A) and (B) at
x
, say
(X,\Sigma)
is Whitney regular.

Example 7.64
.

As we will see, most spaces we’ve discussed so far are Whitney regular. Non-examples of Whitney regular spaces which could be found in
[
Whi15
]
,
[
Mat12
]
, or
[
Pfl01
]
will fail in our context because we demand that if
S,R\in\Sigma
are strata with
S\neq R
and
S\subseteq\overline{R}
then
\dim S<\dim R
.

In the subcartesian setting in which we are working, the Whitney conditions are somewhat unnatural as they have been formulated above. We will now end this section with the discussion of a stronger condition that resembles condition (S) in the definition of a singular foliation (see Definition
7.15
).

Proposition 7.65
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space. If the map

\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)\times X\to T\Sigma;\quad(V,x)\mapsto V_{x}

is surjective, then
(X,\Sigma)
is Whitney (A).

Proof.

For all that follows, fix a differentiable stratified space
(X,\Sigma)
. First note that if

\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)\times X\to T\Sigma;\quad(V,x)\mapsto V_{x}

is surjective, then for any open subset
U\subseteq X
so is

\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma_{U})\times U\to T\Sigma_{U};\quad(V,x)\mapsto V_{x}.

Thus, passing to charts, we may assume that
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a subset. Now suppose
S,R\in\Sigma
are two strata,
x\in S
, and
\{y_{n}\}\subseteq R
is a sequence and
\tau\subseteq T_{x}\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a subspace so that

\lim_{n}y_{n}=x

and

\lim_{n}T_{y_{n}}R=\tau

in the
\dim(R)
Grassmannian of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. We want to show that
T_{x}S\subseteq\tau
. To do this, choose
v\in T_{x}S
and let
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
be a stratified vector field with
V_{x}=v
. Identify
T_{z}\mathbb{R}^{N}=\mathbb{R}^{N}
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and for any subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
, write
P_{W}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
for the orthogonal projection onto
W
with respect to the standard metric on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. By definition of the topology on the
\dim(R)
Grassmannian of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|P_{T_{y_{n}}R}-P_{\tau}\|=0,

where
\|\cdot\|
is the standard operator norm. By continuity of
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{y_{n}}=v

In particular, we conclude

\lim_{n\to\infty}(V_{y_{n}}-P_{W}(V_{y_{n}}))=V_{x}-P(V_{x})=v-P(v)

Furthermore, since
P_{T_{y_{n}}R}(V_{y_{n}})=V_{y_{n}}
we have for any
n

\|V_{y_{n}}-P_{\tau}(V_{y_{n}})\|\leq\|V_{y_{n}}-P_{T_{y_{n}}R}(V_{y_{n}})\|+\|P_{T_{y_{n}}R}(V_{y_{n}})-P_{\tau}(V_{y_{n}})\|\leq\|P_{T_{y_{n}}R}-P_{\tau}\|\cdot\|V_{y_{n}}\|.

Hence,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|V_{y_{n}}-P_{\tau}(V_{y_{n}})\|=\|v-P_{\tau}(v)\|\leq 0\cdot\|v\|=0.

Hence,
P_{\tau}(v)=v
and thus
v\in\tau
.
∎

Example 7.66
.

Let us now return to the union of the coordinate axes
X
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
along with the stratification
\Sigma
given by

\Sigma=\{\{(0,0)\},H_{-},H_{+},V_{-},V_{+}\}

from Example
7.3
. We saw in Example
6.63
that the Zariski vector fields do not span the Zariski tangent bundle. That is, the map

X\times\mathfrak{X}(X)\to TX;\quad(x,V)\mapsto V_{x}

is not surjective. As we saw, this is due to the fact that
T_{(0,0)}X=T_{(0,0)}\mathbb{R}^{2}
, but if
V\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
, then
V_{(0,0)}=0
. However, it’s now easy to see that the stratified vector fields do span the stratified tangent bundle, i.e. the map

X\times\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)\to T\Sigma

is surjective. Hence,
(X,\Sigma)
is Whitney (A).

As a consequence, any stratification of a manifold which is also a singular foliation is Whitney (A). In fact, this also holds more generally.

Corollary 7.67
.

The following are all Whitney (A).

(1)

Let
(M,g,\mathscr{F})
be an SRF. Then the dimension-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is Whitney (A).

(2)

If
G
is a connected Lie group and
M
a proper
G
-space, then both the orbit-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
and the infinitesimal stratification
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
are Whitney (A).

Proof.

(1)

By Theorem
7.36
, the dimension-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is a singular foliation, hence satisfies Proposition
7.65
and so is Whitney (A).

(2)

Same argument holds for the orbit-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
. For the canonical stratification, let
\pi:M\to M/G
be the quotient map,
x\in M
, and
v\in T_{[}x]\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G)
a stratified tangent vector. In particular, if
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
is the orbit-type stratum containing
x
then
v\in T_{[}x](S/G)
hence admits a lift to

\widetilde{v}\in T_{x}S

since the map
S\to S/G
is a surjective submersion. As we saw in Example
7.18
, there exists equivariant vector field
\widetilde{V}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)^{G}
and Lie algebra element
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
so that

\widetilde{V}_{x}+\xi_{M}(x)=\widetilde{v}.

By Theorem
7.60
,
\widetilde{V}
projects to an element of
\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G))
and clearly

T_{x}\pi(\widetilde{v})=T_{x}\pi(\widetilde{V}_{x}+\xi_{M}(x))=T_{x}\pi(\widetilde{V}_{x})=V_{[x]}.

In particular, we have just shown that the map

M/G\times\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G))\to T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G);\quad([x],V)\mapsto V_{[x]}

is surjective and thus
(M/G,\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G))
is Whitney (A) by Proposition
7.65
.

∎

As for Whitney (B), we will also be attempting to dispose of it by making another analogy with singular foliations, namely that they are “locally trivial” in some sense. A necessary result that we will need to make this leap is due to Pflaum.

Proposition 7.68

(
[
Pfl01
]
)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space and
S,R\in\Sigma
two strata with
S\subseteq\overline{R}
and
x\in S
. If there exists a chart
\phi:U\subseteq X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
about
x
so that
(\phi(S\cap U),\phi(R\cap U))
is Whitney (B) at
x
, then for any chart
\psi:W\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{M}
about
x
,
(\phi(S\cap W),\phi(R\cap W))
satisfy Whitney (B).

30.2
Local Triviality

One of the main reasons we care about Whitney (B) is mostly due to its consequences, in particular the existence of control data
[
Mat73
]
. This is not an aspect of this thesis which we will be discussing. Thankfully, there is a stronger (and in my opinion, more natural) condition than Whitney (B) called Smoothly Locally Trivial With Conical Fibres due to Zimhony
[
Zim24
]
, which we will be discussing below. The nice consequence of this condition is that it not only implies the existence of control data like Whitney (B), but this control data will in fact be smooth! See
[
Zim24
]
for more information about smooth control data.

To begin, let’s discuss a weaker version of smoothly locally trivial with conical fibres, namely locally trivial.

Definition 7.69

(Local Triviality)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space. Say
X
is (smoothly) locally trivial if for each stratum
S\in\Sigma
and point
x\in S
, we can find a chart

\phi:U\subseteq X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}

with
\phi(x)=(0,0)
such that

(1)

there exists open neighbourhood
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
of
0
so that

\phi(S\cap U)=V\times\{0\};

(2)

if
R\in\Sigma
satisfies
S\subseteq\overline{R}
, then there exists submanifold
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{M}
so that

\phi(U\cap R)=V\times C.

We will call the singular chart
\phi:U\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}
a
splitting chart
centred at
x
and
S
.

Example 7.70
.

Suppose
(M,\mathscr{F})
is a singular foliation of a manifold so that
\mathscr{F}
is also a stratification. By a result of Stefan
[
Ste74
]
,
\mathscr{F}
satisfies local triviality.

Remark 7.71
.

Shortly, we will be introducing an even stronger condition of local triviality, so I will leave the less trivial examples for later.

Proposition 7.72
.

Suppose
(X,\Sigma)
is a locally trivial differentiable stratified space which admits differentiable partitions of unity. Then the map

X\times\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)\to T\Sigma

is surjective.

Proof.

Fix a stratum
S\in\Sigma
,
x\in S
, and
v\in T_{x}S
. Let
\phi:U\subseteq X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}
be a splitting chart centred on
x
and
S
as in Definition
7.69
. Write
\phi(S\cap U)=\Omega\times\{0\}
for open neighbourhood
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
about
0
.

Since
S
is a smooth manifold, we can find a vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}(S\cap U)
so that
V_{x}=v
. Push
V
forward via
\phi
to a vector field
W\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
. We can extend
W
to a vector field on
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{M}
by

W_{(a,b)}=(W_{a},0)\in T_{(a,b)}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{M}),

for
(a,b)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{M}
. Trivially,
W
is tangent to the images of the strata
\phi(R\cap U)
for
R\in\Sigma
. Hence, we can push
W
forward by
\phi^{-1}
to get a stratified vector field
\widetilde{V}\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
. Using bump functions, we can then extend
\widetilde{V}
to a global stratified vector field with
\widetilde{V}_{x}=v
.
∎

Corollary 7.73
.

Locally trivial differentiable stratified spaces are Whitney (A).

Proof.

An easy consequence of Proposition
7.65
.
∎

30.3
Locally Conical and Quasi-Homegeneity

Local triviality is a powerful property for a stratified space to have as it implies Whitney (A), but it does not necessarily imply Whitney (B) holds. To ensure this stronger condition holds, we need to impose that the local trivializations are homogeneous as well.

Definition 7.74
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a locally trivial differentiable stratified space,
x\in X
,
S\in\Sigma
, and
\phi:U\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}
a splitting chart centred about
x
and
S
. Write
\phi(U\cap S)=V\times\{0\}
for an open neighbourhood
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
of
0
.

Say
\phi:U\to\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}
is quasi-homogeneous of weight
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{M})
if for any stratum
R\in\Sigma
, there exists a submanifold
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\{0\}
which is closed under the
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action

\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{M};\quad(t,(x_{1},\dots,x_{M}))\mapsto(t^{d_{1}}x_{1},\dots,t^{d_{M}}x_{M})

such that

\phi(R\cap U)=V\times C.

If for any
S\in\Sigma
and
x\in S
we can find a quasi-homogeneous chart of weight
\mathbf{d}
for some
\mathbf{d}
, then say
(X,\Sigma)
is quasi-homogeneous. If we can always choose
\mathbf{d}
so that
\mathbf{d}=(1,\dots,1)
, then say
(X,\Sigma)
is locally conical.

Example 7.75
.

Consider the coordinate axes

X=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ xy=0\}

with the stratification

\Sigma=\{\{(0,0)\}\}\cup\pi_{0}(X\setminus\{(0,0)\})

that was discussed in Example
7.3
. The inclusion
\iota:X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
defines a locally conical chart centred on
(0,0)
as the stratum containing
(0,0)
is the singleton
\{(0,0)\}
and any of the other strata, which are open rays emanating from
(0,0)
are all closed under positive scalar multiplication.

To show that the quasi-homogeneous condition from Definition
7.74
is stronger than being Whitney regular, we will first need a Lemma.

Lemma 7.76
.

Let
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{n}
and consider the
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action

\mu:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n};\quad(t,x)\mapsto\mu_{t}(x)=(t^{d_{1}}x_{1},\dots,t^{d_{n}}x_{n}).

(85)

Suppose
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
is an embedded
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-invariant submanifold. Then
C\cap S^{n-1}
is an embedded submanifold.

Proof.

First let us consider the weighted Euler-like vector field
E\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
defined by the following action on functions. If
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, define

E_{x_{0}}f:=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}f\circ\mu_{e^{t}}(x_{0}).

In terms of coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
we have

E=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}x_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}.

In particular, notice that
E=0
only at
\{0\}
. Furthermore, since
C
is closed under the action of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, it follows that
E
is tangent to
C
.

Now consider the map

R:C\to\mathbb{R};\quad c\mapsto\|c\|^{2},

where
\|\cdot\|
is the standard norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. I claim
1
is a regular value of
R
. Indeed, if
c=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\in R^{-1}(1)
then we can show

T_{c}R(E_{c})=2\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}c_{i}^{2}\bigg)\frac{d}{d\lambda}\bigg|_{\lambda=1}.

Observe that since
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\geq 1
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}c_{i}^{2}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}=R(c)=1>0.

In particular,
T_{c}R\neq 0
and hence
1
is a regular value. Therefore,

R^{-1}(1)=C\cap S^{n-1}

is an embedded submanifold.
∎

We are now in the position to show the main result of this section.

Theorem 7.77
.

If
(X,\Sigma)
is quasi-homogeneous in the sense of Definition
7.74
, then
(X,\Sigma)
is Whitney regular.

Proof.

This is an adaption of an argument due to David Miyamoto.

Suppose
(X,\Sigma)
is quasi-homogeneous and fix two strata
S,R\in\Sigma
and
x\in S
. Due to Proposition
7.68
, we may work in a quasi-homogeneous chart with weight
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{M})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{M}
centred at
x
and
S
. That is, we may assume

S=\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\},

and

R=\mathbb{R}^{k}\times C

for some submanifold
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\{0\}
closed under the action

\mu:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{M};\quad y\mapsto\mu_{t}(y)=(t^{d_{1}}y_{1},\dots,t^{d_{M}}y_{M}).

Suppose
\{(x_{n},y_{n})\}\subseteq R
and
\{(x_{n}^{\prime},0)\}\subseteq S
are two sequences such that

(1)

x_{n}\neq x_{n}^{\prime}
for all
n
.

(2)

Both sequences converge to
(0,0)

(3)

The tangent spaces
T_{(x_{n},y_{n})}R
converge to some
\tau\subseteq T_{(0,0)}\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

(4)

The lines
\ell_{n}
connecting
(x_{n},y_{n})
to
(x_{n}^{\prime},0)
converge to some line
\ell\subseteq T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{M}
.

First I claim that there exists
\overline{y}\in S^{M-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{M}
and a subspace
\tau^{\prime}\subseteq T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{M}
so that

\tau=T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus(T_{0}\mathbb{R}\cdot\{\overline{y}\}+\tau^{\prime}).

(86)

To see this, first notice that since

T_{(x_{n},y_{n})}R=T_{x_{n}}\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus T_{y_{n}}C

we must have

\tau=T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus\tau^{\prime\prime},

where
\tau^{\prime\prime}\subseteq T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{M}
. Now observe that since none of the
y_{n}
’s are zero and
C
is closed under the action of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, a quick continuity argument shows there exists
t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for all
n
so that

\mu_{t_{n}}(y_{n})\in C\cap S^{M-1}

for all
n
. Since
S^{M-1}
is compact, there exists a subsequence of
\{\mu_{t_{n}}(y_{n})\}
which converges to some
\overline{y}\in S^{M-1}
. Furthermore, using the compactness of Grassmannians and Lemma
7.76
, we may assume that the sequence of subspaces
T_{\mu_{t_{n}}(y_{n})}C\cap S^{M-1}
converges to some
\tau^{\prime}
. In now claim that

\tau^{\prime\prime}=T_{0}(\mathbb{R}\cdot\overline{y})+\tau^{\prime}.

(87)

Indeed,
\tau^{\prime}
is tangent to
S^{M-1}
while the line
\mathbb{R}\cdot\overline{y}
is perpendicular. Hence,
T_{0}(\mathbb{R}\cdot y)
and
\tau^{\prime}
intersect trivially. Hence, the dimensions of both sides of Equation (
87
) are equal to the dimension of
S
. Furthermore, clearly both
T_{0}(\mathbb{R}\cdot\overline{y}),\tau^{\prime}\subseteq\tau^{\prime\prime}
. Thus, equality follows.

Two consequences:

(1)

T_{(0,0)}S\subseteq\tau
.

Indeed, this follows from the fact that
T_{(0,0)}S=T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus\{0\}
and
\tau=T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus\tau^{\prime\prime}
.

(2)

\ell\subseteq\tau
.

Observe that each line
\ell_{n}
has direction vector

(x_{n},y_{n})-(x_{n}^{\prime},0)=(x_{n}-x_{n}^{\prime},y_{n}).

Clearly
x_{n}-x_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
still and each
y_{n}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}\cdot y_{n}/\|y_{n}\|
which converges to
T_{0}\mathbb{R}\cdot\overline{y}
. Hence, they all converge.

∎

Every stratified space we’ve considered thus far is quasi-homogeneous.

Theorem 7.78

(
[
MR25
]
)
.

Let
(M,g,\mathscr{F})
be an SRF and
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
the dimension-type stratification. Then
(M,\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F}))
is locally conical.

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
be a dimension stratum and
x\in S
. Let

\phi:U\subseteq M\to V\subseteq T_{x}S\oplus\nu_{x}(M,S)

be a local normal form neighbourhood centred on
x
. Then,

\phi(U\cap S)=V\cap(T_{x}S\oplus\{0\}).

Now suppose that
R\in\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
is another dimension-type stratum with
S\subseteq\overline{R}
. If
\dim(R)=\dim(S)+d
, then

\phi(U\cap R)=V\cap(T_{x}S\oplus\Sigma_{d}),

where
\Sigma_{d}\subseteq\nu_{x}(M,S)
is the dimension-type stratum containing leaves of dimension
d
in
\nu_{x}(M,S)
. By the Homothety Lemma (Lemma
7.32
),
\Sigma_{d}
is a conical submanifold. Therefore
\phi:U\to V
is a conical neighbourhood.
∎

Corollary 7.79
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
M
a proper
G
-space. Then the infinitesimal stratification
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M)
is locally conical.

Proof.

Just apply Corollary
7.53
.
∎

Theorem 7.80

(
[
Zim24
]
)
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
M
a proper
G
-space. Write
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
for the orbit-type stratification of
M
. Then
(M,\mathcal{S}_{G}(M))
is locally conical.

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
be an orbit-type stratum,
x\in S
, and
U\subseteq M
a maximal slice chart about
x
, i.e. a
G
-equivariant diffeomorphism

\phi:U\to G\times_{G_{x}}\nu_{x}(M,G).

mapping
x
to
[e,0]
. For convenience, write
K=G_{x}
and
V=\nu_{x}(M,G)
. Choosing a
K
-invariant metric on
V
, let
V^{\prime}:=(V^{K})^{\perp}
be the orthogonal complement to the fixed point set
V^{K}
. Then, there is a canonical
G
-equivariant diffeomorphism

G\times_{K}V=(G\times_{K}V^{\prime})\times V^{K}.

Letting
A_{1}=(G/K)\times V^{K}
and
A_{2}=G\times_{K}V^{\prime})
we see that we have a diffeomorphism

\phi:U\to A_{1}\oplus A_{2}

from
U
to a sum of two vector bundles over
G/K
. Observe that

\phi(U\cap S)=G\times_{K}V^{K}=(G\times_{K}V^{K})\oplus(G\times_{K}\{0\})=A_{1}\oplus\{0\}

Next, if
R>S
and say
R\in\pi_{0}(M_{(H)})
for some compact subgroup
H\leq K
, then first

\phi(U\cap M_{(H)})=(G\times_{K}V^{\prime}_{(H)})\times V^{K}

and so there exists a connected component
R^{\prime}\in\pi_{0}(V^{\prime}_{(H)})
so that

\phi(U\cap R)=(G\times_{K}R^{\prime})\times V^{K}=A_{1}\oplus C,

where
C=G\times_{K}R^{\prime}
.
C
is clearly a submanifold of
A_{2}
and since the action of
K
on
V
is linear, the orbit-type strata are all conical.
∎

Theorem 7.81

(
[
Zim24
]
)
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
M
a proper
G
-space, and let
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
denote the canonical stratification of
M/G
. Then
(M/G,\mathcal{S}_{G}(M/G))
is quasi-homogeneous.

Proof.

Suffices to assume
M=G\times_{K}V
where
K\leq G
a compact subgroup and
V
is a finite-dimensional linear
K
-representation. Choose a
K
-invariant metric on
V
and let
F=(V^{K})^{\perp}
. Observe that

G\times_{K}V=(G\times_{K}F)\times V^{K}.

Thus,

(G\times_{K}V)/G\cong V^{K}\times(F/K)

Now, as is shown by Duistermaat
[
Dui
, pg. 16]
, we can find a generating set
q_{1},\dots,q_{M}\in\mathbb{R}[F]^{K}
so that

(1)

each of the
q_{i}
are homogeneous

(2)

If
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are coordinates on
V^{K}
, then

x_{1},\dots,x_{n},q_{1},\dots,q_{M}

is a basis for
\mathbb{R}[V^{K}\times F]^{K}
.

Thus, we get a Hilbert map (see Definition
6.31
)

p:V^{K}\times F\to V\times\mathbb{R}^{M};\quad(v,w)\mapsto(v,q_{1}(w),\dots,q_{M}(w))

which induces an embedding

p:V^{k}\times F/K\to V^{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{M}

For each
i=1,\dots,M
, let
d_{i}=\deg(q_{i})
then and write

\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{M})
. Letting
M=V^{K}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}
, we can define a monoid action
\mu:\mathbb{R}\times M\to M
by

\mu_{t}(v,y_{1},\dots,y_{M})=(v,t^{d_{1}}y_{1},\dots,t^{d_{M}}y_{M}).

I now claim that
p:V^{K}\times F/K\hookrightarrow V^{K}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}
is a quasi-homogeneous chart about the stratum
V^{K}\times\{0\}
with weight
\mathbf{d}
. We first observe that

p(V^{K}\times\{0\})=V^{K}\times\{0\}

which is the first condition from Definition
7.74
. For the second, first let
R\subseteq V^{K}\times F/K
be any other stratum. Then
R
is the connected component of the orbit-type
V^{K}\times F_{(H)}/K
for some compact subgroup
K\leq H
.
F_{(H)}
is closed under scalar multiplication by
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, so it immediately follows that so is
R
. Hence, since
p
is made up of homogeneous polynomials, we have
p(R)
is quasi-homogeneous.
∎

†
†
margin:

8

Stratified Pseudobundles

Pseudobundles are a natural generalization of smooth vector bundles to the subcartesian setting. A pseudobundle
E
over a subcartesian space
X
can be thought of as almost a vector bundle, except the rank of the fibres is allowed to vary. Their utility for our later discussion on singular quantization is that they provide a convenient language for discussing the many “singular vector bundles” we will need to consider, like stratified tangent bundles and stratified polarizations.

The study of pseudobundles was first initiated by Marshall
[
Mar75
]
and, as is characteristic for older subcartesian literature, are defined in terms of atlases of local trivializations. I followed this approach in my paper
[
Ros24
]
where I defined “stratified vector bundles”. I have subsequently realized that this terminology of pseudobundles exists and so we will using this term instead. I also have given a much more general definition of a pseudobundle in terms of the differentiable functions in analogy with definining a subcartesian structure in terms of its underlying differential structure.

31
Pseudobundles

Definition 8.1

(Pseudobundle)
.

Let
X
be a subcartesian space and
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. A
\mathbb{K}
-pseudobundle over
X
consists of the following data

(i)

a subcartesian space
E
and a surjective differentiable map
\pi:E\to X
; and

(ii)

a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space structure equipped to each fibre
E_{x}=\pi^{-1}(x)
,
x\in X
;

subject to the following conditions.

(1)

Writing

E\times_{\pi}E:=\{(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\times E\ |\ \pi(e_{1})=\pi(e_{2})\}

and equipping
E\times_{\pi}E
with the subset subcartesian structure inherited from
E\times E
, then the induced global scalar multiplication and addition maps

\displaystyle\mu:\mathbb{K}\times E\to E;\quad

\displaystyle(t,e)\mapsto\mu_{t}(e)=t\cdot e

\displaystyle+:E\times_{\pi}E\to E;

\displaystyle\quad(e_{1},e_{2})\mapsto e_{1}+e_{2}

are differentiable.

(ii)

The map
\pi:E\to M
is a topological quotient map.

(iii)

The subcartesian structure
C^{\infty}(E)
is generated by the pullbacks of functions on
X
,
\pi^{*}C^{\infty}(X)
, and the real linear functions
C_{(1)}^{\infty}(E)
, where

C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E):=\{f\in C^{\infty}(E)\ |\ f(t\cdot e)=t\cdot f(e)\text{ for all }(t,e)\in\mathbb{K}\times E\}.

That is, for any function
f\in C^{\infty}(E)
and any point
e\in E
, there exists an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq E
of
e
, functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\in C^{\infty}(X)
, linear functions
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\in C^{\infty}(E)_{(1)}(E)
, and a smooth function
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})
so that

f|_{U}=F(\pi^{*}g_{1},\dots,\pi^{*}g_{n},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m})

We will refer to
\mathbb{R}
-pseudobundles simply as pseudobundles and
\mathbb{C}
-pseudobundles as complex pseudobundles.

Pseudobundles are a straightforward generalization of vector bundles. The last condition that the smooth structure of a vector bundle is generated by pullbacks of smooth functions on the base and linear functions amounts to passing to trivializing neighbourhoods and using the same techniques as in the proof of Lemma
6.54
. For more general pseudobundles, let us now turn to Zariski tangent bundles.

Example 8.2
.

Let
X
be a subcartesian space. By Theorem
6.55
, the Zariski tangent bundle
\pi:TX\to X
satisfies most of the axioms of a pseudobundle, except by definition the subcartesian structure on
TX
is defined to be

C^{\infty}(TX)=\langle\pi^{*}C^{\infty}(X)\cup dC^{\infty}(X)\rangle

where
dC^{\infty}(X)
is the set of functions of the form

df:TX\to\mathbb{R};\quad v\mapsto v(f)

for
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
. Clearly
dC^{\infty}(X)\subseteq C^{\infty}_{(1)}(TX)\subseteq C^{\infty}(TX)
and hence

C^{\infty}(TX)=\langle\pi^{*}C^{\infty}(X)\cup dC^{\infty}(X)\rangle\subseteq\langle\pi^{*}C^{\infty}(X)\cup C^{\infty}_{(1)}(TX)\rangle\subseteq C^{\infty}(TX).

Thus,
\pi:TX\to X
defines a pseudobundle.

Example 8.3
.

Thanks to the previous example, we can now use Zariski tangent bundles to show that pseudobundles need not be vector bundles. Indeed, consider the union of the coordinate axes in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

X=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ xy=0\}.

As we saw in Example
6.40
,

\dim(T_{(x,y)}X)=\begin{cases}1,\quad&(x,y)\neq(0,0)\\
2,\quad&(x,y)=(0,0)\end{cases}

Many of the other “singular vector bundles” we’ve considered so far have been subsets of either vector bundles or Zariski tangent bundles. And so, to see that they are all indeed pseudobundles, we need the following definition.

Definition 8.4

(Subbundles)
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a pseudobundle. A subbundle if a pair of subsets
A\subseteq E
and
Y\subseteq X
such that
\pi(A)=Y
and so that the restriction
\pi|_{A}:A\to Y
is a pseudobundle where
A
and
Y
are equipped with the subset subcartesian structures.

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a pseudobundle and
A\subseteq E
a subset with
Y=\pi(A)
. If
A
is closed under scalar multiplication and fibre-wise addition, then
\pi|_{A}:A\to Y
is a subbundle.

Proof.

Fix a subset
A\subseteq E
which is closed under scalar multiplication and fibre-wise addition. Endowing
A
and its image
Y=\pi(A)\subseteq X
with the subspace subcartesian structures, we see that
\pi|_{A}:A\to Y
already satisifies most of the conditions of a subbundle, except we need to show

C^{\infty}(A)=\langle(\pi|_{A})^{*}C^{\infty}(Y)\cup C^{\infty}_{(1)}(A)\rangle.

Clearly,
\langle(\pi|_{A})^{*}C^{\infty}(Y)\cup C^{\infty}_{(1)}(A)\rangle\subseteq C^{\infty}(A)
, so we only need to show the reverse containment. To do this, fix
f\in C^{\infty}(A)
and
a\in A
. Then in a neighbourhood
U\subseteq E
of
a
, we can find
g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\in C^{\infty}(X)
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E)
, and a function
G\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
so that

f|_{U\cap A}=G(\pi^{*}g_{1},\dots,\pi^{*}g_{k},h_{1},\dots,h_{m})|_{U\cap A}

By possibly shrinking
U
, we may assume that
\pi(U)\subseteq X
is an open subset. In which case, we observe for all
i=1,\dots,k
and
j=1,\dots,m
,

\pi^{*}g_{i}|_{A}=(\pi|_{A})^{*}(g_{i}|_{Y})\in(\pi|_{A})^{*}C^{\infty}(Y)

and

h_{j}|_{A}\in C^{\infty}_{(1)}(A)

In particular,
f\in\langle(\pi|_{A})^{*}C^{\infty}(Y)\cup C^{\infty}_{(1)}(A)\rangle
.
∎

Example 8.6
.

Given any singular foliation
\mathscr{F}
of a manifold
M
, the tangent bundle to the foliation
T\mathscr{F}\subseteq TM
is endowed with the structure of a pseudobundle via the subspace differential structure and the restrictions of the vector bundle operations on
TM
to
T\mathscr{F}
.

Example 8.7
.

In a similar vein to Example
8.6
, given a differentiable stratified space
(X,\Sigma)
, the stratified tangent bundle
T\Sigma\subseteq TX
inherits the structure of a pseudobundle from the Zariski tangent bundle
TX
.

Definition 8.8

(Pseudobundle Morphism)
.

Let
\pi_{1}:E^{1}\to X_{1}
and
\pi_{2}:E^{2}\to X_{2}
be two pseudobundles. A morphism is a pair of differentiable maps
f:X_{1}\to X_{2}
and
\phi:E^{1}\to E^{2}
such that

(1)

the diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(2)

For all
x_{1}\in X_{1}
, the induced map

\phi_{x}=\phi|_{E^{1}_{x}}:E^{1}_{x}\to E^{2}_{f(x_{1})}

is linear.

Example 8.9
.

In this language, another way of interpreting Corollary
7.28
is that a smooth map
F:M_{1}\to M_{2}
between two foliated spaces
(M_{1},\mathscr{F}_{1})
and
(M_{2},\mathscr{F}_{2})
is foliate
\iff
the derivative
TF:TM_{1}\to TM_{2}
restricts to a pseudobundle morphism
TF:T\mathscr{F}_{1}\to T\mathscr{F}_{2}
.

Definition 8.10

(Restriction of pseudobundles)
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a pseudobundle and
Y\subseteq X
a subset. Define the restricted pseudobundle
\pi_{Y}:E|_{Y}\to Y
by

E|_{Y}:=\pi^{-1}(Y)\quad\quad\pi_{Y}:=\pi|_{E|_{Y}}.

As one may easily verify, restricting a pseudobundle to a subset returns a pseudobundle once again.

Definition 8.11

(Subtrivial pseudobundle)
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a pseudobundle. Say
\pi:E\to X
is locally subtrivial if for any
x\in X
, we can find a neighbourhood
U\subseteq X
of
x
and a morphism of pseudobundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E|_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\times\mathbb{C}^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}}

for some
N
such that
\phi:E_{U}\to\phi(E_{U})
is an isomorphism of pseudobundles over
U
. Call the pair
(U,\phi)
a
local trivialization
. If around any point
x\in X
we can find local trivializations so that
\phi(E|_{U})=U\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then call
\pi:E\to X
a
differentiable vector bundle
over
X
.

Example 8.12
.

Recall that for a subcartesian space
X
, any local chart
\phi:U\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
differentiates to a local chart
T\phi:TU\hookrightarrow T\mathbb{R}^{N}=\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
. By construction these charts for
TX
define a subtrivial structure on
TX
.

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a subtrivial pseudobundle and
\pi|_{A}:A\to Y
a subbundle. Then
\pi|_{A}:A\to Y
is also subtrivial.

Proof.

Let
y\in Y
and let
(\phi,U)
be a local trivialization of
\pi:E\to X
about
y
. Then clearly the composition

A|_{U\cap Y}\hookrightarrow E|_{U}\hookrightarrow U\times\mathbb{R}^{N}

defines a local trivialization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A|_{U\cap Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U\cap Y)\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\cap Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi|_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}}

∎

Corollary 8.14
.

All pseudobundles in Examples
8.2
,
8.6
, and
8.7
are subtrivial.

As a final elementary definition, we define sections of pseudobundles.

Definition 8.15

(Sections of Pseudobundle)
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a pseudobundle. A
section
is a differentiable map
\sigma:E\to X
such that
\pi\circ\sigma=\text{id }_{X}
. Write
\Gamma(E)
for the space of all sections.

Example 8.16
.

Let
X
be a subcartesian space, then by Proposition
6.56
the set of Zariski vector fields
\mathfrak{X}(X)
is equal to the sections
\Gamma(TX)
of the Zariski tangent bundle.

Note that due to Example
6.63
, we see that in general, given a pseudobundle
\pi:E\to X
, the map

X\times\Gamma(E)\to E;\quad(x,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(x)

need not be surjective. For this reason, we give one final definition.

Definition 8.17

(Enough Sections)
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a pseudobundle. We say
\pi:E\to X

has enough sections
if the map

X\times\Gamma(E)\to E;\quad(x,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(x)

is surjective.

Example 8.18
.

Clearly differentiable vector bundles and tangent bundles to singular foliations have enough sections. Similarly, if
(X,\Sigma)
is a locally trivial stratified space, then by Proposition
7.72
,
T\Sigma\to X
has enough sections as well.

32
Quotients of Equivariant Vector Bundles

Now we have the setup to return back to our discussion on equivariant vector bundles from Section
5
. For all that follows, let
G
be a connected Lie group and
\pi:E\to M
a proper equivariant vector bundle. In Definition
2.34
, we defined a distinguished pseudobundle inside
E
given by

\widetilde{E}:=\bigcup_{x\in M}E_{x}^{G_{x}}

As we saw in Theorem
2.35
, if
M
has only one orbit-type, then
\widetilde{E}
is a
G
-equivariant vector bundle over
M
. In general,
\widetilde{E}
will only be a pseudobundle.

Example 8.19
.

Indeed, consider the
S^{1}
-equivariant vector bundle

pr_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2},

where each copy of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
carries with it the natural
S^{1}
action by rotations about the origin. Since the origin
0
is a fixed point of the
S^{1}
action, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\{x\}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{S^{1}_{x}}

\displaystyle=(\{0\}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{S^{1}})\bigsqcup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\{x\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\{0\}\times\{0\})\sqcup((\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}^{2})

Thus, the rank of
\widetilde{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}
is
0
over the origin and
2
elsewhere. Hence, the restriction

\widetilde{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

is not a vector bundle.

Nonetheless, we saw in Proposition
2.39
that
\widetilde{E}
is spanned by the equivariant sections. That is,

M\times\Gamma(E)^{G}\to\widetilde{E};\quad(x,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(x)

is surjective and hence
\widetilde{E}
has enough sections. Furthermore, quotient
\widetilde{E}/G\to M/G
is a vector bundle and there is a canonical surjection

\Gamma(E)^{G}\to\Gamma(\widetilde{E}/G).

We would now like to generalize these results to the singular case. For that, we need the following definition.

Definition 8.20

(Equivariantly Generated)
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
\pi:E\to M
a proper equivariant vector bundle, and
A\subseteq M
a subset closed under the
G
-action. Say
E
is equivariantly generated over
A
, if the map

A\times\Gamma(E|_{A})^{G}\to E|_{A};\quad(a,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(a)

is surjective.

Suppose now that
A\subseteq M
is a sliceable subset in the sense of Definition
6.35
. Writing
\rho:M\to M/G
for the quotient map, recall from Proposition
6.36
that

C^{\infty}(A/G)=\{f:A/G\to\mathbb{R}\ |\ (\rho|_{A})^{*}f\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}|_{A}\}

is a subcartesian structure on
A/G
. With this, we can now prove the following.

Theorem 8.21
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
\pi:E\to M
a proper equivariant vector bundle, and
A\subseteq M
a sliceable subset. Suppose
E
is equivariantly generated over
A
. Then the following holds.

(1)

The induced map
\overline{\pi}:(E|_{A})/G\to A/G
is a differentiable vector bundle over
A/G
.

(2)

Write
\rho_{M}:M\to M/G
and
\rho_{E}:E\to E/G
for the quotient maps. Then there is an induced linear isomorphism

(\rho_{E})_{*}:\Gamma(E|_{A})^{G}\to\Gamma((E|_{A})/G).

Proof.

(1)

First observe that since
A
is closed and sliceable, so is
E|_{A}
. Indeed, around any point
a\in A
we can find a neighbourhood of
a
so that
\pi:E\to M
is locally
G
-equivariantly isomorphic to the vector bundle

\pi:G\times_{K}(V\times W)\to G\times_{K}V;\quad[g,(v,w)]\mapsto[g,v],

where
K\leq G
is a compact subgroup and
V,W
are linear
K
-representations and so that
A
is locally diffeomorphic to
G\times_{K}S
, where
S\subseteq V
is a closed
G
-invariant subspace. Then, under this local isomorphism,
E|_{A}
becomes identified with

E|_{A}\cong G\times_{K}(S\times W)

(88)

and hence
E|_{A}
is sliceable.

Now, let
A_{0}:=A/G
and
E_{0}:=(E|_{A})/G
. Since both
A
and
E|_{A}
are sliceable, we get canonical subcartesian structures
C^{\infty}(A_{0})
and
C^{\infty}(E_{0})
given by

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(A_{0})

\displaystyle=\{f:A_{0}\to\mathbb{R}\ |\ (\rho_{M}|_{A})^{*}f\in C^{\infty}(M)|_{A}^{G}\}

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(E_{0})

\displaystyle=\{f:E_{0}\to\mathbb{R}\ |\ (\rho_{E}|_{E|_{A}})^{*}f\in C^{\infty}(E)|_{E|_{A}}^{G}\}

Clearly the induced map
\overline{\pi}:E_{0}\to A_{0}
is differentiable and a topological projection. Now to endow the fibres of
\overline{\pi}:E_{0}\to A_{0}
with vector space structures. Fix
a\in A
, and consider the map

E_{a}\to(E_{0})_{[a]};\quad e\mapsto\rho_{E}(e).

(89)

I claim
E_{a}
is a bijection. Clearly the map in Equation (
89
) is surjective so we only have to show injective. Suppose now that
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{a}
so that
\rho_{E}(e)=\rho_{E}(e^{\prime})
. By definition, this implies there exists
g\in G
so that
g\cdot e=e^{\prime}
. By equivariance of the map
\pi:E\to M
, we obtain
g\cdot a=a
and hence
g\in G_{a}
. Since
E
is equivariantly generated over
A
, we have by Theorem
2.35
that
E_{a}^{G_{a}}=E_{a}
. Thus,
g\cdot e=e
and hence
e=e^{\prime}
.

Thus, let us endow
(E_{0})_{[a]}
with the vector spaces structure from
E_{a}
. Making use of the normal form in Equation (
88
), we see that around every point
a\in A
, we have a
G
-equivariant isomorphism of differentiable vector bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E|_{U\cap A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times_{K}(S\times W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\cap A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times_{K}S}

As we already saw, since
E
is equivariantly generated over
A
,
E_{a}^{G_{a}}=E_{a}
for all
a\in A
. Thus, since
W
corresponds to the fibre over
[e,0]
we must have
W^{K}=W
and thus
G\times_{K}(S\times W)=(G\times_{K}S)\times W
. Hence, we obtain diffeomorphisms of subcartesian spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}|_{U\cap A/G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G/K)\times W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U\cap A)/G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/K}

This at once proves that the scalar multiplication and addition on
E_{0}
are differentiable, as well as providing charts showing that
E_{0}
is a vector bundle.

(2)

The proof from here is pretty well identical to the proof of Proposition
2.39
.

∎

33
Stratified pseudobundles

Given that many of the singular spaces that have been of interest to us have come equipped with natural stratifications, it is only natural to ask when is the pseudobundle compatible with this stratification. This is where the idea of a stratified pseudobundle arises.

Definition 8.22

(Stratified Pseudobundle)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space and
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. A stratified
\mathbb{K}
-pseudobundle over
(X,\Sigma)
is a
\mathbb{K}
-pseudobundle
\pi:E\to X
such that the induced partition

\pi^{-1}\Sigma=\{\pi^{-1}(S)\ |\ S\in\Sigma\}

satisfies the following.

(1)

(E,\pi^{-1}\Sigma,C^{\infty}(E))
is a weakly differentiable stratified space in the sense of Definition
7.10
.

(2)

For each
S\in\Sigma
, the map

\pi_{S}:=\pi|_{\pi^{-1}(S)}:\pi^{-1}(S)\to S

is a smooth
\mathbb{K}
-vector bundle.

Example 8.23
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space and
\pi:E\to X
a differentiable vector bundle of rank
k
. Then for each stratum
S\in\Sigma
, the restriction
E|_{S}\to S
is clearly a smooth vector bundle as locally
E|_{S}
is diffeomorphic to
U\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
which provides smooth charts. This also shows that
\pi^{-1}\Sigma
is a stratification as around any point
x\in X
, we have a trivialization
E|_{U}\cong U\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
. In this trivialization, the pieces of
\pi^{-1}\Sigma
have the form
\{S\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\}
which satisfies the axiom of the frontier.

Example 8.24
.

Let
X
be the union of coordinate axes in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
i.e.

X=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ xy=0\}.

Endow
X
with the stratification

\Sigma=\{\{(0,0)\},H_{+},H_{-},V_{+},V_{-}\}

from Example
7.3

H_{-}
H_{+}
V_{-}
V_{+}
\{(0,0)\}

Explicitly,

\displaystyle H_{+}

\displaystyle=(0,\infty)\times\{0\}

\displaystyle H_{-}

\displaystyle=(-\infty,0)\times\{0\}

\displaystyle V_{+}

\displaystyle=\{0\}\times(0,\infty)

\displaystyle V_{-}

\displaystyle=\{0\}\times(-\infty,0)

Then the stratified tangent bundle
T\Sigma
has a natural partition into smooth vector bundles

T\Sigma=T\{(0,0)\}\sqcup TH_{+}\sqcup TH_{-}\sqcup TV_{+}\sqcup TV_{-}.

It’s easy to see that
T\Sigma
satisfies the axioms of a being a stratified pseudobundle except, perhaps, the frontier condition. For that observe that as subsets of
T\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\overline{T\{(0,0)\}}

\displaystyle=\{(0,0,0,0)\}

\displaystyle\overline{TH_{+}}

\displaystyle=([0,\infty)\times\{0\})\times(\mathbb{R}\times\{0\})

\displaystyle\overline{TH_{-}}

\displaystyle=((-\infty,0]\times\{0\})\times(\mathbb{R}\times\{0\})

\displaystyle\overline{TV_{+}}

\displaystyle=(\{0\}\times[0,\infty))\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\overline{TV_{-}}

\displaystyle=(\{0\}\times(-\infty,0])\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})

And so, we can manually check that the partition
\pi^{-1}\Sigma
satisfies the frontier condition as the only stratum that intersects the closure of any other stratum in
\{(0,0,0,0)\}
and clearly it is contained in the closures of the other strata.

More generally, suppose
(X,\Sigma)
is a differentiable stratified space. Due to Example
8.7
, the stratified tangent bundle
\pi:T\Sigma\to X
is a pseudobundle. Furthermore, the partition

\pi^{-1}\Sigma=\{TS\ |\ S\in\Sigma\}

is clearly a partition of
T\Sigma
into smooth vector bundles over each of the strata of
X
. So the only possible way
T\Sigma
could fail to be a stratified pseudobundle is if the partition
\pi^{-1}\Sigma
failed to be a stratification. In this case, it’s straightforward to see that on that front the only condition that could fail would be the frontier condition.

Theorem 8.25

(
[
Pfl01
]
)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space and let
\pi:T\Sigma\to X
be the stratified tangent bundle. Then the partition

\pi^{-1}\Sigma=\{TS\ |\ S\in\Sigma\}

makes
(T\Sigma,\pi^{-1}\Sigma,C^{\infty}(T\Sigma))
into a weakly differentiable stratified space
\iff

(X,\Sigma)
is Whitney A.

As we discussed in Subsection
30.1
, examples of non Whitney A stratified spaces are rather pathological and will not make an appearance in this thesis. Thus, all stratified tangent bundles henceforth will be stratified spaces. Thus, the following result will hold.

Corollary 8.26
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space. Then
\pi:T\Sigma\to(X,\Sigma)
is a stratified pseudobundle
\iff

(X,\Sigma)
is Whitney (A).

Whitney condition (A) can very naturally be extended to more general stratified pseudobundles.

Definition 8.27

(Linear Whitney A)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space and
\pi:E\to X
a pseduobundle such that for all
S\in\Sigma
, the restriction
\pi_{S}:E|_{S}\to S
is a smooth vector bundle. Say
\pi:E\to X
is
Linear Whitney A
if for any two strata
S,R\in\Sigma
,
x\in S
, and local trivialization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E|_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\times\mathbb{R}^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}}

the following condition holds. Write
r
for the rank of
E|_{R}
and
s
for the rank of
E|_{S}
.

(A)

If
\{x_{k}\}\subseteq R
is a sequence such that
\{\phi(E_{x_{k}})\}
converges to
\tau\subseteq\{x\}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
in
\text{Gr}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, then
E_{x}\subseteq\tau
.

This definition is a straight replacement of the stratified tangent bundle with a more general pseudobundle. Thus, it is no surprise that a differentiable stratified space is Whitney (A)
\iff
its stratified tangent bundle is Linear Whitney (A). Also as should be expected, Pflaum’s Theorem can be easily ported to this more general context.

Proposition 8.28
.

Let
\pi:E\to(X,\Sigma)
be a Linear Whitney A pseudobundle over
(X,\Sigma)
. Then
\pi:E\to(X,\Sigma)
is a stratified pseudobundle.

Proof.

The only thing that could possibly go wrong here is that the induced partition
(E,\pi^{-1}(\Sigma))
from Definition
8.22
is not a stratification. On that front, it’s easy to see that the only failure could be the frontier condition. And so to show that
\pi^{-1}\Sigma
satisifies the frontier condition, let
S,R\in\Sigma
be two strata with
E|_{S}\cap\overline{E|_{R}}\neq\emptyset
. We show
E|_{S}\subseteq\overline{E|_{R}}
. To do so, fix
e_{0}\in E|_{S}
.

By continuity of the bundle map
\pi:E\to X
, we obtain that
S\cap\overline{R}\neq\emptyset
and hence
S\subseteq\overline{R}
. Thus, if we write
x_{0}=\pi(e_{0})
, then there exists a sequence
\{x_{k}\}\subseteq\overline{R}
such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}x_{k}=x_{0}.

Passing now to a local trivialization, centred on
x

\phi:E|_{U}\to U\times\mathbb{R}^{N}

consider the sequence
\{\phi(E_{x_{k}})\}\subseteq\text{Gr}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
where
r
is the rank of the smooth vector bundle
E|_{R}\to R
. As we saw in the discussion at the beginning of Section
30.1
,
\text{Gr}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is compact and hence after passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\{\phi(E_{x_{k}})\}
converges to some
\tau\in\text{Gr}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. By Whitney (A), we have
E_{x_{0}}\subseteq\tau
and thus
e_{0}\in\tau
.

Recall that the topology on
\text{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is the subspace topology from
\text{End}(V)
. Using the canonical inner-product on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, the topology on
\text{End}(V)
is generated by the operator norm

\|T\|:=\text{inf}_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|T(v)\|}{\|v\|}.

In particular,

\lim_{k}E_{x_{k}}=\tau

if and only if

\lim_{k}\|P_{E_{x_{k}}}-P_{\tau}\|=0

(90)

where for any subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we write
P_{W}
for the orthogonal projection onto
W
. In particular, observe that
\{P_{E_{x_{k}}}(e_{0})\}\subseteq E|_{R}
is a sequence on
E|_{R}
and by Equation (
90
) we have

0=\lim_{k}\|P_{E_{x_{k}}}(e_{0})-P_{\tau}(e_{0})\|=\lim_{k}\|P_{E_{x_{k}}}(e_{0})-e_{0}\|

since
e\in\tau
. Thus,
\{P_{E_{x_{k}}}(e_{0})\}
converges to
e_{0}
and thus
e_{0}\in\overline{E|_{S}}
. Therefore,
\pi^{-1}\Sigma
satisfies the frontier condition.
∎

As we discussed in Subsection
30.1
, the Whitney conditions may seem somewhat unnatural for a smooth geometer. However, thankfully, the same foliation-theoretic upgrade that we discussed for the usual Whitney A still holds for Linear Whitney A.

Theorem 8.29
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a differentiable stratified space and
\pi:E\to X
a pseudobundle. Suppose the following two conditions hold.

(1)

For each stratum
S\in\Sigma
, the restriction
\pi_{S}:E|_{S}\to S
is a smooth vector bundle.

(2)

\pi:E\to X
has enough sections, i.e the map

X\times\Gamma(E)\to E;\quad(x,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(x)

is surjective.

Then
\pi:E\to X
is Linear Whitney A.

Proof.

Near identical proof to Proposition
7.65
.
∎

Example 8.30
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
\pi:E\to M
a proper equivariant vector bundle. Recall that

\widetilde{E}=\bigcup_{x\in M}E_{x}^{G_{x}}

has enough sections. Thus, if we show
\widetilde{E}|_{S}\to S
is a smooth vector bundle for all orbit-type strata
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
, then we will have shown that
\widetilde{E}\to(M,\mathcal{S}_{G}(M))
is a Whitney (A) stratified pseudobundle. Indeed fixing
x\in M
, we may pass to the local normal form of proper vector bundles and assume that
E=G\times_{K}(V\times W)
,
M=G\times_{K}V
, and
\pi:E\to M
is the map
\pi([g,v,w])=[g,v]
for compact
K\leq G
and linear
K
-representations
V
and
W
. In this case, the stratum
S
containing
x
has the local form

S=G\times_{K}V^{K}\cong(G/K)\times V^{K}.

Note that since stabilizers of points in
S
are conjugate to
K
, it follows that

\widetilde{E}|_{S}=G\times_{K}(V^{K}\times W^{K})=(G/K)\times V^{K}\times W^{K}.

This provides a local trivialization for
\widetilde{E}|_{S}
and hence
\widetilde{E}|_{S}\to S
is a smooth vector bundle.

Example 8.31
.

Continuing on with the last example, let
G
be a connected Lie group,
K\leq G
a compact subgroup, and
V
a
K
-representation. Write
M=G\times_{K}V
. As we saw in Example
2.30
,

TM=G\times_{K}(V\times(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})\times V).

Now observe that if we let
S=G\times_{K}V^{K}
, then on one hand by Proposition
2.21
,

\widetilde{TM}|_{S}=G\times_{K}(V^{K}\times(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})^{K}\times V^{K})\cong(G/K)\times V^{K}\times(\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)/\mathfrak{k})\times V^{K}

while

TS\cong(G/K)\times V^{K}\times(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})\times V^{K}.

Thus,
\widetilde{TM}|_{S}\subseteq TS
. From here, we easily deduce that for any proper
G
-space
M
that
\widetilde{TM}\subseteq T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
, where
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
is the orbit-type stratification, with equality if and only if all the subgroups appearing as stabilizers are normal. In particular, we have shown the following.

Corollary 8.32
.

Let
G
be a connected abelian Lie group and
M
a proper
G
-space. Then
\widetilde{TM}=T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
.

We have already shown that many of the pseudobundles we’ve encountered so far have enough sections. So, provided the pseudobundle is compatible with a stratification on the base space, the result will be a stratified pseudobundle.

Corollary 8.33
.

Suppose
(M,\Sigma)
is a stratified space and
\mathscr{F}
is a singular foliation on
M
. For each
x\in M
, write
L_{x}\in\mathscr{F}
for the leaf through
x
. Further, suppose each stratum
S\in\Sigma
satisfies the following two conditions

(1)

S=\bigcup_{x\in S}L_{x}

(2)

The induced foliation
\mathscr{F}_{S}
on
S
is regular.

Then the tangent bundle
T\mathscr{F}\to M
is a stratified pseudobundle over
(M,\Sigma)
.

This opens the door to many more examples of stratified pseudobundles.

Example 8.34
.

Let
(M,g,\mathscr{F})
be a singular Riemannian foliation. Then the dimension-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{dim}(M,\mathscr{F})
satisfies the conditions of Corollary
8.33
, and hence gives a stratified pseudobundle.

Example 8.35
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
M
a proper
G
-space. Then the orbit foliation
\mathscr{F}_{G}=\{G\cdot x\ |\ x\in M\}
saturates the orbit-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
. That is any orbit-type stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
is a union of orbits which all have the same dimension, hence by Frobenius defines a regular foliation.

34
Complexification and Stratified Distributions

In our discussion so far, pseudobundles were allowed to have base field either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
. In this section, we will show how to produce a complex pseudobundle from a real one. This will then allow us to discuss complex distributions over subcartesian spaces, an important idea as we move towards stratified polarizations.

Definition 8.36

(Complexification)
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a real pseudobundle. Define the complexification, denoted
\pi_{\mathbb{C}}:E\otimes\mathbb{C}\to X
to be the disjoint union

E\otimes\mathbb{C}:=\bigcup_{x\in X}E_{x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C},

with
\pi_{\mathbb{C}}:E\otimes\mathbb{C}\to X
the natural projection.

We wish to endow
\pi_{\mathbb{C}}:E\otimes\mathbb{C}\to X
with the structure of a complex pseudobundle. To do this, we first need to give
E\otimes\mathbb{C}
a subcartesian structure. For this note that for all
x\in X
we have

E_{x}\otimes\mathbb{C}=E_{x}\oplus iE_{x}

where
i\in\mathbb{C}
is the square root of
-1
. Hence, we may view
E\otimes\mathbb{C}
as
E\times_{\pi}E\subseteq E\times E
. Under this identification,
\pi_{\mathbb{C}}
may be viewed as

\pi_{\mathbb{C}}:E\otimes\mathbb{C}\to X;\quad(e_{1},e_{2})\mapsto\pi(e_{1}).

Recall from Example
6.6
that if we write
pr_{1},pr_{2}:E\times E\to E
for the projections onto the first and second factor, respectively, then

C^{\infty}(E\times E)=\langle pr_{1}^{*}C^{\infty}(E)\cup pr_{2}^{*}C^{\infty}(E)\rangle.

By definition of
\pi:E\to X
being a pseudobundle, we have

C^{\infty}(E)=\langle\pi^{*}C^{\infty}(X)\cup C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E)\rangle.

Hence, using the fact that
E\times_{\pi}E\subseteq E\times E
is a closed subset and that
\pi\circ pr_{1}|_{E\times_{\pi}E}=\pi\circ pr_{2}|_{E\times_{\pi}E}
, we deduce

C^{\infty}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})=\langle\pi_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}C^{\infty}(X)\cup pr_{1}^{*}C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E)\cup pr_{2}^{*}C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E)\rangle

(91)

Next, define complex scalar multiplication by
