\mu^{\mathbb{C}}:\mathbb{C}\times E_{\mathbb{C}}\to E_{\mathbb{C}};\quad(x+iy,e_{1}+ie_{2})\mapsto(xe_{1}-ye_{2}+i(xe_{2}+ye_{1}))

(92)

This is a combination of addition and scalar multiplication on
E
and hence is differentiable. Finally, define addition

(E\otimes\mathbb{C})\times_{\pi_{\mathbb{C}}}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})\to E\otimes\mathbb{C};\quad(e_{1}+ie_{2},u_{1}+iu_{2})\mapsto e_{1}+u_{1}+i(e_{2}+u_{2}).

(93)

Theorem 8.37
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a real pseudobundle. Then
\pi_{\mathbb{C}}:E\otimes\mathbb{C}\to X
together with the subcartesian structure in Equation (
91
), complex scalar multiplication in Equation (
92
), and addition in Equation (
93
) is a complex pseudobundle over
X
. Furthermore, if
\pi:E\to X
satisfies any of the following properties, so does
\pi_{\mathbb{C}}:E\otimes\mathbb{C}\to X
.

•

Subtrivial

•

Vector bundle

•

Enough sections

•

Linear Whitney (A)

Proof.

We’ve already done most of the proof already, we just need to check that

C^{\infty}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})=\langle\pi_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}C^{\infty}(X)\cup C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})\rangle,

where
C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})
are linear with respect to the induced real scalar multiplication. Clearly,

\langle\pi_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}C^{\infty}(X)\cup C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})\rangle\subseteq C^{\infty}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})

so we only need to show the reverse containment. This is a triviality since by the definition of
C^{\infty}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})
in Equation (
91
), clearly
pr_{1}^{*}C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E),pr_{2}^{*}C^{\infty}(E)\subseteq C^{\infty}_{(1)}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})
.

The other conditions follows trivially.
∎

A consequence of the fact that
E\otimes\mathbb{C}
is identified with a subset of
E\times E
is the following.

Corollary 8.38
.

Let
\pi:E\to X
be a pseudobundle. Then any section
\sigma\in\Gamma(E\otimes\mathbb{C})
can uniquely be written in the form

\sigma=\sigma_{1}+i\sigma_{2}.

That is, as a real vector space,
\Gamma(E\otimes\mathbb{C})=\Gamma(E)\otimes\mathbb{C}
.

Our main examples thus far of pseudobundles have been Zariski and stratified tangent bundles, thus we can now apply Theorem
8.37
to complexify them.

Definition 8.39

(Complex Zariski/Stratified Tangent Bundle)
.

(1)

Let
X
be a subcartesian space. Call
TX\otimes\mathbb{C}
the
complexified Zariski tangent bundle
of
X
. Write
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
for sections of
TX\otimes\mathbb{C}
and call the elements of
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)

complex Zariski vector fields
.

(2)

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be Whitney regular. Call
T\Sigma\otimes\mathbb{C}
the
complexified stratified tangent bundle
of
(X,\Sigma)
. Write
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)
for sections of
T\Sigma\otimes\mathbb{C}
and call the elements of
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)
complex stratified vector fields.

Let
X
be a subcartesian space. Recall that the elements of the Zariski tangent bundle
TX
have an interpretation as derivations of
C^{\infty}(X)
. We can give an analagous interpretation of
TX\otimes\mathbb{C}
in terms of differentiable complex-valued functions
C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})
.

Proposition 8.40
.

Let
X
be a subcartesian space and
x\in X
. Then we have a canonical identification of
T_{x}\otimes\mathbb{C}
with the space of derivations
v:C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{C}
such that

v(fg)=v(f)g(x)+f(x)v(g).

(94)

Proof.

First, given
v\in T_{x}X\otimes\mathbb{C}
, define derivation

v:C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{C}

as follows. Using Corollary
8.38
, write
v=v_{1}+iv_{2}
for
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
. Then, given a function
f\in C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})
we can also write
f=f_{1}+if_{2}
for
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(X)
. Now define

v(f):=v_{1}(f_{1})-v_{2}(f_{2})+i(v_{1}(f_{2})+v_{2}(f_{1}))

It is then a straightforward matter to check that
v
satisfies Equation (
94
). For the converse, given a complex derivation
v:C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying Equation (
94
), one easily verifies that
v_{1},v_{2}:C^{\infty}(X)\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

v_{1}(f)=\frac{v(f)+\overline{v(f)}}{2}\quad\quad v_{2}(f)=\frac{v(f)-\overline{v(f)}}{2i}

for
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
define elements of
T_{x}X
which satisfy
v=v_{1}+iv_{2}
.
∎

This then allows us to conclude the following.

Corollary 8.41
.

Let
X
be a subcartesian space. Then the complex Zariski vector fields
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
can canonically be identified with the set of complex derivations of
C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
8.40
fibre-wise.
∎

Let us now move to the stratified setting. Given a Whitney regular stratified space, the complexified stratified tangent bundle
T\Sigma\otimes\mathbb{C}
has a natural stratification, namely

T\Sigma\otimes\mathbb{C}=\bigcup_{S\in\Sigma}TS\otimes\mathbb{C}.

In the next chapters, we will be studying generalizations of polarizations from Chapter
4
which will be complex subbundles of stratified tangent bundles. To set this up, let us define distributions.

Definition 8.42

(Stratified Complex Distribution)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a Whitney regular stratified space. A
stratified complex distribution
is a subbundle
D\subseteq TX\otimes\mathbb{C}
over
X
such that
D\to(X,\Sigma)
is a complex stratified pseudobundle. That is,
D\subseteq T\Sigma\otimes\mathbb{C}
is a pseudobundle, for each stratum
S\in\Sigma

D|_{S}\subseteq TS\otimes\mathbb{C}
is a smooth complex subbundle, and the partition

D=\bigcup_{S\in\Sigma}D|_{S}

is a weakly differentiable stratification of
D
.

Example 8.43
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a Whitney stratified space and
E\subseteq T\Sigma
a subbundle such that
E\to(X,\Sigma)
is a stratified pseudobundle. Then clearly the complexification
E\otimes\mathbb{C}\subseteq T\Sigma\otimes\mathbb{C}
is a stratified complex distribution. We call distributions of this form
real
distributions.

Example 8.44
.

Let
G
be a connected abelian Lie group and
(M,I)
a complex manifold such that
M
is a proper
G
-space and
I
is a
G
-invariant complex structure. Then we get two complex
G
-invariant distributions on
M
, namely the holomorphic and antiholomorphic tangent bundles
T^{1,0}M
and
T^{0,1}M
. It then follows from Example
8.30
that

\widetilde{T^{1,0}M}=\bigcup_{x\in M}(T^{1,0}_{x}M)^{G_{x}}

and

\widetilde{T^{0,1}M}=\bigcup_{x\in M}(T^{0,1}_{x}M)^{G_{x}}

are stratified distributions over
(M,\mathcal{S}_{G}(M))
. As we saw in Corollary
8.32
,
\widetilde{TM}=T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
. Thus, we have for each orbit-type stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)

TS\otimes\mathbb{C}=\widetilde{T^{1,0}M}|_{S}\oplus\widetilde{T^{0,1}M}|_{S}.

From here, we see that the above decomposition defines complex structures
\{I_{S}\}
on each stratum.

Note for a Whitney regular stratified space
(X,\Sigma)
, both
\mathfrak{X}(X)
and
\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
have natural Lie bracket structures given by

[V,W]f=V(W(f))-W(V(f))

for
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
. By Corollary
8.38
we have
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)=\mathfrak{X}(X)\otimes\mathbb{C}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)=\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}
, so we can extend the Lie bracket complex-linearly. This bracket can also be realized by the identification of
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
with complex derivations of
C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})
.

Definition 8.45

(Involutive)
.

Let
D\subseteq T\Sigma\otimes\mathbb{C}
be a complex stratified distribution. Say
D
is involutive if for all strata
S\in\Sigma
,
D|_{S}\subseteq TS\otimes\mathbb{C}
is involutive.

Example 8.46
.

Returning now to the case of a connected Lie group
G
and a proper complex
G
-space
(M,I)
from Example
8.44
, we see that
\widetilde{T^{1,0}M}
and
\widetilde{T^{0,1}M}
are examples of involutive stratified distributions since over each stratum, they are given by the holomorphic and antiholomorphic tangent bundles of induced complex structures.

Now to get examples of involutive distributions, we will first need a Lemma.

Lemma 8.47
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a Whitney regular stratified space and
V,W\in\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)
. Then for every stratum
S\in\Sigma
we have

[V|_{S},W|_{S}]=[V,W]|_{S}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{C}}(S).

Proof.

Follows immediately from the fact that the complex Lie bracket is simply the complex-linear extension of the usual Lie bracket and from Corollary
7.59
.
∎

Proposition 8.48
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a Whitney stratified space and
D\subseteq T\Sigma\otimes\mathbb{C}
an involutive complex stratified distribution. Then, for any
V,W\in\Gamma(D)
, we have
[V,W]\in\Gamma(D)
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
8.47
stratum-wise.
∎

Finally, I would like to give a characterization of when a complex distribution on a manifold induces a stratified distribution on a subset.

Lemma 8.49
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a locally closed subspace equipped with a stratification
\Sigma
by embedded submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that
(X,\Sigma)
is Whitney regular. Furthermore, assume let
D\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a complex distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
which is also closed as a subspace, and for each
S\in\Sigma
, let

D_{S}:=D\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}S.

Suppose the following properties hold

(1)

D_{S}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}S
is a complex distribution.

(2)

If
S\subseteq\overline{R}
, then
\text{rank}(D_{S})\leq\text{rank}(D_{R})
.

Then the union

D_{X}:=\bigcup_{S\in\Sigma}D_{S}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma

is a stratified complex distribution on
(X,\Sigma)
.

Proof.

We only need to show that
D_{X}
satisfies the frontier condition. Since this is a local condition, we may assume
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
for some
N
.

To show frontier, let
S,R\in\Sigma
be two strata and suppose
D_{S}\cap\overline{D_{R}}\neq\emptyset
. We show
D_{S}\subseteq D_{R}
. By continuity, we have
S\cap\overline{R}\neq\emptyset
and hence
S\subseteq\overline{R}
.

Let
x\in S
and let
\{x_{k}\}\subseteq R
be a sequence converging to
x
. Since Grassmannians are compact, after possibly passing to a subsequence, we can find
W\subseteq T_{x}\mathbb{R}^{N}
so that
T_{x_{k}}R
converges to
W
in the
\dim(R)
Grassmannian of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Making use of the canonical metric on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we can canonically find orthogonal projections

\Pi_{k}:T_{x_{k}}R\to(D_{R})_{x_{k}}

for each
k
. Extend by
0
to obtain orthogonal projections
\Pi_{k}:T_{x_{k}}\mathbb{R}^{N}\to(D_{R})_{x_{k}}
. By assumption,
D|_{R}
has constant rank along
R
, denoted by
r
. It then follows that the sequence of projections
\{\Pi_{k}\}
lies in the Grassmannian of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
of dimension
r
subspaces. Since this is compact, after potentially passing to a subsequence, we may assume
\{\Pi_{k}\}
converges to some orthogonal projection
\Pi_{\infty}
onto a rank
r
subspace
A
of
T_{x}\mathbb{R}^{N}
. By continuity, it follows that
A\subseteq W
.

Now, observe for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
that
\{\Pi_{k}(w)\}
is a sequence in
D_{R}
and that

\Pi_{\infty}(w)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\Pi_{k}(w).

Since
D
is assumed to be closed, it follows that
\Pi_{\infty}(v)\in D
. Again, using continuity, we also have
\Pi_{\infty}(v)\in D_{x}
. By the dimension assumption, it follows that
\Pi_{\infty}
can be viewed as a projection onto
W\cap D_{x}
and hence contains
(D_{S})_{x}
since
T_{x}S\subseteq W
.

In particular, for any
v\in(D_{S})_{x}
let
v_{k}=\Pi_{k}(v)\in(D_{R})_{x}
. Then

\lim_{k\to\infty}v_{k}=\Pi_{\infty}(v)

Since
v
lies in the image of
\Pi_{\infty}
and
\Pi_{\infty}
is a projection, it follows that
\Pi_{\infty}(v)=v
and hence
v\in\overline{D_{R}}
. Therefore,
D_{S}\subseteq\overline{D_{R}}
.
∎

Part III   Singular Quantization

†
†
margin:

9

Symplectic Stratified Spaces

Now that we have laid the foundations for the study of singular spaces (at least from a subcartesian and stratified perspective), we can now begin our study of symplectic stratified spaces. These are spaces which arose in the work of Sjamaar and Lerman
[
SL91
]
in their study of singular reduced spaces. Their construction will be examined in great detail in Sections
36
,
37
, and
38
. Mol
[
Mol24
]
has refined their ideas greatly into the setting of symplectic groupoids with his “Hamiltonian stratifications”, which we will not be discussing here.

Now, given that is was mentioned in Chapter
6
that subcartesian spaces are not particularly well-suited to a theory of differential forms, it might seem surprising that we can even discuss symplectic geometry–an area defined by distinguished
2
-forms. The key here is that we are studying stratified spaces, spaces which are partitioned into manifolds where differential form theory makes sense. Furthermore, Poisson structures, being “covariant” in nature, are readily definable in a subcartesian setting. So, taking a page out of the theory of symplectic foliations of Poisson structures, we define a symplectic stratified space to be a collection of symplectic forms on each of the strata and a Poisson structure on the total space, such that the inclusion is Poisson. In this way, symplectic stratified spaces are like locally finite symplectic foliations. This idea can be refined even further (and made literal) through Hamiltonian stratifications as studied by Mol
[
Mol24
]
.

35
Symplectic Stratified Spaces

Definition 9.1

(Stratified Symplectic Space)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma,C^{\infty}(X))
be a Whitney stratified space. A symplectic structure on
X
consists of the following data.

(1)

A symplectic form
\omega_{S}
on each stratum
S\in\Sigma
.

(2)

A Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on
C^{\infty}(X)
.

Subject to the compatibility condition that for any stratum
S\in\Sigma
, the inclusion
S\hookrightarrow X
is Poisson. We will write
(X,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
for a symplectic stratified space if the stratification and subcartesian structure are understood from context.

Example 9.2
.

Let
(M,\pi)
be a Poisson manifold and recall from Example
7.19
that we have a canonical foliation
\mathscr{F}_{\pi}
of
M
by symplectic submanifolds. Due to Proposition
7.65
, if
\mathscr{F}_{\pi}
is locally finite and has embedded leaves, then
(M,\mathscr{F}_{\pi})
is a Whitney (A) stratified space. If we suppose further that
\mathscr{F}_{\pi}
is Whitney regular, then
(M,\mathscr{F}_{\pi},\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is a symplectic stratified space.

Example 9.3
.

Consider the Poisson structure

\pi=r^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y}

on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
from Example
3.27
. As we saw, the symplectic foliation
\mathscr{F}_{\pi}
has two symplectic leaves, namely the origin
\{0\}
and its complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
. This is equal to the orbit-type stratification of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by the canonical
S^{1}
-action, hence is a Whitney stratification. Therefore,
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathscr{F}_{\pi},\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is a symplectic stratified space.

Example 9.4
.

A stratification of a Poisson manifold by submanifolds which are also symplectic, need not be a symplectic stratified space. Indeed, write

\mathbb{R}^{4}=\{(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\ |\ x_{i},y_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\}

and let
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{can}
be the canonical Poisson bracket induced by the canonical symplectic form

\omega_{can}=dx_{1}\wedge dy_{1}+dx_{2}\wedge dy_{2}.

Furthermore, let

S=\{(x_{1},y_{1},0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\}

be an embedded copy of symplectic
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Indeed, we can see that

\omega_{S}=\omega_{0}|_{S}=dx_{1}\wedge dy_{1}

and hence
(S,\omega_{S})
is symplectic. However,
S\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{4}
is not Poisson. Indeed, consider
f=x_{1}+x_{2}
and
g=y_{1}+y_{2}
. Then, we can easily compute that

\{f,g\}_{can}=-2

whereas

\{f|_{S},g|_{S}\}_{S}=-1,

where
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{S}
is the induced Poisson structure on
S
from
\omega_{S}
. In particular,
\{f,g\}|_{S}\neq\{f|_{S},g|_{S}\}_{S}
and hence
S\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{4}
is not Poisson. Therefore, the stratification

\Sigma=\{S,\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus S\}

is not a symplectic stratification even though each stratum has a canonical symplectic form.

Just as was the case for Poisson manifolds, symplectic stratified spaces have a notion of a Hamiltonian vector field.

Definition 9.5
.

Let
X
be a symplectic stratified space and
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
a smooth map. The (Zariski) Hamiltonian vector field defined by
f
, denote
V_{f}
is the derivation

V_{f}:C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(X);\quad g\mapsto\{f,g\}.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
X
be a symplectic stratified space and
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
. Then
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
and for any stratum
S\in\Sigma
, if
V^{S}_{f|_{S}}\in\mathfrak{X}(S)
denotes the Hamiltonian vector field induced by the symplectic form
\omega_{S}
, then

V^{S}|_{f|_{S}}=V_{f}|_{S}.

Proof.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
. To first show that
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
, fix a stratum
S\in\Sigma
. Letting
g\in I_{S}
be any function which vanishes on
S
, we have

V_{f}(g)|_{S}=\{f,g\}|_{S}=\{f|_{S},g|_{S}\}_{S}=\{f|_{S},0\}_{S}=0,

(95)

where
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{S}
is the Poisson bracket induced by the symplectic form
\omega_{S}\in\Omega^{2}(S)
. By Proposition
6.58
, it then follows that
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(X,S)
. Since
S
was an arbitrary stratum, it follows that
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
.

Fixing a stratum
S\in\Sigma
once again, to show
V^{S}|_{f|_{S}}=V_{f}|_{S}
we use Equation (
95
). Indeed, since
C^{\infty}(S)=\langle C^{\infty}(X)|_{S}\rangle
, it suffices to show that

V^{S}_{f|_{S}}(g|_{S})=V_{f}|_{S}(g|_{S})

for any
g\in C^{\infty}(X)
. To that end, note that since
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(X,S)
we have from another application of Proposition
6.58
that

V_{f}|_{S}(g|_{S})=V_{f}(g)|_{S}=\{f,g\}|_{S}

On the other hand,

V^{S}_{f|_{S}}(g|_{S})=\{f|_{S},g|_{S}\}_{S}=\{f,g\}|_{S}.

Hence the result.
∎

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
(X,\Sigma,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
be a symplectic stratified space. Then the map

X\times C^{\infty}(X)\to T\Sigma;\quad(x,f)\mapsto V_{f}(x)

is surjective.

Proof.

Let
x\in X
,
S\in\Sigma
the stratum containing
x
, and
v\in T_{x}S
. Since
S
is symplectic, we can find a function
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
so that
V^{S}_{f}(x)=v
, where
V^{S}_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(S)
denotes the Hamiltonian vector field with respect to
S
. Since
C^{\infty}(S)=\langle C^{\infty}(X)|_{S}\rangle
as a Sikorski structure, we can find an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq X
of
x
and a function
F\in C^{\infty}(X)
so that

f|_{S\cap U}=F|_{S\cap U}.

By Lemma
9.6
,
V_{F}|_{S\cap U}=V^{S}_{f}|_{S\cap U}
and thus
V_{F}(x)=v
.
∎

In this way, we see that symplectic stratified spaces are a generalization of Poisson manifolds with locally finite symplectic foliations.

36
The Orbit-Type and Canonical Stratifications of the momentum map

So far, we have only discussed symplectic stratified spaces where the underlying differentiable space is a manifold. Now that we are armed with the Hamiltonian slice theorem as in Theorem
3.49
, we can discuss now some truly singular examples. For all that follows, we will let
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a proper
G
-space for a connected Lie group
G
. Suppose now that
J^{-1}(0)
is nonempty, let
M_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G
, write
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
for the quotient map.

Lemma 9.8
.

Both
J^{-1}(0)
and
M_{0}
are canonically subcartesian spaces, where

C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))=C^{\infty}(M)|_{J^{-1}(0)}

and

C^{\infty}(M_{0})=\{f:M_{0}\to\mathbb{R}\ |\ \pi_{0}^{*}f\in C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}\}.

Proof.

C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))=C^{\infty}(M)|_{J^{-1}(0)}
being a subcartesian structure is a consequence of Proposition
6.17
due to
J^{-1}(0)
being closed. As for
C^{\infty}(M_{0})
being subcartesian, this is precisely the statement of Proposition
6.36
.
∎

We want to show now that
M_{0}
is canonically a symplectic stratified space. As it turns out, thanks to the local normal form from proper Hamiltonian spaces (see Theorem
3.49
), the procedure for defining the stratification is nearly identical to obtaining the canonical stratification on
M/G
.

Definition 9.9
.

Define the
orbit-type
stratification of
J^{-1}(0)
, denoted
\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
to be the partition

\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)}\pi_{0}(S\cap J^{-1}(0)).

Furthermore, define the
canonical stratification
of
M_{0}
by

\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})=\{\pi_{0}(S)\ |\ S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))\}.

Although we called the two partitions defined above stratifications, we still need to show that they are, for instance, partitions into submanifolds with respect to the subcartesian structures in Lemma
9.8
.

Lemma 9.10
.

The pieces of the orbit-type partition
\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
are
G
-invariant embedded submanifolds. Furthermore, for any
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
and
x\in S
, there exists
G_{x}
-equivariant linear symplectomorphism of linear
G_{x}
symplectic representations

\phi_{x}:(T_{x}M,\omega_{x})\to((\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})\times(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{*}\oplus S\nu_{x}(M,G))

under which,

T_{x}\phi(T_{x}S)=(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})\times\{0\}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G)

Proof.

Let us fix
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
and
x\in X
. Since being an embedded submanifold and the structure of tangent spaces is a local question, we pass to a symplectic slice centred on
x
. That is, we may assume
M=G\times_{G_{x}}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G))
,
x=[e,0,0]
, with the symplectic form given by Lemma
3.47
and the momentum map given by

J:G\times_{G_{x}}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G))\to\mathfrak{g}^{*};\quad[g,p,v]\mapsto\text{Ad}_{g}^{*}(p+\mathfrak{p}\circ J_{x}(v)),

where
\mathfrak{p}:\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a
K
-equivariant splitting of the short exact sequence

0\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}\to 0

and
J_{x}:S\nu_{x}(M,G)\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}
is the quadratic momentum map. Observe that

J^{-1}(0)=G\times_{G_{x}}(\{0\}\times J_{x}^{-1}(0))

Hence, by Lemma
3.43
,

S=J^{-1}(0)_{(G_{x})}=G\times_{G_{x}}(\{0\}\times(J_{x}^{-1}(0))^{G_{x}})\cong(G/K)\times S\nu_{x}(M,G)^{G_{x}}

(96)

Hence,
S
is an embedded submanifold. Now, observe that

T_{[e,0,0]}G\times_{K}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G))=(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})\times\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G)

Note that we can identify
\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}
with
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{*}
and that by construction of the symplectic form on normal form neighbourhoods, the symplectic form
\omega
restricts to the canonical symplectic form on
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})\times(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{*}=T^{*}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})
. Hence,

T_{[e,0,0]}M=(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})\times(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})^{*}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G).

Next, from our description of
S
in Equation (
96
) we have

T_{[e,0,0]}S=(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{x})\times\{0\}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G).

∎

Corollary 9.11
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
. Then
\omega|_{S}
is basic and the induced
2
-form
\omega_{S}\in\Omega^{2}(S/G)
is symplectic.

Proof.

Since
\omega
is invariant, it follows that
\omega|_{G\cdot x}=0
for all
x\in M
. In particular, due to Lemma
9.10
, it follows for any
x\in S
that

\ker((\omega|_{S})_{x}^{\flat})=T_{x}(G\cdot x).

Therefore,
\omega|_{S}
is basic and hence there exists a unique
2
-form
\omega_{S}\in\Omega^{2}(S/G)
satisfying

\pi_{S}^{*}\omega_{S}=\omega|_{S}.

This form being symplectic is a consequence of linear symplectic reduction (Proposition
3.12
).
∎

Making use of some results, we now have shown that
\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
and
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
are partitions into connected smooth manifolds and that each piece of
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
is symplectic. To finish showing each partition is a stratification, we just need to show that they are both locally finite and satisfy the axiom of the frontier. To do this, we first show it’s true for linear symplectic representations.

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
K
be a compact Lie group and
(V,\omega)
a symplectic
K
-representation. Write
J_{V}:V\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
for the quadratic momentum map. Then the partition

\mathcal{S}_{K}(J^{-1}(0))=\bigcup_{H\leq K}\pi_{0}(J_{V}^{-1}(0)\cap V_{(H)})

is finite stratification of
J^{-1}(0)
. Furthermore, each piece of
\mathcal{S}_{K}(J^{-1}(0))
is invariant under scalar multiplication by
t\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

See
[
Mol24
]
for a proof that
\mathcal{S}_{K}(J^{-1}(0))
is finite.

We will now show that
\mathcal{S}_{K}(J^{-1}(0))
is a stratification with strata closed under scalar multiplication by elements of
(0,1)
at the same time. First, recall that by Lemma
3.43

V^{K}
is a symplectic subspace and its symplectic complement
W=(V^{K})^{\omega}
is a symplectic subrepresentation. Furthermore, letting
J_{W}:W\to\mathfrak{k}^{*}
be the quadratic momentum map, we have

J_{V}^{-1}(0)=V^{K}+J_{W}^{-1}(0).

In particular, for any subgroup
H\leq K
we have

J_{V}^{-1}(0)_{(H)}=V^{K}+J_{W}^{-1}(0)_{(H)}

and

J_{V}^{-1}(0)_{(K)}=V^{K}.

Now, since
J_{W}
is an invariant quadratic map (i.e.
J_{W}(tw)=t^{2}J_{W}(w)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
w\in W
), it follows that the orbit-type pieces
J_{W}^{-1}(0)_{(H)}
are closed under scalar multiplication by non-zero scalars. Hence, so are their connected components. Using this, we can now show for any
R\in\mathcal{S}_{K}(J^{-1}(0))
that

J_{V}^{-1}(0)_{(K)}\subseteq\overline{R}.

Indeed, supposing
R\subseteq V_{(H)}
for some
H\leq K
, there exists a connected component
R^{\prime}\subseteq J_{W}^{-1}(0)_{(H)}
so that

R=V^{K}+R^{\prime}.

Fixing any
v\in V^{K}
and
w\in R^{\prime}
, consider now the sequence
\{v+w/n\}_{n>0}
. Since
R^{\prime}
is closed under scalar multiplication by non-zero scalars, this sequence lies in
R
. Hence, the limit

\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigg(v+\frac{w}{n}\bigg)=v

lies in
\overline{R}
. Therefore,
J_{V}^{-1}(0)_{(K)}\subseteq\overline{R}
.

Now for any two arbitrary strata
S,R\in\mathcal{S}_{K}(J^{-1}(0))
with
S\cap\overline{R}\neq\emptyset
, pass to a symplectic slice neighbourhood around
x\in S\cap\overline{R}
. Then repeating the same argument in the symplectic normal space
S\nu_{x}(V,K)
shows
S\subseteq\overline{R}
.
∎

Theorem 9.13

(
[
SL91
,
Zim24
]
)
.

Both
\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
and
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
are quasi-homogeneous stratifications. Furthermore, for each stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
, there exists a unique symplectic form
\omega_{S/G}\in\Omega^{2}(S/G)
such that

(\pi_{0}|_{S})^{*}\omega_{S/G}=\omega|_{S}.

Proof.

First to show
\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
is a quasi-homogeneous stratification let
x\in J^{-1}(0)
and
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
the stratum containing
x
. Passing to a symplectic slice neighbourhood, we may assume

M=G\times_{G_{x}}(\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\circ}\times S\nu_{x}(M,G))

with

J^{-1}(0)=G\times_{G_{x}}(\{0\}\times J_{x}^{-1}(0)),

where
J_{x}:S\nu_{x}(M,G)\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}
is the quadratic momentum map. Thus, since
\mathcal{S}_{G_{x}}(J_{x}^{-1}(0))
is a finite stratification by invariant subsets, it follows immediately that
\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
is a quasi-homogeneous stratification.

Since the quotient map
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
is an open quotient, it follows that
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
is also a stratification. Now using an argument near identical to the one from Theorem
7.81
, we see that
\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
is also quasi-homogeneous.
∎

37
Manifolds of Symmetry and Singular Reduced Space

Let
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space. The strata of the reduced space
M_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G
can also be realized through usual Marsden-Weinstein-Meyer reduction, but we need to pass to the manifolds of symmetry first. Indeed, fix
x\in J^{-1}(0)
and write
K=G_{x}
. Recall from Corollary
2.15
and Proposition
2.22
that the manifold of symmetry
M_{K}=\{y\in M\ |\ G_{y}=K\}
is a submanifold of
M
and, furthermore,

T_{y}M_{K}=(T_{y}M)^{K}

for any
y\in M_{K}
. As we have seen, the fixed point set of a symplectic representation is once again symplectic, hence
M_{K}
is a symplectic submanifold of
M
. Now, by Corollary
2.15
,
M_{K}
is a free
N_{G}(K)/K
-space where
N_{G}(K)
is the normalizer group of
K
. Since the
G
-action preserves the symplectic form, it follows that
N_{G}(K)/K
preserves the form on
M_{K}
as well.

We will now show that this is a Hamiltonian action. To set this up, let
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
. Since
K
acts trivially on
M_{K}
, it easily follows that
\xi_{M}|_{M_{K}}=0
. In particular, for any
y\in M_{K}
and any
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
we have

\langle J(y),\xi\rangle=0

and thus
J(M|_{K})\subseteq\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}
. Now let
\iota:\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}
be the inclusion, then since
\iota
is
K
-equivariant, it follows that

\iota^{*}\circ J(M|_{K})\subseteq\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)^{*}

Using the identification
\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\cong(\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
we obtain a canonical
K
-equivariant map

J_{K}:M_{K}\to(\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)/\mathfrak{k})^{*}.

Since the action of
G
on
M
is Hamiltonian, it follows that so is the action of
N_{G}(K)
on
M_{K}
and hence
N_{G}(K)/K
. Thus, we obtain the following.

Proposition 9.14
.

Let
K\leq G
be a compact subgroup with
M_{K}\neq\emptyset
. Then
J_{K}:M_{K}\to(\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
is a momentum map for the
N_{G}(K)/K
action on
M_{K}
.

Since the action of
N_{G}(K)/K
on
M_{K}
is free and proper, usual Marsden-Weinstein-Meyer reduction applies and thus
J_{K}^{-1}(0)/(N_{G}(K)/K)
is canonically a symplectic manifold. As it turns out, this symplectic manifold is precisely one of the canonical strata of the singular reduced space!

Theorem 9.15
.

Let
x\in J^{-1}(0)
and write
K=G_{x}
.

(1)

J^{-1}(0)\cap M_{K}=J_{K}^{-1}(0)
.

(2)

Write
(M_{K})_{0}
for the reduced space of
J_{K}:M_{K}\to(\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
. If
x\in J_{K}^{-1}(0)
and
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
is the orbit-type stratum through containing
x
, then there is a canonical symplectomorphism between
S/G
and the connected component of
(M_{K})_{0}
containing
[x]
.

Proof.

(1)

This follows immediately from the fact that
J_{K}
is obtained by restricting
J
.

(2)

Recall from Corollary
2.15
that
G\cdot M_{K}=M_{(K)}
. Thus, we obtain a
G
-equivariant bijection

G\times_{N_{G}(K)}J_{K}^{-1}(0)\to J^{-1}(0)_{(K)}

Furthermore, this bijection must preserve the restricted symplectic form on each connected component of
J^{-1}(0)_{(K)}
. Hence, the result then follows.

∎

38
The Poisson Bracket on Reduced Space

Let
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space. We have nearly shown that
(M_{0},\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0}))
is a symplectic stratified space. Indeed, by Theorem
9.13
,
M_{0}
is quasi-homogeneous and each stratum is equipped with a canonical symplectic form. Furthermore, as we saw in Theorem
7.77
quasi-homogeneous implies Whitney regular. So the only last piece of the puzzle is the Poisson bracket. To do this, we need to first endow
C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}=C^{\infty}(M)^{G}|_{J^{-1}(0)}
with a Poisson bracket.

Recall now if
M
has a single orbit-type, then
J^{-1}(0)
is a coisotropic submanifold of
M
. In particular, this means that if
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, then its Hamiltonian vector field
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is tangent to
J^{-1}(0)
. In particular, for any
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
we can define

\{f|_{J^{-1}(0)},g|_{J^{-1}(0)}\}_{J^{-1}(0)}:=V_{f}|_{J^{-1}(0)}(g|_{J^{-1}(0)}),

which endows
C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))
with a Poisson structure. In the singular case, a similar construction will not necessarily work for the entire subcartesian structure
C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))=C^{\infty}(M)|_{J^{-1}(0)}
, but we can provide a Poisson bracket to
C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}
. First, we need to establish a relationship between the manifolds of symmetry and
J^{-1}(0)
.

Lemma 9.16
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
and let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
. Then the Hamiltonian vector field
V_{f}
is tangent to
S
. In symbols,
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M,S)^{G}
.

Proof.

Fix
f\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
and
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
. From the defining equation of
V_{f}
,

df=\omega(V_{f},\cdot)

and the fact that
\omega
is invariant, it follows that
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)^{G}
. Now fix
x\in S
, we will show
V_{f}(x)\in T_{x}S
.

As we showed in Proposition
2.39
, equivariant vector fields are tangent to the manifolds of symmetry. Thus, if we write
K=G_{x}
, then
V_{f}(x)\in T_{x}M_{K}
. Furthermore, by Theorem
9.15
, we have

J^{-1}(0)\cap M_{K}=J_{K}^{-1}(0)

Observe that for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)
, we have by the equivariance of
f
that
\xi_{M}(f)=0
. This then implies that
V_{f}|_{M_{K}}(J_{K}^{\xi})=0
for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)
. Thus, since
0
is a regular value of
J_{K}
, we obtain that
V_{f}
is tangent to
J_{K}^{-1}(0)
. The result then follows.
∎

Corollary 9.17
.

If
f\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
, the Hamiltonian vector field of
f
is tangent to
J^{-1}(0)
as a Zariski vector field and the restriction is an equivariant stratified vector field. In symbols,
V_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and
V_{f}|_{J^{-1}(0)}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0)))^{G}
.

Recall from Proposition
6.58
that if
X
is a subcartesian space,
Y\subseteq X
is a subspace, and
V\in\mathfrak{X}(X,Y)
, then for any
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
we have

V|_{Y}(f|_{Y})=V(f)|_{Y}.

Using this, we can now define a Poisson bracket on
C^{\infty}(M)^{G}|_{J^{-1}(0)}

Definition 9.18
.

Define the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{J^{-1}(0)}
on
C^{\infty}(M)^{G}|_{J^{-1}(0)}
by

\{f|_{J^{-1}(0)},g|_{J^{-1}(0)}\}_{J^{-1}(0)}:=V_{f}(g)|_{J^{-1}(0)},

where
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
.

And now, we can finally endow the reduced space with a Poisson bracket.

Definition 9.19

(Poisson Bracket on Reduced Space)
.

Let
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M_{0})
. Define their Poisson bracket
\{f,g\}_{0}\in C^{\infty}(M_{0})
to be the unique function so that

\pi_{0}^{*}\{f,g\}_{0}=\{\pi_{0}^{*}f,\pi_{0}^{*}g\}_{J^{-1}(0)}

Before we can prove the main result of this section, we need to discuss the Poisson brackets on the strata of
M_{0}
.

Proposition 9.20
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
be an orbit-type stratum,
\pi_{S}:S\to S/G
the quotient map, and
\omega_{S}\in\Omega^{2}(S/G)
the unique symplectic form satisfying
\pi_{S}^{*}\omega_{S}=\omega|_{S}
. Then, given
f\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
, we have

(\pi_{S})_{*}(V_{f}|_{S})=V_{f_{0}},

where
f_{0}\in C^{\infty}(S/G)
satisfies
\pi_{S}^{*}f_{0}=f|_{S}
and
V_{f_{0}}\in\mathfrak{X}(S/G)
is the Hamiltonian vector field of
f_{0}
with respect to
\omega_{S}
.

Proof.

Fix
f\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
and let
f_{0}\in C^{\infty}(S/G)
satisfy
\pi_{S}^{*}f_{0}=f|_{S}
. First note that since
S
is assumed to have only one orbit-type, by Corollary
2.40
, the map

(\pi_{S})^{*}:\mathfrak{X}(S)^{G}\to\mathfrak{X}(S/G)

is surjective. In particular, there exists vector field
\widetilde{V}\in\mathfrak{X}(S)^{G}
so that
(\pi_{S})_{*}\widetilde{V}=V_{f_{0}}
. From this, we can easily deduce that

\pi_{S}^{*}(\omega_{S}(V_{f_{0}},\cdot))=(\pi_{S}^{*}\omega_{S})(\widetilde{V},\cdot)=\omega|_{S}(\widetilde{V},\cdot).

On the other hand, by definition,
df_{0}=\omega_{S}(V_{f_{0}},\cdot)
and thus

\pi_{S}^{*}(\omega_{S}(V_{f_{0}},\cdot))=\pi_{S}^{*}(df_{0})=df|_{S}.

Using the fact that
V_{f}
is tangent to
S
, we obtain
df|_{S}=\omega|_{S}(V_{f}|_{S},\cdot)
and hence we have

\omega|_{S}(\widetilde{V},\cdot)=\omega|_{S}(V_{f}|_{S},\cdot)

Locally extending
\widetilde{V}
and using the non-degeneracy of
\omega
, we obtain
\widetilde{V}=V_{f}|_{S}
and thus the result follows.
∎

Theorem 9.21
.

The reduced space
(M_{0},\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0}),\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{0})
is a symplectic stratified space.

Proof.

We need to show the inclusions of the strata are Poisson. To do so, let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
be a orbit-type stratum,
\omega_{S}\in\Omega^{2}(S/G)
the induced symplectic form, and
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M_{0})
. Writing
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{S}
for the Poisson bracket on
C^{\infty}(S/G)
, we show

\{f,g\}_{0}|_{S/G}=\{f|_{S/G},g|_{S/G}\}_{S}

To do this, let
F,G\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
satisfy
\pi_{0}^{*}f=F|_{J^{-1}(0)}
and
\pi_{0}^{*}g=G|_{J^{-1}(0)}
. If we write
\pi_{S}:=\pi_{0}|_{S}
, then by Proposition
9.20
, we have

\pi_{S}^{*}\{f|_{S/G},g|_{S/G}\}_{S}=\pi_{S}^{*}(V_{f|_{S/G}}(g|_{S/G}))=V_{F|_{S}}(G|_{S})=\{F,G\}|_{S}

Conversely, by the definition of the Poisson bracket on
M_{0}
,

\pi_{S}^{*}\{f,g\}_{0}|_{S/G}=(\pi_{0}^{*}\{f,g\}_{0})|_{S/G}=\{\pi_{0}^{*}f,\pi_{0}^{*}g\}_{J^{-1}(0)}|_{S}=\{F,G\}|_{S}

Hence,

\pi_{S}^{*}\{f|_{S/G},g|_{S/G}\}_{S}=\pi_{S}^{*}(\{f,g\}_{0}|_{S/G})

and so, since
\pi_{S}
is a surjective submersion, we obtain

\{f|_{S/G},g|_{S/G}\}_{S}=\{f,g\}_{0}|_{S/G}

which completes the proof.
∎

39
Hamiltonian Modules

Let us now introduce an important class of vector fields on a symplectic stratified space, called a Hamiltonian Module. These are a distinguished class of vector fields which contain all the Hamiltonian vector fields, together with a generating set for the stratified tangent bundle. As differential forms are not a well-behaved topic in the subcartesian setting, these will allow us to give a “smoothness” criterion to connections of line bundles later on.

Definition 9.22

(Hamiltonian Module)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
be a symplectic stratified space. A Hamiltonian module is a sub Lie algebra
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
such that

(1)

\mathcal{H}
contains all Hamiltonian vector fields.

(2)

For any stratum
S\in\Sigma
,
x\in S
, and smooth vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}(S)
, we can find a vector field
W\in\mathcal{H}
and a neighbourhood
U\subseteq X
of
x
so that

V|_{U\cap S}=W|_{U\cap S}.

Example 9.23
.

Suppose
(M,\pi)
is a Poisson manifold so that the symplectic foliation
\mathscr{F}_{\pi}
is a Whitney regular stratification of
M
. Then, the set of all smooth vector fields tangent to the leaves
\mathfrak{X}(\mathscr{F}_{\pi})
is a Hamiltonian module of
(M,\mathscr{F}_{\pi},\{\cdot,\cdot\})
.

Example 9.24
.

Returning now to the Poisson structure

\pi=r^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y}

from Example
3.27
. The symplectic foliation has two leaves,
\{0\}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
and defines a symplectic stratification. And so, one possible Hamiltonian module would be

\mathfrak{X}(\mathscr{F}_{\pi})=\{V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ |\ V_{0}=0\}.

However, we can produce an even smaller Hamiltonian module by controling the order of vanishing at the origin.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We will say
V
vanishes to order
2
at
0
in a neighbourhood
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the origin, we can find a vector field
W\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
so that

V|_{U}=r^{2}W.

Define then

\mathcal{H}=\{V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ |\ V\text{ vanishes to order }2\text{ at the origin}\}.

I claim
\mathcal{H}
is a Hamiltonian module. Indeed, any
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the Hamiltonian vector field
V_{f}
is given by

V_{f}=r^{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}-r^{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}

Thus,
V_{f}=r^{2}V_{f}^{can}
, where
V_{f}^{can}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the Hamiltonian vector field with respect to the canonical symplectic form on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Next given any vector field
W\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\})
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
, using bump functions we can find open neighbourhoods
A\subset B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
of
x
and a vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

V|_{A}=W|_{A}

and so that
V|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B}=0
. By definition,
V\in\mathcal{H}
as it vanishes to order
\infty
at
0
. Thus,
\mathcal{H}
is a Hamiltonian module.

For all that follows, we let
G
be a connected Lie group,
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space, and
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
an equivariant prequantum line bundle. We will be showing in this section that
L
induces a stratified prequantum line bundle on the singular reduced space
M_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G
. To do this, we first need to construct the Hamiltonian module. Recall that we saw in Corollary
9.17
the Hamiltonian vector fields of equivariant functions on
M
were tangent to the strata of
J^{-1}(0)
. This result can be somewhat generalized.

Proposition 9.25
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
, then
V|_{J^{-1}(0)}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}
.

Proof.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and let
K\leq G
be a compact subgroup. Since
V
is
G
-invariant, we have
V|_{M_{K}}\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{K})
. So we just need to show that
V|_{M_{K}}
is tangent to
J^{-1}(0)\cap M_{K}
. Observe that from Theorem
9.15
that

J^{-1}(0)\cap M_{K}=J_{K}^{-1}(0),

where
J_{K}:M_{K}\to(\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
is the induced momentum map from the free
N_{G}(K)/K
action on
M_{K}
. Since the action of
N_{G}(K)/K
on
M_{K}
is free, it follows that for any
x\in J_{K}^{-1}(0)
we have

T_{x}J_{K}^{-1}(0)=\ker(T_{x}J_{K}).

In particular,
V|_{M_{K}}
is tangent to
J_{K}^{-1}(0)
if and only if
V|_{M_{K}}J_{K}
along
J_{K}^{-1}(0)
. This follows immediately from the fact that
VJ=0
along
J^{-1}(0)
and that
J|_{M_{K}}=J_{K}
. Hence the result.
∎

Corollary 9.26
.

The equivariant Zariski vector fields on
J^{-1}(0)
coincide with the equivariant stratified vector fields. That is,
\mathfrak{X}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}=\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0)))^{G}
.

Proof.

Fix
V\in\mathfrak{X}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}
. Then since
J^{-1}(0)
is closed, we can extend
V
to a vector field
W\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
. After averaging, we may assume
W\in\mathfrak{X}(M)^{G}
. By assumption
W|_{J^{-1}(0)}=V
. Hence, by Proposition
9.25
,
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0)))^{G}
. The reverse inclusion is obvious.
∎

So we see that equivariant Zariski vector fields tangent to
J^{-1}(0)
must be tangent to the strata of
J^{-1}(0)
themselves. This is somewhat expected since equivariant vector fields on all of
M
must themselves be tangent to the orbit-type strata. This now provides us with a natural class of vector fields to push down to the stratified quotient.

Proposition 9.27
.

Suppose
V\in\mathfrak{X}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and
\pi:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
the quotient map, then the fibrewise pushforward
\pi_{*}V
defines a stratified vector field on
M_{0}
.

Proof.

Fix
V\in\mathfrak{X}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}
. Since
V
is
G
-invariant, we have
(\pi_{0})_{*}V\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{0})
. Furthermore, by Corollary
9.26
,
V
is tangent to the strata. Hence, if
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
is an orbit-type stratum, we have

(\pi_{0}|_{S})_{*}(V|_{S})\in\mathfrak{X}(S/G).

Now I claim that
(\pi_{0})_{*}V
is also stratified and that

(\pi_{0})_{*}V|_{S/G}=(\pi_{0}|_{S})_{*}(V|_{S})

(97)

First, let us suppose
f\in C^{\infty}(M_{0})
satisfies
f|_{S/G}=0
. Lifting
f
to
F\in C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}
we see that
F|_{S}=0
. Hence, for any
x\in S
we have

T_{x}\pi_{0}(V)f=V_{x}(\pi_{0}^{*}f)=V_{x}(F)=0.

Thus,
(\pi_{0})_{*}V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0}))
. Now to show Equation (
97
). Let
f\in C^{\infty}(S/G)
be a smooth function and let
F\in C^{\infty}(S)^{G}
be a lift. Observe that

\displaystyle((\pi_{0})_{*}V)|_{S/G}(f|_{S/G})

\displaystyle=V|_{S}(\pi_{0}^{*}f|_{S/G})

\displaystyle=V|_{S}(F|_{S})

Furthermore,

\displaystyle(\pi_{0}|_{S})_{*}(V|_{S})(f|_{S/G})

\displaystyle=V|_{S}((\pi_{0}|_{S})^{*}f|_{S/G})

\displaystyle=(V|_{S})(F|_{S})

These two expressions are equal and so equality in Equation (
97
) holds.
∎

Due to the constructions above, we get a natural map

\pi_{*}:\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}\to\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0}))

(98)

given by restricting a vector field to
J^{-1}(0)
, then pushing it forward as in Proposition
9.27
.

Lemma 9.28
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
be an invariant smooth function. Then under the map in Equation (
98
), we have

\pi_{*}(V_{f})=V_{f_{0}},

where
f_{0}\in C^{\infty}(M_{0})
is the unique smooth function so that

\pi_{0}^{*}f_{0}=f|_{J^{-1}(0)}.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
9.20
stratum-wise.
∎

Definition 9.29

(Hamiltonian Module for Singular Reduction)
.

Define the Hamiltonian module
\mathcal{H}_{G}(M_{0})
for
M_{0}
to be the image of the map
\pi_{*}
in Equation (
98
). We will call elements of
\pi_{*}

liftable stratified vector fields
.

Remark 9.30
.

In an earlier version of this thesis, I had a faulty proof that
\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0}))=\mathcal{H}_{G}(M_{0})
. I still believe that this is true, and my original proof goes through in the case that
M_{0}
is a manifold with corners. For more general quotients like the ones being considered in this thesis, perhaps other arguments need to be put forward.

Theorem 9.31
.

\mathcal{H}_{G}(M_{0})
in Definition
9.29
is indeed a Hamiltonian module.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.28
, the Hamiltonian vector fields of
M_{0}
lie in
\mathcal{H}(M_{0})
. So we only have to show now that vector fields of the strata of
M_{0}
admit extensions to elements of
\mathcal{H}(M_{0})
. Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
be an orbit type stratum and
V\in\mathfrak{X}(S/G)
a compactly supported vector field. Then we can find an invariant lift
W\in\mathfrak{X}(S)^{G}
. Fix
x\in S
so that
\pi(x)
lies in the support of
V
. Since
J^{-1}(0)
is smoothly locally trivial, we can find an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
of
x
and a diffeomorphism to an open neighbourhood
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
around
(0,0)

\phi:U\to\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}

such that
\phi(x)=(0,0)
,
\phi(U\cap S)=\Omega\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{0\})
and, if
S\subseteq\overline{R}
, then
\phi(U\cap R)=\Omega\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times L)
, where
L\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\{0\}
is a submanifold. We can then push
W
forward to
\Omega\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{0\})
by
\phi
. The image of
W
by
\phi
can then be trivially extended to all of
\Omega
by declaring

(\phi_{*}W)_{(x,y)}=(\phi_{*}W)_{(x,0)}\in T_{(x,y)}\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Pushing back to
\Omega
, we have then extended
W
. Thus, in a neighbourhood of any point
x\in S
, we can extend the invariant lift
W
to a neighbourhood of
x
in
M
, which remains tangent to the strata of
J^{-1}(0)
. Using an invariant bump function, we can, after possibly shrinking
U
, obtain a
G
-invariant vector field
W\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
with
\pi_{*}(W|_{U\cap S})=V|_{(U\cap S)/G}
. Hence the claim.
∎

40
Singular Reduction of Cotangent Bundles

In Section
11
, we discussed in great detail the symplectic reduction of cotangent bundles in the case where the base manifold has only one orbit-type. We now would like to generalize that discussion to the arbitrary case. This discussion is based on the work found in
[
PROSD07
]
.

For all that follows, let
G
be a connected Lie group,
Q
a proper
G
-space and
J:T^{*}Q\to Q
the Hamiltonian lift to the cotangent bundle. Write

•

\tau_{Q}:T^{*}Q\to Q
for the bundle projection map.

•

\theta_{Q}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}Q)
for the Liouville
1
-form and
\omega_{Q}=-d\theta_{Q}
for the canonical symplectic form.

Recall that for any
\alpha\in T^{*}Q
, we define
J(\alpha)\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by the pairing

\langle J(\alpha),\xi\rangle=\langle\alpha,\xi_{Q}(\tau(\alpha))\rangle,

where
\xi_{Q}\in\mathfrak{X}(Q)
is the fundamental vector field associated to
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. In particular, since the fundamental vector fields span the tangent spaces to the orbits on
Q
, we have

J^{-1}(0)=\bigsqcup_{q\in Q}(T_{q}(G\cdot q))^{\circ}.

Hence,
J^{-1}(0)
is a pseudobundle over
Q
. Note that since the orbit-type strata of
Q
are saturated by
G
-orbits of the same dimension, it follows that for each orbit-type stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
, the restriction
J^{-1}(0)|_{S}\to S
is a
G
-equivariant vector bundle. Hence,
J^{-1}(0)
can be partitioned canonically into equivariant smooth vector bundles

J^{-1}(0)=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)}J^{-1}(0)|_{S}.

However, it should be noted that despite the fact that this is a
G
-invariant partition of
J^{-1}(0)
into embedded submanifolds, this is
not
the orbit-type stratification of
J^{-1}(0)
. Indeed, consider the following example.

Example 9.32
.

Consider the canonical action of
S^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by rotations about the origin. Identifying
\mathbb{R}^{2}=\mathbb{C}
, this action is simply multiplication by elements of
S^{1}
:

S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2};\quad(e^{it},x)\mapsto e^{it}x.

Let
J:T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be the Hamiltonian lift of this action. Using the canonical trivialization
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the
S^{1}
action becomes

e^{it}\cdot(x,y)=(e^{it}x,e^{it}y)

and the momentum map becomes

J(x,y)=x_{1}y_{2}-x_{2}y_{1}.

where
x=(x_{1},x_{2})
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
. Its easy to see that the orbit-type stratification of the base
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is given by

\mathcal{S}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\{\{0\},\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\}

Given the description of the momentum map, we see that restricting
J^{-1}(0)
to the stratum
\{0\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
is

J^{-1}(0)|_{\{0\}}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Clearly
J^{-1}(0)|_{\{0\}}
is equivariantly isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and hence can be further subdivided into strata.

To obtain a nice description of the orbit-type stratification of
J^{-1}(0)
, let us introduce another pseudobundle.

Definition 9.33

(Conormal Bundle)
.

Define the
Conormal Bundle
to the orbit type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
on the base manifold to be the pseudobundle
\nu^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q))
defined fibre-wise by

\nu^{*}_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)):=\bigcup_{q\in Q}(T_{q}\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q))^{\circ}\subseteq T^{*}Q.

Write
\nu^{*}(S):=\nu^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q))|_{S}
for any stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
and call
\nu^{*}(S)
the conormal bundle to
S
.

It’s easy to see that if
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
is an orbit-type stratum, then
\nu^{*}(S)
is a
G
-equivariant vector bundle over
S
and hence admits its own orbit-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{G}(\nu^{*}(S))
. Let us now look at the local structure of both
J^{-1}(0)
and
\nu^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q))
.

Recall the Slice Theorem (Theorem
2.13
), for any
q\in Q
with
G_{q}=K
, there is a
G
-equivariant diffeomorphism between a neighbourhood of
q
and a neighbourhood of
[e,0]
in
G\times_{K}V
for
V=\nu_{q}(Q,G)
. As we saw in Example
2.30
, the cotangent bundle of slice neighbourhoods has the form

T^{*}(G\times_{K}V)=G\times_{K}(V\times\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times V^{*})

As we saw in Proposition
2.11
, if
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
is the unique orbit-type stratum containing
q
, then in this neighbourhood,
S
becomes identified with
G\times_{K}V^{K}
. Thus, we have

T^{*}Q|_{S}=G\times_{K}(V^{K}\times\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times V^{*})

and

T^{*}S=G\times_{K}(V^{K}\times\mathfrak{k}^{\circ}\times(V^{K})^{*})

Thus the canonical projection
T^{*}Q|_{S}\to T^{*}S
is induced by the canonical projection
V^{*}\to(V^{K})^{*}
coming from the inclusion
V^{K}\hookrightarrow V^{*}
. Therefore, in this neighbourhood, we have

\nu^{*}(S)=G\times_{K}(V^{K}\times\{0\}\times(V^{K})^{\circ}).

(99)

Proposition 9.34
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
be an orbit-type stratum and
\tau:\nu^{*}(S)\to S
the conormal bundle. Suppose
S\subseteq Q_{(K)}
for some compact
K\leq G
.

(1)

The orbit-type
\nu^{*}(S)_{(K)}
is the image of the zero section
S\hookrightarrow\nu^{*}(S)
.

(2)

If
R\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(\nu^{*}(S))
is an orbit-type stratum, then
R\subseteq\nu^{*}(S)_{(H)}
for some compact subgroup
H\leq K
. Furthermore, the restriction of the projection
\tau:\nu^{*}(S)\to S
induces a
G
-equivariant fibre bundle
\tau|_{R}:R\to S
.

Proof.

(1)

This follows from Equation (
99
). Indeed, the canonical projection
V^{*}\to(V^{K})^{*}
restricts to a linear isomorphism
(V^{*})^{K}\to(V^{K})^{*}
. Hence, the fixed point set of the annihilator
(V^{K})^{\circ}
under the action of
K
is just the origin. In particular,

\nu^{*}(S)_{(K)}=G\times_{K}(V^{K}\times\{0\}\times((V^{K})^{\circ})^{K})=G\times_{K}(V^{K}\times\{0\}\times\{0\})

hence is the zero section.

(2)

Since the projection
\tau:\nu^{*}(S)\to S
is equivariant, we must have
G_{\alpha}\subseteq G_{\tau(\alpha)}
for any
\alpha\in\nu^{*}(S)
. Thus, it follows if
R\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{G}(\nu^{*}(S))
is an orbit-type stratum, then
R\subseteq\nu^{*}(S)_{(H)}
for some subgroup
H\leq K
.

In the local form in Equation (
99
), we have

\nu^{*}(S)_{(H)}=G\times_{K}(V^{K}\times\{0\}\times(V^{K})^{\circ}_{(H)})

The restriction of the projection
\tau:\nu^{*}(S)\to S
locally has the form

G\times_{K}(V^{K}\times\{0\}\times(V^{K})^{\circ}_{(H)})\to G\times_{K}V^{K};\quad[g,v,0,\lambda]\mapsto[g,v]

Hence, restricting to connected components, we trivially get a
G
-equivariant fibre bundle over
S
.

∎

Lemma 9.35
.

Suppose
Q
is equipped with a
G
-invariant metric and let
\iota_{S}:T^{*}S\hookrightarrow T^{*}Q|_{S}
be the induced splitting of the short exact sequence

0\to\nu^{*}(S)\to T^{*}Q|_{S}\to T^{*}S\to 0.

Writing
J_{S}:T^{*}S\to S
for the Hamiltonian lift of the
G
-action on
S
, we have the following.

(1)

\iota_{S}^{*}\theta_{Q}=\theta_{S}
, where
\theta_{S}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}S)
is the Liouville
1
-form.

(2)

The diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}

Proof.

(1)

Let
x\in S
,
\alpha\in T_{x}^{*}S
, and
v\in T_{\alpha}(T^{*}S)
. Writing
\tau_{S}:T^{*}S\to S
recall that

\theta_{S}(v)=\langle\alpha,(\tau_{S})_{*}v\rangle.

Now, we have

\iota_{S}^{*}\theta_{Q}(v)=\theta_{Q}((\iota_{S})_{*}v)=\langle\iota_{S}(\alpha),(\tau_{Q})_{*}(\iota_{S})_{*}v\rangle

Clearly
\tau_{Q}\circ\iota_{S}=\tau_{S}
and hence

\langle\iota_{S}(\alpha),(\tau_{Q})_{*}(\iota_{S})_{*}v\rangle=\langle\iota_{S}(\alpha),(\tau_{S})_{*}v\rangle.

By definition of the splitting, since
(\tau_{S})_{*}v\in TS
, we have

\langle\iota_{S}(\alpha),(\tau_{S})_{*}v\rangle=\langle\alpha,(\tau_{S})_{*}v\rangle

Hence the result.

(2)

Similar to (1).

∎

Thus, if we define

T^{*}\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q):=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)}\iota_{S}(T^{*}S)

we get the following.

Proposition 9.36
.

Suppose
Q
has a
G
-invariant Riemannian metric. Then,
T^{*}Q=T^{*}\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)\oplus\nu^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q))
. That is, for each stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
,

T^{*}Q|_{S}=\iota_{S}(T^{*}S)\oplus\nu^{*}(S).

Furthermore,

J^{-1}(0)|_{S}=J_{S}^{-1}(0)\oplus\nu^{*}(S)

Proof.

Follows from the definition of
\iota_{S}
and Lemma
9.35
.
∎

Now that we have these results in hand, we can state the following Theorem.

Theorem 9.37

(
[
PROSD07
]
)
.

For every orbit-type stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
there exists a unique orbit-type stratum
Z_{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
with the following properties.

(1)

If
\tau:T^{*}Q\to Q
is the bundle projection, then
\tau(Z_{S})=\overline{S}
. Furthermore,

\tau|_{Z_{S}}:Z_{S}\to\overline{S}

is an open
G
-invariant surjective map.

(2)

For each
S,R\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
with
S\leq R
, there exists an orbit-type stratum
C_{S}^{R}\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(\nu^{*}(S))
so that

Z_{R}|_{S}=J_{S}^{-1}(0)\oplus C_{S}^{R}

with
C_{R}^{R}\subseteq\nu^{*}(R)
being the image of the zero section.

(3)

The map

\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)\to\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0));\quad S\mapsto Z_{S}

is a bijection.

Corollary 9.38
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
be an orbit-type stratum of the base and
Z_{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
the corresponding stratum in Theorem
9.37
. Then
J_{S}^{-1}(0)\subseteq Z_{S}
as an open dense set.

Proof.

Since
S
is an open dense set in its closure
\overline{S}
, it follows from (1) that
Z_{S}|_{S}\subseteq Z_{S}
is an open dense set. From (2),
Z_{S}|_{S}=J_{S}^{-1}(0)\oplus\{0\}
. Hence the result.
∎

Now we would like to get a description of the reduced space. As we already know from Theorem
3.61
, given an orbit-type stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
, the reduced space
J_{S}^{-1}(0)/G
is canonically symplectomorphic to the cotangent bundle
T^{*}(S/G)
. Since
J_{S}^{-1}(0)\subseteq Z_{S}
as an open dense set, we deduce the following.

Lemma 9.39
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
be an orbit-type stratum and consider the canonical embedding
\phi:T^{*}(S/G)\hookrightarrow Z_{S}/G
induced by the canonical symplectomorphism
T^{*}(S/G)\cong J_{S}^{-1}(0)/G
. Then there is a
1
-form
\alpha_{S}\in\Omega^{1}(Z_{S})
satisfying the following.

(1)

(\pi_{0}|_{Z_{S}})^{*}\alpha_{S}=\theta_{Q}|_{Z_{S}}
.

(2)

d\alpha_{S}=-\omega_{Z_{S}/G}
where
\omega_{Z_{S}/G}\in\Omega^{2}(Z_{S}/G)
is the canonical symplectic form coming from singular reduction.

(3)

\phi^{*}\alpha_{S}=\theta_{S/G}
, where
\theta_{S/G}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}(S/G))
is the Liouville
1
-form.

Proof.

(1)

\theta_{Q}|_{Z_{S}}
is equivariant and vanishes on the
G
-orbits contained in
Z_{S}
. Hence, it immediately follows that
\theta_{Q}|_{Z_{S}}
is basic. Hence, there exists
\alpha_{S}\in\Omega^{1}(Z_{S}/G)
so that

(\pi_{0}|_{Z_{S}})^{*}\alpha_{S}=\theta_{Q}|_{Z_{S}}

(2)

Observe that
(\pi_{0}|_{Z_{S}})^{*}d\alpha_{S}=d\theta_{Q}|_{Z_{S}}=-\omega_{Q}|_{Z_{S}}
.
\omega_{Z_{S}/G}
is the unique
2
-form satisfying
(\pi_{0}|_{Z_{S}})^{*}\omega_{Z_{S}/G}=\omega_{Q}|_{Z_{S}}
hence it follows.

(3)

Write
\tau_{S}:T^{*}S\to S
for the bundle map and
\pi_{S}:S\to S/G
for the quotient. Recall that the symplectomorphism
\phi:T^{*}(S/G)\to J_{S}^{-1}(0)/G
was induced by the canonical map

\widetilde{\phi}:\pi_{S}^{-1}T^{*}(S/G)\to J_{S}^{-1}(0)

given by dualizing the derivative map
T\pi:TS\to\pi_{S}^{-1}T(S/G)
. Furthermore, recall from Lemma
3.60
that

\pi_{S}^{-1}T^{*}(S/G)\subseteq S\times T^{*}(S/G)

and that we showed
\widetilde{\phi}^{*}\theta_{S}=pr_{2}^{*}\theta_{S/G}
, where
\theta_{S}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}S)
is the Liouville
1
-form. Applying (1) then finishes the proof.

∎

Thus, for each stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
, the corresponding canonical stratum
Z_{S}/G\in\mathcal{S}_{G}((T^{*}Q)_{0})
contains the cotangent bundle
T^{*}(S/G)
as an open dense set. Unless
S
is a minimal stratum,
Z_{S}/G
will be strictly larger than the cotangent bundle of
S/G
, as the following result shows.

Theorem 9.40

(
[
PROSD07
]
)
.

Fix an orbit-type strata
S,R\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M)
satisfying
R<S
. Then
(Z_{S}|_{R})/G
is coisotropic in
Z_{S}/G
.

†
†
margin:

10

Singular Reduction of Prequantum Line Bundles

This chapter is dedicated to developing an elementary theory of stratified prequantum line bundles. Sniatycki
[
Śn13
]
laid much of the groundwork for this chapter, although my work will differ from his in imposing a smoothness condition of the prequantum connection.

41
Stratified Prequantum Line Bundles

We now define a prequantum line bundle over a symplectic stratified space. There is a natural definition that we could do as a generalization of prequantum line bundles of Poisson manifolds given by Vaisman
[
Vai91
]
. The only technical issue we unfortunately have to deal with is that we won’t be able to to use every vector field when defining the prequantum connection. So, we will have to restrict ourselves to using Hamiltonian modules.

Definition 10.1

(Stratified Prequantum Line Bundle)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
be a stratified symplectic space and
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}(X)
a Hamiltonian module. A
\mathcal{H}
prequantum line bundle consists of a triple
(\mathcal{L},\nabla,\{\nabla^{S}\}_{S\in\Sigma})
where

(i)

\mathcal{L}\to X
is a differentiable complex line bundle.

(ii)

\nabla:T\Sigma\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L})\to\mathcal{L}
is a bilinear mapping such that for any
v\in T_{x}\Sigma
,
f\in C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})
, and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
we have

\nabla_{v}(f\sigma)=v(f)\sigma(x)+f(x)\nabla_{v}\sigma

(iii)

\nabla^{S}
is a prequantum connection on
\mathcal{L}|_{S}\to S
for each stratum
S\in\Sigma

Satisfying

(1)

For any
V\in\mathcal{H}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
, the map

\nabla_{V}\sigma:X\to\mathcal{L};\quad\mapsto\nabla_{V_{x}}\sigma

is an element of
\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
.

(2)

For every
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
and
f,g\in C^{\infty}(X)
we have

[\nabla_{V_{f}},\nabla_{V_{g}}]\sigma-\nabla_{V_{\{f,g\}}}\sigma=-i\{f,g\}\sigma

(3)

If
S\in\Sigma
is a stratum,
V\in\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
, then

(\nabla_{V}\sigma)|_{S}=\nabla^{S}_{V|_{S}}(\sigma|_{S}).

Example 10.2
.

If
(M,\omega)
is a symplectic manifold and
\Sigma
a stratification of
M
by symplectic submanifolds, then a stratified prequantum line bundle over
(M,\Sigma,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is the same thing as a usual prequantum line bundle.

Example 10.3
.

Somewhere we could get potentially new and interesting is with Poisson manifolds where the symplectic foliation defines a stratification. Here the definition appears similar to that of a prequantum line bundle of a Poisson manifold as in
[
Vai91
]
, but there a prequantum line bundle need not quantize each symplectic leaf.

Example 10.4
.

Let

\pi_{can}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y}

be the canonical symplectic Poisson structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
\pi=r^{2}\pi_{can}
as in Example
3.27
. As we saw in Example
9.3
, the symplectic foliation
\mathscr{F}_{\pi}=\{\{0\},\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\}
is a symplectic stratification of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and in Example
9.24
the set of vector fields
\mathcal{H}
vanishing to order
2
at the origin defines a Hamiltonian module. Let us now use this module to define a prequantum line bundle.

Let
(r,\theta)
be polar coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=r\cos(\theta)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=r\sin(\theta)

On the stratum
S=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
, let
d\theta\in\Omega^{1}(S)
be the
1
-form defined by

d\theta=\frac{-ydx+xdy}{r^{2}}.

Observe that on
S
, we have

d\bigg(\frac{r^{2}}{2}d\theta\bigg))=rdr\wedge d\theta=dx\wedge dy=\omega_{can},

the canonical symplectic form on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In Example
3.27
we saw that the symplectic form
\omega_{S}
on
S
induced by
\pi
has the form

\omega_{S}=\frac{\omega_{can}}{r^{2}}=\frac{dr\wedge d\theta}{r}

Thus, we may define primitive
\alpha_{S}\in\Omega^{1}(S)
by

\alpha_{S}=\ln(r)d\theta.

With this set-up we can now define the prequantum line bundle.

Let
\mathcal{L}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}
and identify
\Gamma(\mathcal{L})=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{C})
. Define the map

\nabla:\mathcal{H}\times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{C}

by

\nabla_{v}f=\begin{cases}0,\quad&v\in T_{0}\mathscr{F}_{\pi}\\
v(f)+i\alpha(v)f(x),\quad&v\in T_{x}S\end{cases}

for
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{C})
. We can readily see that
\nabla
induces prequantum connections on each of the strata. The connection
\nabla
also pairs smoothly with elements of the Hamiltonian module. Indeed, if
V\in\mathcal{H}
, then by definition, there exists a neighbourhood
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the origin and a vector field
W\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
so that

V|_{U}=r^{2}W|_{U}.

Thus, over
U\setminus\{0\}
, we have

\nabla_{V}f=V(f)+i\alpha(V)f

Observe that

\alpha(V)=\alpha(r^{2}W)=r^{2}\ln(r)d\theta(W)=\ln(r)(-ydx(W)+xdy(W))

Writing

W=W_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+W_{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}

we then obtain that

\alpha(V)=\ln(r)(-yW_{x}+xW_{y})

Using that
x=r\cos(\theta)
and
y=r\sin(\theta)
, we can then show that

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\ln(r)(-yW_{x}+xW_{y})=0.

In particular, it follows that
\alpha(V)
can be smoothly extended to the origin by
0
. In particular,
\nabla_{V}f
can be smoothly extended to the origin by
0
. Hence,
\nabla_{V}f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{C})
.

Now just as with usual prequantum line bundles, we get a prequantum operator here as follows. Fix
f\in C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})
and define

\mathcal{Q}{pre}(f):\Gamma(\mathcal{L})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L});\quad\sigma\mapsto-i\nabla_{V_{f}}\sigma+f\sigma.

(100)

Proposition 10.5
.

The prequantum operator in Equation (
100
) satisfies the following properties.

(1)

\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(1_{X})=\text{id }_{\Gamma(\mathcal{L})}
, where
1_{X}\in C^{\infty}(X)
is the constant
1
function.

(2)

If
f,g\in C^{\infty}(X)
, then

i[\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(f),\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(g)]=\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(\{f,g\}).

Proof.

(1)

First, observe that
V_{1_{X}}=0
. Indeed, taking any
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
we have

\{1_{X},f\}=\{1_{X}\cdot 1_{X},f\}=2\{1_{x},f\}

which implies
\{1_{x},f\}=0
and hence
V_{1_{x}}=0
. Thus, since
\nabla_{V_{1_{x}}}
is defined point-wise, it follows that
\nabla_{V_{1_{x}}}=0
. Thus, for any section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
we have

\mathcal{Q}_{pre}(1_{X})\sigma=-i\nabla_{V_{1_{X}}}+1_{X}\sigma=\sigma.

(2)

Identical proof to Proposition
5.7
.

∎

A final property of stratified prequantum line bundles is that we can glue together the various prequantum connections on each stratum to get a differentiable map between the complexified stratified tangent bundle and the prequantum line bundle.

Proposition 10.6
.

For each stratum
S\in\Sigma
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
, we get a smooth map of complex vector bundles

\nabla^{S}\sigma:T^{\mathbb{C}}S\to\mathcal{L};\quad v\mapsto\nabla^{S}_{v}\sigma

These piece together to a (not necessarily differentiable) map of complex pseudo bundles

\nabla\sigma:T^{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma\to\mathcal{L}

(101)

defined by

\nabla\sigma|_{T^{\mathbb{C}}S}=\nabla^{S}\sigma.

Proof.

The first statement is simply Corollary
5.11
applied to each stratum.
∎

Remark 10.7
.

I suspect the map in Equation (
101
) actually is differentiable, but my attempts to prove it thus far have proven fruitless.

42
The Singular Reduction of Prequantum Line Bundles

We now show that if a prequantum line bundle is generated by its equivariant sections, then it descends to a stratified prequantum line bundle over the singular reduced space. Let us now fix a connected Lie group
G
, a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, and an equivariant prequantum line bundle
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)\to(M,\omega)
. Using ideas from Section
19
, we can produce singular versions of the prequantum line bundle reduction of Guillemin and Sternberg.

Proposition 10.8
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)
is equivariantly generated over
J^{-1}(0)
. That is, the map

J^{-1}(0)\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)};\quad(x,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(x)

is surjective. Then

(i)

\mathcal{L}_{0}:=(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/G
is naturally a differentiable complex line bundle over
J^{-1}(0)/G
.

(ii)

The projection
\pi_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)}\to\mathcal{L}_{0}
induces a bijection

(\pi_{\mathcal{L}})_{!}:\Gamma(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})^{G}\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0}).

(iii)

The map

\kappa:\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0});\quad\sigma\mapsto(\pi_{\mathcal{L}})_{!}(\sigma|_{J^{-1}(0)})

is a surjection.

Proof.

Each of these statements are just special cases of Theorem
8.21
.
∎

We will now attempt to use
\kappa
to define a prequantum connection on
\mathcal{L}_{0}
. Namely, given an equivariant section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
and a stratified tangent vector
v\in T\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
, the projection
(\pi_{0})_{*}v\in T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
is a stratified tangent vector and
\kappa(\sigma)\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
is a section of the reduced bundle. Hence, we would like to define

\nabla^{0}_{(\pi_{0})_{*}v}\kappa(\sigma):=\pi_{\mathcal{L}}(\nabla_{v}\sigma).

(102)

Working stratum-by-stratum and applying Theorem
5.22
from the non-singular case, we prove the following.

Lemma 10.9

(
[
Śn13
]
)
.

Equation (
102
) gives a well-defined map

\nabla^{0}:T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})\to\mathcal{L}_{0};\quad(v,\sigma)\mapsto\nabla^{0}_{v}\sigma.

Recall that we define the Hamiltonian module of the singular quantization
\mathcal{H}(M_{0})
to be the image of the map

\pi_{*}:\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}\to\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0}))

given by restricting vector fields to
J^{-1}(0)
, then point-wise differentiating. We would now like to show for any
V\in\mathcal{H}(M_{0})
and section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
, that the natural pairing
\nabla^{0}_{V}\sigma
is a smooth section of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
. First, we need a Lemma.

Lemma 10.10
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)
is equivariantly generated over
J^{-1}(0)
. Fix
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
and
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
. If

\pi_{*}(V_{1})=\pi_{*}(V_{2})\text{ and }\sigma_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\sigma_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)},

then

\nabla_{V_{1}}\sigma_{1}|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\nabla_{V_{2}}\sigma_{2}|_{J^{-1}(0)}.

Proof.

Utilizing a similar trick as in Lemma
5.19
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{V_{1}}\sigma_{1}-\nabla_{V_{2}}\sigma_{2}=\nabla_{V_{1}-V_{2}}\sigma_{1}+\nabla_{V_{2}}(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}).

Since
\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and
\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
are
C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
modules, it suffices to show

\nabla_{V}\sigma|_{J^{-1}(0)}=0

for all
V\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
whenever
\pi_{*}(V)=0
or
\sigma|_{J^{-1}(0)}=0
. Working stratum-by-stratum, this reduces to the proof Theorem of part (i) of
5.24
.
∎

Corollary 10.11
.

Given any vector fields
V\in\mathcal{H}(M_{0})
and any section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
, the pairing
\nabla^{0}_{V}\sigma
is a smooth section. More specifically, if
W\in\mathfrak{X}(M,J^{-1}(0))^{G}
and
\tau\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
satisfy
\pi_{*}W=V
and
\kappa(\tau)=\sigma
, then

\nabla^{0}_{V}\sigma=\kappa(\nabla_{W}\tau).

Proof.

Fixing
V,W,\sigma,\tau
as in the statement, let
x\in J^{-1}(0)
and
[x]\in M_{0}
its image under the quotient map. Then, by Lemma
10.9

\nabla^{0}_{V_{[x]}}\sigma=\pi_{\mathcal{L}}(\nabla_{W_{x}}\tau)

By definition,
\kappa(\nabla_{W}\tau)([x])=\pi_{\mathcal{L}}(\nabla_{W_{x}}\tau)

∎

Corollary 10.12
.

Given any two functions
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M_{0})
and any section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
we have

[\nabla^{0}_{V_{f}},\nabla^{0}_{V_{g}}]\sigma-\nabla^{0}_{V_{\{f,g\}_{0}}}=-i\{f,g\}_{0}\sigma.

Proof.

Let
F,G\in C^{\infty}(M)^{G}
be lifts of
f
and
g
and
\tau\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})^{G}
be a lift of
\sigma
. Note that
\pi_{*}V_{F}=V_{f}
and
\pi_{*}V_{G}=V_{g}
. Hence, successive applications of Corollary
10.11
yields

\nabla^{0}_{V_{f}}(\nabla^{0}_{V_{g}}\sigma)=\nabla^{0}_{V_{f}}\kappa(\nabla_{V_{G}}\tau)=\kappa(\nabla_{V_{F}}\nabla_{V_{G}}\tau)

Thus,

\displaystyle[\nabla^{0}_{V_{f}},\nabla^{0}_{V_{g}}]\sigma-\nabla^{0}_{V_{\{f,g\}_{0}}}

\displaystyle=\kappa([\nabla_{V_{F}},\nabla_{V_{G}}]\tau-\kappa(\nabla_{V_{\{F,G\}}}\tau)

\displaystyle=\kappa(-i\{F,G\}\tau)

Note for any
x\in J^{-1}(0)
, if
[x]\in M_{0}
is its image under the quotient map
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
, then by the fibre-wise linearity of
\pi_{\mathcal{L}}
and the definition of the Poisson bracekt on
M_{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\kappa(-i\{F,G\}\tau)([x])

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{L}}(-i\{F,G\}(x)\tau(x))

\displaystyle=-i\{F|_{J^{-1}(0)},G|_{J^{-1}(0)}\}_{J^{-1}(0)}(x)\pi_{\mathcal{L}}(\tau(x))

\displaystyle=-i\{f,g\}_{0}\sigma(x)

Hence the result.
∎

Now we need to establish the stratum-wise prequantum line bundles.

Proposition 10.13
.

Let
K\leq G
be a compact subgroup and let
M_{K}
denote the manifold of symmetry. Let
N_{G}(K)\leq G
for the normalizer subgroup of
K
,
\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)=\text{Lie}(N_{G}(K))
for its Lie algebra, and write

J_{K}:M_{K}\to\mathfrak{n}_{G}(K)^{*}

for the induced Hamiltonian free Hamiltonian
N_{G}(K)
-space structure. Then
\mathcal{L}|_{M_{K}}\to M_{K}
is canonically an equivariantly generated
N_{G}(K)
-equivariant prequantum line bundle over
J_{K}^{-1}(0)
.

Proof.

Since
M_{K}
is a symplectic submanifold, the restriction
\mathcal{L}|_{M_{K}}\to M_{K}
is automatically a prequantum line bundle. Since
\mathcal{L}
is
G
-equivariant, it automatically follows that
\mathcal{L}|_{M_{K}}
is
N_{G}(K)
equivariant. Finally, to show
\mathcal{L}|_{M_{K}}
is equivariantly generated over
J_{K}^{-1}(0)
, let
x\in J)K^{-1}(0)
. Then,

(\mathcal{L}|_{M_{k}})_{x}^{N_{G}(K)_{x}}=\mathcal{L}_{x}^{N_{G}(K)_{x}}=\mathcal{L}_{x}^{K}=\mathcal{L}_{x}.

Hence the result.
∎

Corollary 10.14
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)
is equivariantly generated over
J^{-1}(0)
and
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
be an orbit-type stratum, then
(\mathcal{L}|_{S})/G\to S/G
is canonically a prequantum line bundle over
S/G
with prequantum connection
\nabla^{S}
satisfying

\pi_{0}^{-1}\nabla^{S}=\nabla|_{S}.

Proof.

By Proposition
10.13
,
\mathcal{L}_{0}=(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/G
is a differentiable complex line bundle over
J^{-1}(0)
. In particular, this implies the restriction to
S/G
,
\mathcal{L}_{0}|_{S/G}=(\mathcal{L}|_{S})/G
is a smooth complex line bundle over
S/G
. Using nearly the exact same proof as in Theorem
5.24
part (ii), we can easily show that
\nabla|_{S}
induces a connection
\nabla^{S}
on
\mathcal{L}_{0}
which satisfies

\pi_{0}^{-1}\nabla^{S}=\nabla|_{S}.

To show
\nabla^{S}
is prequantum, fix
x\in S
and write
K=G_{x}
. Then by Theorem
9.15
, the map

J_{K}^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow S

induces a symplectomorphism

(M_{K})_{0}=J_{K}^{-1}(0)/N_{G}(K)\to S/G.

(103)

By Proposition
10.13
,
\mathcal{L}|_{M_{K}}\to M_{K}
is equivariantly generated over
J_{K}^{-1}(0)
and hence by Theorem
5.24
,

(\mathcal{L}|_{M_{K}})_{0}:=(\mathcal{L}|_{J_{K}^{-1}(0)})/N_{G}(K)

is canonically a complex line bundle over
(M_{K})_{0}
with prequantum connection
\nabla^{K}
satisfying

\pi_{K}^{-1}\nabla^{K}=\nabla|_{J_{K}^{-1}(0)},

where
\pi_{K}:J_{K}^{-1}(0)\to(M_{K})_{0}
is the quotient map. It’s then a straightforward matter of chasing diagrams to show the map of line bundles

\mathcal{L}|_{J_{K}^{-1}(0)}\to\mathcal{L}|_{S}

induces an isomorphism of line bundles

(\mathcal{L}|_{M_{K}})_{0}\to\mathcal{L}_{0}|_{S/G}

and that the pullback of
\nabla^{S}
under this map is the same as
\nabla^{K}
. The result then follows.
∎

Theorem 10.15
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)
is an equivariantly generated prequantum line bundle over
J^{-1}(0)
and let

\mathcal{L}_{0}:=(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/G.

Then there exists a canonical
\mathcal{H}_{G}(M_{0})
-connection
\nabla^{0}
and prequantum connections
\nabla^{S}
on each
\mathcal{L}|_{S}\to S
,
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
, such that
(\mathcal{L}_{0},\nabla^{0},\{\nabla^{S}\}_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})})
is a stratified prequantum line bundle over
M_{0}
.

Proof.

Apply Theorem
9.31
and Corollary
10.14
.
∎

Example 10.16
.

Just like the non-singular case, the reduction of prequantum line bundles over cotangent bundles is particularly clear. Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
Q
a proper
G
-space, and
J:T^{*}Q\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the Hamiltonian lift. Writing
\theta_{Q}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}Q)
for the Liouville
1
-form, the trivial bundle
\mathcal{L}=T^{*}Q\times\mathbb{C}
together with the connection
\nabla=L-i\theta_{Q}
is an equivariant prequantum line bundle over
T^{*}Q
. Since
G
only acts on the first factor of
\mathcal{L}
, it trivially follows that
\mathcal{L}
is singularly reducible. Indeed, we have

\mathcal{L}_{0}=(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/G=(T^{*}Q)_{0}\times\mathbb{C}.

Now, recall from Theorem
9.37
that if
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
is an orbit-type stratum of
Q
then there is a unique orbit-type stratum
Z_{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
which contains
J_{S}^{-1}(0)
as an open dense set, where
J_{S}:T^{*}S\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is the Hamiltonian lift of the
G
action on
S
. Furthermore, we recall that the quotient
Z_{S}/G
has a canonical
1
-form
\alpha_{S}\in\Omega^{1}(Z_{S}/G)
so that
d\alpha_{S}=-\omega_{S}
, where
\omega_{S}\in\Omega^{2}(Z_{S}/G)
is the reduced symplectic form and so that

(\pi_{0}|_{Z_{S}})^{*}\alpha_{S}=\theta_{Q}|_{Z_{S}}

for the reduction map
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}
. It then follows immediately that the reduced prequantum connection
\nabla^{0}
on
\mathcal{L}_{0}
restricts to the prequantum connection

\nabla^{0}=L-i\alpha_{S}

on
Z_{S}/G
.

As a final remark, I would like to speculate on future work. Let
\mathfrak{g}_{J^{-1}(0)}\subseteq T\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
be the subbundle of the stratified tangent bundle with fibres given by tangent spaces to orbits. It’s straightforward to show that
\pi_{*}:T\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))\to T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
induces a differentiable equivariant map between pseudo bundles

\pi_{*}:T\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))/\mathfrak{g}_{J^{-1}(0)}\to T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})

which is an isomorphism on each fibre. Taking by a quotient by
G
, we get a map

\pi_{*}:(T\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))/\mathfrak{g}_{J^{-1}(0)})/G\to T\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0}).

(104)

I conjecture that this map is in fact an isomorphism. If this is the case, then we see that we can extend
\nabla^{0}
to all stratified vector fields.

Corollary 10.17
.

Suppose the map in Equation (
104
) is an isomorphism of pseudo bundles. Then for each
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})
, then map

\nabla\sigma:T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})\to\mathcal{L}_{0}

as in Proposition
10.6
is a differentiable map of complex pseudo bundles. Hence,
\nabla
can be extended to a full connection

\nabla:\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0}))\times\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0}).

43
Singular Reduction of Prequantum Line Bundles over Toric Manifolds

Recall the construction of a prequantum line bundle on a toric manifold from Section
20.2
. Let
\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Delzant polytope defined by

\Delta=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ \langle x,v_{i}\rangle\geq\lambda_{i}\},

where
v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are the vertices and let

J:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N};\quad(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(|z_{1}|^{2},\dots,|z_{n}|^{2})-\lambda,

where
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})
. As we saw,
\mathbb{C}^{N}
together with
J
and the canonical
T^{N}
action is a Hamiltonian
T^{N}
-space. Further recall that if we let
\mathfrak{k}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
be the kernel of the linear map

\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{n};\quad e_{i}\mapsto v_{i},

(105)

\iota:\mathfrak{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
the inclusion, and
K=\exp(\mathfrak{k})\subseteq T^{N}
, then
M_{\Delta}=(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)/K
is canonically a Hamiltonian
T^{N}
space with an induced effective
T^{n}
action via the homomorphism
T^{N}\to T^{n}
induced by Equation (
105
). For our own convenience, we will be viewing
M_{\Delta}
as a Hamiltonian
T^{N}
space.

As for the prequantum line bundle, recall that
\mathbb{C}^{N}
has a canonical
T^{N}
equivariant prequantum line bundle
(\mathcal{L}_{can},\nabla^{can})\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
, where

\mathcal{L}_{can}=\mathbb{C}^{N}\times\mathbb{C}

and

\nabla^{can}=d+i\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{r_{j}^{2}}{2}d\theta_{j}

where
(r_{j},\theta_{j})
are polar coordinates. As we saw, if both
\lambda
and
\iota^{*}(\lambda)
are integral, then we can equip
\mathcal{L}_{can}
with a
T^{N}
action via the homomorphism

\rho:T^{N}\to S^{1};\quad(e^{i\theta_{1}},\dots,e^{i\theta_{N}})\mapsto e^{-i\langle\lambda,(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{N})\rangle}

(106)

With this action,
(\mathcal{L}_{can},\nabla^{can})
is reducible over
J^{-1}(0)
and hence induces a prequantum line bundle

\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}:=(\mathcal{L}_{can}|_{(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)})/K

over
M_{\Delta}
with prequantum connection
\nabla^{\Delta}
. This is a
T^{N}
-equivariant (and hence
T^{n}
-equivariant) prequantum line bundle. Similar to our discussion of
M_{\Delta}
, we will be viewing
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
as a
T^{N}
-equivariant prequantum line bundle for reasons of convenience.

Given that
(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta},\nabla^{\Delta})\to M_{\Delta}
is
T^{N}
-equivariant, it automatically follows that it is also
H
-equivariant for any subgroup
H\leq T^{N}
. That is, if we fix
H\leq T^{N}
and let

J_{\Delta,H}:M_{\Delta}\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}

(107)

be the induced momentum map, then
(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta},\nabla^{\Delta})\to M_{\Delta}
is an
H
-equivariant prequantum line bundle. We would like to now explore for which subgroups
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
is equivariantly generated over
J_{\Delta,H}^{-1}(0)
.

Theorem 10.18
.

Let
H\leq T^{N}
be a subtorus and write
\mathfrak{h}=\text{Lie}(H)
. Further, write

J_{\Delta,H}:M_{\Delta}\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}

for the induced momentum map. If
H\leq\ker(\rho)
, where
\rho:T^{N}\to S^{1}
is the homomorphism if Equation (
106
), then
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}\to M_{\Delta}
is singularly reducible over
