J_{\Delta,H}^{-1}(0)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)
and
[x]\in M_{\Delta}
its image under the quotient map
(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)\to M_{\Delta}
. We show that

(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})_{[x]}^{H_{[x]}}=(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})_{[x]}

To show this, let

\pi_{\mathcal{L}}:(\iota\circ J)^{-1}(0)\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}

be the quotient map. Then I claim for any
x\in(\iota\circ J)^{-1}(0)
that

\pi_{\mathcal{L}}(\{x\}\times\mathbb{C}^{(KH)_{x}})=(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})_{[x]}^{H_{[x]}}.

This is an immediate consequence of the fact that
(KH)_{x}=H_{[x]}
and the equivariance of
\pi_{\mathcal{L}}
. Furthermore, since
H
acts trivially on
\mathbb{C}
and
K
acts freely on
(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)
, we conclude the desired result.
∎

Note that although
H
acts “trivially” on
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
it need not act trivially on
M_{\Delta}
. Furthermore, there are no guarantees that it will have nice stabilizers, hence the reduced space
J_{\Delta,H}^{-1}(0)/H
will be a stratified symplectic space in general.

†
†
margin:

11

Singular Reduction of Polarizations

Now that we have explored the singular reduction of the prequantum line bundle, we now turn to the much more subtle matter of the polarization. As we will see, it is much easier to create examples where the prequantum line bundle can be reduced as in Theorem
10.15
than to find examples of reducible polarizations. I think this points to a potential for a more generalized (and presumably much more ill-behaved) generalization of the notion of a polarization which could be well-suited to things like cotangent bundles.

44
Stratified Polarizations

To begin with, we give a deceptively simple definition of a stratified polarization. I say deceptively simple because it can be quite the struggle to verify.

Definition 11.1

(Stratified Polarization)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
be a symplectic stratified space and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma
a complex stratified distribution on
X
. Call
\mathcal{P}
a stratified polarization if
\mathcal{P}|_{S}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}S
is a polarization for each
S\in\Sigma
.

Given a polarization
\mathcal{P}
, define the polarized functions by

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(X):=\{f\in C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})\ |\ v(f)=0\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{P}\}.

We can see this is the most obvious generalization of Definition
4.11
. The assumption that
\mathcal{P}
be
stratified
is actually quite difficult to verify in examples (and very often does not hold). In particular, it can be highly non-obvious if the frontier condition is met. Nonetheless, when it does hold, we get some powerful conclusions.

Proposition 11.2
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a stratified polarization on a stratified symplectic space
(X,\Sigma,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
.

(i)

\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
is involutive. That is, if
V,W\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
, then
[X,Y]\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
.

(ii)

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(X)
is a Poisson subalgebra of
C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

(1)

Let
V,W\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
. Since
\Gamma(\mathcal{P})\subseteq\mathfrak{X}^{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)
and
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)
is involutive, it follows that
[V,W]\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)
. In particular, for all
S\in\Sigma
, we have

[V,W]|_{S}=[V|_{S},W|_{S}].

Since we are assuming
V|_{S},W|_{S}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P}|_{S})
and
\mathcal{P}|_{S}
is involutive, it follows that
[V|_{S},W|_{S}]\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P}|_{S})
. Since
S
was arbitrary, we conclude that
[V,W]\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P})
.

(2)

Let
f,g\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(X)
,
x\in X
,
S\in\Sigma
the unique stratum containing
x
, and
v\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
. We show
v(\{f,g\})=0
. Indeed, observe that since
v\in T^{\mathbb{C}}S
and the inclusion
S\hookrightarrow X
is Poisson, we get

v(\{f,g\})=v(\{f,g\}|_{S})=v(\{f|_{S},g|_{S}\}).

Using the fact that
v\in T^{\mathbb{C}}S
once again, we have
v(f)=v(f|_{S})
and
v(g)=v(g|_{S})
. Since we are assuming
f,g\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(X)
, it follows that
f|_{S},g|_{S}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}|_{S}}(S)
and hence by Proposition
4.15
,
\{f|_{S},g|_{S}\}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}|_{S}}(S)
. Hence,

v(\{f,g\})=v(\{f,g\}|_{S})=v(\{f|_{S},g|_{S}\})=0.

Since
x
,
S
, and
v
were arbitrary, we conclude that
\{f,g\}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(X)
.

∎

Example 11.3
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold equipped with its canonical stratification
\Sigma
by connected components. If
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
is any polarization, then it automatically is a stratified polarization.

Example 11.4
.

For me, the most archetypal stratified polarizations arise from toric geometry. Let
\mathbb{T}
be a torus with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
and suppose

J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}

is a toric manifold in the sense of Definition
3.53
. Since
\mathbb{T}
is abelian, the orbit-type stratification
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{T}}(M)
is a stratification by symplectic submanifolds. As we have discussed in Example
9.4
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{T}}(M)
is not a symplectic stratification since the Hamiltonian vector fields are not tangent to the strata. Nevertheless, we can illustrate the principles of a stratified polarization beautifully in this setting.

First, a real stratified polarization. Let
\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{T}}
denote the singular foliation of
M
given by the
\mathbb{T}
-orbits and define

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}:=T\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{T}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{T}}(M).

Since
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the complexification of the tangent bundle of a singular foliation, clearly it is involutive. To show now that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
restricts to a polarization on each stratum, fix an orbit-type stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{T}(M)
and let
x\in S
. Then the quotient group
N=\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{T}_{x}
acts freely on
S
and using the Delzant construction, we can easily see that
\dim(S)=2\dim(\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{T}_{x})
. Hence, by Example
4.14
, the quotient map

\pi_{S}:S\to S/N

is a Lagrangian fibration. In particular,
\ker(T\pi_{S})\otimes\mathbb{C}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}S
is a polarization and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{S}=\ker(T\pi_{S})\otimes\mathbb{C}
. Hence, modulo
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{T}}(M)
not exactly being a stratified symplectic space,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
is a stratified polarization. Finally for the polarized functions, observe that

C^{\infty}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)=C^{\infty}(M)^{\mathfrak{t}}

the
\mathfrak{t}
-invariant functions. Since the action of
T
on
M
is symplectic, it follows that
C^{\infty}(M)^{\mathfrak{t}}
is a Poisson subalgebra. Note that since the leaves of the
\mathbb{T}
-action are Lagrangian on an open dense set, it’s straightforward to see that
C^{\infty}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
has a trivial Poisson bracket.

Thus, we have now the archetypal example of a real stratified polarization. Toric geometry also provides for us a natural family of stratified Kähler polarizations as well. Let
J
be a
\mathbb{T}
-invariant compatible complex structure on
M
. These exist due to the Delzant construction of
(M,\omega)
from its moment polytope. Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be the induced Riemannian metric. Pulling back the metric to any of the strata, we get another compatible metric and hence another Kähler structure.

Thus, for each
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{T}}(M)
let
J_{S}
be the induced compatible
\mathbb{T}
-invariant complex structure and let
T^{1,0}_{J_{S}}S
denote the holomorphic tangent bundle with respect to
J_{S}
. We define

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}:=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{T}(M)}T^{1,0}_{J_{S}}S

As we already saw in Example
8.44
, we can also realize

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigcup_{x\in M}((T^{1,0}_{J})_{x}M)^{\mathbb{T}_{x}}

which as we saw is a stratified complex distribution. From the description of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}
the union of holomorphic tangent bundles,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}
is eminently a stratified polarization. Note that if
f\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}}(M)
is a complex polarized function, then
f
is antiholomorphic over an open dense set. This implies that
f
must be globally antiholomorphic. Furthermore, since the inclusions of the strata
S\hookrightarrow M
are antiholomorphic, we conclude that this must be all the polarized functions. That is, the
\mathcal{P}
polarized functions coincide with the antiholomorphic functions on
M
. This is a Poisson subalgebra.

Example 11.5
.

We can somewhat massage the above example to get a true example of stratified polarizations. In particular, let us return to our test example of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
along with the Poisson structure

\pi=r^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y}.

For a real stratified polarization, let

\partial_{\theta}:=-y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+x\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Letting
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the image of the map

\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}\to T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{R}^{2};\quad(x,\lambda)\mapsto\lambda\partial_{\theta}(x)

then
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
is a stratified polarization. This of course is the complexification of the tangent bundle of the foliation induced by the canonical
S^{1}
action on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, hence is involutive.
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
vanishes at
0
and is rank
1
elsewhere, hence is Lagrangian. For complex, let
I_{can}
be the canonical complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Letting

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}=\widetilde{T^{0,1}_{I_{can}}\mathbb{R}^{2}}

with respect to the
S^{1}
-action, we see that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}
is also a polarization.

Recall that symplectic stratified spaces
(X,\Sigma,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
are assumed to be Whitney regular. In particular, they are assumed to satisfy the Whitney A condition. This condition allows us to show the following.

Proposition 11.6
.

Let
(X,\Sigma,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
be a symplectic stratified space,
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma
a stratified polarization, and
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
. If
f|_{S}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}|_{S}}(S)
for all maximal strata
S\in\Sigma
, then
f\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}(M)
.

Proof.

Fix
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
which is polarized on the maximal strata and let
x\in X
and
v\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
. We show
v(f)=0
.

Writing
R\in\Sigma
for the stratum containing
x
, we can find a maximal stratum
S\in\Sigma
with
R\subseteq\overline{S}
. Since
\mathcal{P}
is assumed to be stratified, we have
\mathcal{P}|_{R}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}|_{S}}
. Hence, we can find a sequence
\{v_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}|_{S}
converging to
v
. Since
S
is maximal, we have

v_{k}(f)=0

for all
k
. Thus, taking a limit we conclude

v(f)=\lim_{k\to\infty}v_{k}(f)=\lim_{k\to\infty}0=0.

Hence,
f
is polarized.
∎

45
The Singular Reduction of Polarizations

We are now finally ready to discuss the singular reduction of polarizations. In order to fix out notation, let
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a proper Hamiltonian
G
-space for connected Lie group
G
, and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
a
G
-invariant polarization. We will write
M_{0}=J^{-1}(0)/G
for the reduced space and
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to M_{0}
for the quotient map. The rest of the following subsection will be devoted to proving the following result.

Theorem 11.7
.

Suppose the subbundle

\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}:=\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))

is a stratified complex distribution over
J^{-1}(0)
, then

\mathcal{P}_{0}:=T\pi_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)})\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})

is a stratified polarization over
M_{0}
.

The main ingredient for the proof is to show the following.

Lemma 11.8
.

In the setup of Theorem
11.7
, for each
x\in J^{-1}(0)
and stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
, we have

T\pi(\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}S)\subset T_{[x]}^{\mathbb{C}}(S/G)

is a complex Lagrangian subspace.

Proof.

Fix
x\in J^{-1}(0)
and stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
containing
x
. Let
K=G_{x}
and write
V=S\nu_{x}(M,G)
for the symplectic normal space, we have by the local normal form in Theorem
9.15
a
K
-equivariant linear symplectomorphism

T_{x}M\cong(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})\oplus(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})^{*}\oplus V.

Write
W=(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})\oplus(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{x}
can be identified with a
K
-invariant complex Lagrangian subspace
L\subseteq W_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus V_{\mathbb{C}}
. Writing
F=(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k})\oplus\{0\}\subseteq W
, we can also identify

T_{x}S=F\oplus V^{K}

and
T_{[x]}(S/G)
with the reduction of
W\oplus V
along
F\oplus V^{K}
. Thus, we are in the situation of Theorem
4.10
. Hence, the image of
L
in the reduction is a complex Lagrangian subspace. This image precisely corresponds to
T\pi(\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}S)
.
∎

Given Lemma
11.8
, we can immediately prove Theorem
11.7
.

Proof.

Of Theorem
11.7

Fix a stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
. Then

\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}|_{S}=\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}S

is a complex involutive distribution on
S
. Since
\mathcal{P}
is
G
-invariant and using Lemma
11.8
, we conclude that the map

T\pi:\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}|_{S}\to T^{\mathbb{C}}(S/G)

is constant rank and constant over the fibres of
\pi:S\to S/G
. Hence, by Lemma
4.29
,

\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S}=T\pi(\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}|_{S})\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}(S/G)

is an involutive complex distribution. Once again, by Lemma
11.8
, the fibres of
\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S/G}
are complex Lagrangian subspace, hence
\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S/G}
is a polarization.

Now to show
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is a complex stratified distribution. We already have

\mathcal{P}_{0}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))

and that
\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}S
is a smooth complex distribution. Since
\mathcal{P}_{0}
inherits its differentiable structure and stratum-wise vector bundle structure from
T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
, we just need to show that the canonical decomposition of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
:

\mathcal{P}_{0}=\bigsqcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})}\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S}

is a stratification. To that end, suppose
S,R\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
satisfy

\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S/G}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{R/G}}\neq\emptyset

By continuity of the projection from
\mathcal{P}_{0}
onto
M_{0}
, we have
(S/G)\cap\overline{R/G}\neq\emptyset
and hence
S/G\subseteq\overline{R/G}
. Consequently,
S\subseteq\overline{R}
as well.

Now, fix
x\in S
and
v\in(\mathcal{P}_{0})_{[x]}
. We can find a lift
w\in\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}S
. By assumption,
\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}
is a stratified distribution, hence we can find a sequence
\{w_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}|_{R}
which converge to
w
. Applying the projection
\pi
and setting
v_{k}=\pi(w_{k})
, we have a sequence
\{v_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{R}
converging to
v
and thus
\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{R}}
.
∎

Motivated by our previous discussion in non-singular reduction and the sole condition applied to the polarization to ensure the reduction was a polarization again, we give the following definition.

Definition 11.9

(Singular Reducibility)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a
G
-invariant polarization. Say
\mathcal{P}
is singularly reducible if

\mathcal{P}_{J^{-1}(0)}=\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))

is a complex stratified distribution of
J^{-1}(0)
.

Example 11.10
.

As we shall go over below, we can obtain plenty of interesting examples of singular reducible polarizations by examining equivariant Kähler structures. Unfortunately, we shall see our first example now of a very natural equivariant polarization, that is not singularly reducible. Indeed, recall the Hamiltonian lift of the canonical
S^{1}
-action on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
J:T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
from Example
9.32
. In this case,
J^{-1}(0)
has two strata:

J^{-1}(0)=\{(0,0)\}\sqcup S

and
S
can be further equivariantly decomposed

S=(\{0\}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}))\sqcup R.

(108)

Since
J(x,x)=0
, it’s straightforward to see that

R=\{(x,tx)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ x\neq 0\text{ and }tin\mathbb{R}\}.

The projection onto the first factor

pr_{1}:R\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}

clearly makes
R
into a free
S^{1}
-equivariant vector bundle and hence the quotient
R/S^{1}\to(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\})/S^{1}
is a smooth rank
1
-vector bundle. Via our discussion in Section
40
, we can see that
R/S^{1}\cong T^{*}(0,\infty)
via the map

(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\})/S^{1}\to(0,\infty);\quad[x]\mapsto\|x\|^{2}.

Observe that
0
is a regular value of
J
restricted to
\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
and so using this we can deduce that

T_{(x,y)}S=\{(v,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ J(x,w)-J(y,v)=0\}

for any
(x,y)\in S
. Using this description together with the fact that if
(x,y)\in S
with
x\neq 0
then
y
is a multiple of
x
, we obtain

T_{(x,y)}S=\begin{cases}\{(\lambda y,w)\ |\ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\},\quad x=0\\
\{(v,tv+\lambda x)\ |\ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\},\quad y=tx\text{ for some }t\in\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

Now let us see how the canonical polarization
\mathcal{P}
intersects the complexification of the tangent bundle of
S
.

Writing
pr_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
for the projection onto the first factor, the canonical real polarization in this case is

\mathcal{P}=\ker(Tpr_{1})\otimes\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}.

Using the description of the tangent space to
S
, we then obtain

\mathcal{P}_{(x,y)}\cap T_{(x,y)}^{\mathbb{C}}S=\begin{cases}\{0\}\times\mathbb{C}^{2},\quad x=0\\
\{0\}\times\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}(x),\quad\text{else}.\end{cases}

Thus,
\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}S
does not have constant rank and hence
\mathcal{P}
is not singularly reducible. Nevertheless, notice something interesting here. If I let
\pi_{S}:S\to S/S^{1}
denote the quotient map, then for any
(x,y)\in S
we have

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}T_{(x,y)}\pi_{S}(\mathcal{P}_{(x,y)}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{(x,y)}S)=1

How could this be? Over the open subset
R\subseteq S
where
x\neq 0
, this is clear as the orbits of the
S^{1}
action intersect the cotangent fibres trivially. Over
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, it’s easy to see that

T^{\mathbb{C}}_{(0,y)}S^{1}\cdot(0,y)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{(0,y)}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{(0,y)}S

and hence

T_{(0,y)}\pi_{S}(\mathcal{P}_{(0,y)}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{(0,y)}S\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}/T^{\mathbb{C}}_{y}(S^{1}\cdot y)

which is clearly
1
-dimensional. As we shall see shortly,
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}R)
is actually a polarization and hence if
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}S)
is smooth, it also is a polarization over
S/S^{1}
.

46
Preservation of Type Under Singular Reduction

Proposition 11.11
.

Let
K\leq G
be a compact subgroup and let

J_{K}:M_{K}\to(\mathfrak{n}_{K}(G)/\mathfrak{k})^{*}

be the canonical momentum map on the manifold of symmetry
M_{K}
. Define

\mathcal{P}_{K}:=\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}M_{K}.

(109)

Then,

(1)

\mathcal{P}_{K}
is an
N_{G}(K)/K
-invariant polarization on
M_{K}
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{P}
is real, then so is
\mathcal{P}_{K}
.

(3)

If
\mathcal{P}
is totally complex, then so is
\mathcal{P}_{K}
.

(4)

If
\mathcal{P}
is singularly reducible, then
\mathcal{P}_{K}
is reducible.

Proof.

Let
x\in J_{K}^{-1}(0)
and let
S\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
be the orbit-type stratum containing
x
.

(\mathcal{P}_{K})_{x}=\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}M_{K}=\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap(T_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}M)^{K}=\mathcal{P}_{x}^{K}.

Hence,
\mathcal{P}_{K}
is a Lagrangian complex distribution on
M_{K}
. Since both
\mathcal{P}
and
T^{\mathbb{C}}M_{K}
are involutive, it follows that
\mathcal{P}_{K}
is also involutive, hence a polarization.
\mathcal{P}_{K}
is invariant under the
N_{G}(K)/K
action because
\mathcal{P}
is invariant. It is also reducible since

(\mathcal{P}_{K})_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}J_{K}^{-1}(0)=(\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}S)^{K}.

The statements about type being preserved for real or totally complex polarizations follows from the identification
(\mathcal{P}_{K})_{x}=\mathcal{P}^{K}_{x}
for each
x\in M_{K}
and from the fact that complex conjugation preserves fixed point sets.
∎

Proposition 11.12
.

Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is a singularly reducible polarization,
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
an orbit-type stratum,
x\in S
, and let
K=G_{x}
. Then, under the canonical symplectomorphism

S/G\cong J_{K}^{-1}(0)/(N_{G}(K)/K)=:(M_{K})_{0}

the polarization
\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S/G}
gets mapped to
(\mathcal{P}_{K})_{0}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}(M_{K})_{0}
.

Proof.

This makes use of the local form.
∎

Corollary 11.13
.

Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is singularly reducible and let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(M_{0})
be a stratum of the singular reduced space.

(1)

If
\mathcal{P}
is real, then
\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S}
is real.

(2)

If
\mathcal{P}
is totally complex, then
\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S}
is totally complex.

(3)

If
\mathcal{P}=T^{0,1}_{J}M
for some compatible
G
-invariant complex structure
J
on
M
, then there exists compatible complex structure
J_{S}
on
S
so that
\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{S}=T^{0,1}_{J_{S}}S
.

Proof.

Each statement is a simply a stratum-by-stratum statement of Proposition
11.12
.
∎

47
When Polarizations Can Be Reduced

Proposition 11.14
.

Suppose
\mathcal{P}
satisfies the property that for any strata
S,R\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
with
S\subseteq\overline{R}
and any
(x,y)\in S\times R
we have

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}S)\leq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{P}_{y}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{y}R).

Then
\mathcal{P}
is singularly reducible if and only if
\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}S
is a complex distribution of
S
for all
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{P}
is fibre-wise Lagrangian, it automatically follows that
\mathcal{P}
is closed as a subset of
T^{\mathbb{C}}M
. Hence, a chart-wise application of Lemma
8.49
completes the proof.
∎

We will finish now with a discussion on when an invariant polarization is singularly reducible.

Lemma 11.15
.

Fix
x\in J^{-1}(0)
and let
K=G_{x}
. Letting
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
be the orbit-type stratum containing
x
. Then,

\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}S=(\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}S)^{K}+\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}(G\cdot x).

(110)

Proof.

Clearly, since both
S
and
\mathcal{P}
are closed under the
G
-action, we have

(\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}S)^{K}+\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}(G\cdot x)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{x}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}S.

For the reverse containment, from the local normal form we have a
K
-equivariant linear symplectomorphism

T_{x}M\cong V\oplus W,

(111)

where
V=\mathfrak{k}\times\mathfrak{k}^{*}
and
W=S\nu_{x}(M,G)
with their canonical symplectic forms
\omega_{V}
and
\omega_{W}
, respectively. Under this symplectomorphism,
T_{x}(G\cdot x)
gets mapped to
F\oplus 0
, where
F=\mathfrak{k}\times\{0\}\subseteq V
, and
T_{x}S
gets mapped to
F\oplus W^{K}
.
Let
L\subseteq V^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus W^{\mathbb{C}}
denote the
K
-invariant Lagrangian subspace
\mathcal{P}_{x}
gets mapped to. Since both
C
and
L
are
K
-invariant, we automatically have

L\cap C^{\mathbb{C}}=(L\cap C^{\mathbb{C}})^{K}+L\cap C^{\mathbb{C}}\cap((V^{\mathbb{C}})^{K}\oplus(W^{\mathbb{C}})^{K})^{\omega}.

It’s easy to see that

C^{\mathbb{C}}\cap((V^{\mathbb{C}})^{K}\oplus(W^{\mathbb{C}})^{K})^{\omega}=(F^{\mathbb{C}}\cap((V^{\mathbb{C}})^{K})^{\omega_{V}})\oplus 0\subseteq F^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0

Hence,

L\cap C^{\mathbb{C}}\cap((V^{\mathbb{C}})^{K}\oplus(W^{\mathbb{C}})^{K})^{\omega}\subseteq L\cap F^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0

Translating back to
T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}M
via the symplectomorphism from Equation (
111
), we get the desired result.
∎

Corollary 11.16
.

If
\mathcal{P}
has constant rank intersections with the orbit foliation on the orbit-type strata of
M
and for each
K\leq G
, the induced polarization
\mathcal{P}_{K}
on
M_{K}
is reducible in the sense of Definition
11.9
, then
\mathcal{P}
is singularly reducible.

Proof.

Since each orbit-type stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
is a sweep of
J_{K}^{-1}(0)\subseteq M_{K}
for some
K
, using Equation (
110
) from Lemma
11.15
, we see that
\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}S
has the form

\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}S=G\cdot(\mathcal{P}_{K}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}J_{K}^{-1}(0)+\mathcal{P}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{S}|_{J_{K}^{-1}(0)})

Using the
G
-invariance of
\mathcal{P}
together with the assumptions, the result then follows.
∎

Now that we have these preliminary results, we can extend the result of Guillemin and Sternberg.

Proposition 11.17
.

Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is a positive
G
-invariant polarization. Then
\mathcal{P}
is reducible.

Proof.

Since the orbits of the
G
-action are isotropic, it immediately follows from Corollary
4.33
that
\mathcal{P}_{K}
is reducible for all compact
K\leq G
and and
\mathcal{P}
intersects the orbit foliation trivially. Thus,
\mathcal{P}
is singularly reducible.
∎

Corollary 11.18
.

G
-invariant Kähler polarizations are always singularly reducible and induce a stratum-by-stratum Kähler structure on the reduced space.

Remark 11.19
.

Using what we developed above, we can recover Hyper Kähler reduction as discussed by Mayrand
[
May20
]

48
Non-Reducible Polarizations

As we saw in Example
11.10
, the canonical polarization of a cotangent bundle need not be reducible. Nevertheless, we do get something useful for the purposes of quantization. To set things up, let
G
be a connected Lie group,
Q
a proper
G
-space, and
J:T^{*}Q\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the Hamiltonian lift. Write
\tau:T^{*}Q\to Q
for the bundle projection. Recall that the canonical polarization on
T^{*}Q
has the form

\mathcal{P}:=\ker(T\tau)\otimes\mathbb{C}.

This is not singularly reducible, but it is equivariant and hence we can define a pseudobundle on the reduction.

Definition 11.20
.

Let
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}
be the reduction map. Define pseudobundle
\mathcal{P}_{0}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}((T^{*}Q)_{0})
by

T^{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{0}(\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))).

Using the fact that for any stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
, we have a corresponding stratum
Z_{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
with the property that
T^{*}(S/G)\subseteq Z_{S}/G
as an open dense set, we can prove the following.

Theorem 11.21
.

For any stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
,
\mathcal{P}_{0}|_{T^{*}(S/G)}
agrees with the canonical polarization on
T^{*}(S/G)
.

Proof.

Since a choice of Riemannian metric induces an embedding of vector bundles
T^{*}S\hookrightarrow T^{*}Q|_{S}
, this reduces to the non-singular case, and hence we can apply Proposition
4.39
to complete the proof.
∎

Remark 11.22
.

Once again, it should be noted that
\mathcal{P}_{0}
in Definition
11.20
does not define a stratified polarization on (
T^{*}Q)_{0}
in the sense of Definition
11.1
. What we have shown in Theorem
11.21
is that for each stratum of
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
there is an open dense set on which
\mathcal{P}_{0}
restricts to a polarization, but it need not define a polarization (or even a vector bundle) over the entire stratum. The reader is encourage to consult Example
11.10
for an example of this phenomenon.

†
†
margin:

12

Singular Reduction and Quantization

Now that we have laid the groundwork of stratified versions of symplectic manifolds, polarizations, and prequantum line bundles, we can now delve into a stratified version of quantization. As we shall see, this will be essentially the same as the classical definition of quantization.

49
Quantization of Symplectic Stratified Spaces

To begin, let us set up the notion of stratified quantization data in analogy with Definition
5.12
.

Definition 12.1

(Stratified Quantization Data)
.

Stratified quantization data will consist of four pieces of data.

(1)

A symplectic stratified space
(X,\Sigma,\{\cdot,\cdot\})

(2)

A Hamiltonian module
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}(X)
.

(3)

A stratified polarization
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma
.

(4)

A stratified prequantum line bundle
(\mathcal{L},\nabla,\{\nabla^{S}\}_{S\in\Sigma})
over
X
.

Write
(X,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
for this data.

Example 12.2
.

Usual quantization data in the sense Definition
5.12
are examples of stratified quantization data.

Definition 12.3
.

Given stratified quantization data
(X,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
, define the quantization by

\mathcal{Q}(X,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})\ |\ \nabla_{v}\sigma=0\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{P}\}.

(112)

If there is no risk of confusion, we will simply write
\mathcal{Q}(X)
for the quantization of
X
.

Example 12.4
.

Through our discussions in Examples
9.3
,
9.24
,
10.4
, and
11.5
, we already have non-trivial examples of quantization data.

Indeed, in Example
9.3
we saw the Poisson structure

\pi=r^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y}

on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
induces a symplectic stratification via its symplectic foliation
\mathscr{F}_{\pi}=\{\{0\},\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\}
. Then in Example
9.24
we saw that this stratification comes equipped with a Hamiltonian module

\mathcal{H}=\{V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ |\ V\text{ vanishes to order }2\text{ at }0\}.

In Example
10.4
we obtained a stratified prequantum line bundle
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)
where

\mathcal{L}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}

and
\nabla
is given by

\nabla|_{\{0\}}=0

and

\nabla|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}=L+i\alpha

for

\alpha=\ln(r)d\theta

with
(r,\theta)
being polar coordinates. Then, in Example
11.5
, we saw that this symplectic stratified space has two natural polarizations, a real polarization
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
spanned by
\partial_{\theta}
and a complex polarization
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}
arising from the canonical complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

What are then the quantizations? First, let us examine the real polarization. In this case, if
f\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}})\subseteq C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{C})
, we see that since
\partial_{\theta}
spans
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
,

0=\nabla_{\partial_{\theta}}f=\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}+i\alpha(\partial_{\theta})f=\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}+i\ln(r)f.

Hence, we must have

f=g(r)e^{-i\ln(r)\theta}.

(113)

Note that since
\theta
is
2\pi
-periodic, a function
f
can only satisfy Equation (
113
) if
f=0
. Hence,
\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}})=0
. Note that as is shown in
[
Śn75
]
, this is to be expected as
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
restricts to a Lagrangian toric fibration on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
. We can still get interesting quantizations in settings like this, but rather than using the classical definition of quantization as in Definition
12.3
, we should adopt either a cohomological approach as we discuss below or we could define the quantization in terms of distinguished leaves of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
called
Bohr-Sommerfeld Leaves
. For this latter approach, we first find all the leaves
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
which admit non-zero flat sections of the restricted bundle
(\mathcal{L}|_{S},\nabla|_{S})\to S
. In this case, it’s easy to see that
\{0\}
is a Bohr-Sommerfeld leaf as are all radius
r
circles
S_{r}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\ln(r)\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. Hence, the Bohr-Sommerfeld quantization would be

\mathcal{Q}_{BS}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}})=\mathbb{C}\oplus_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C},

where I added the subscript “BS” to distinguish this quantization from the quantization obtained via Definition
12.3
.
I leave the curious reader to consult
[
Śn75
]
or
[
Ham10
]
for more information on these two approaches.

As for the complex polarization, writing

\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial y},

it’s not too difficult to see that the polarization
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}
from Example
11.5
is spanned by

r^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}

Note that this lies in the complexification of the Hamiltonian module
\mathcal{H}
. Observe that over
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
we have

\alpha\bigg(r^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}\bigg)=i\ln(r)z,

where
z=x+iy
. Using this, we see that
f\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}})\subseteq C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{C})
if and only if

0=\nabla_{r^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}}f=\frac{\partial f}{\partial\overline{z}}-\ln(r)zf

Using this, it follows that
f=ge^{r\ln(r)}
where
g
is a holomorphic function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Since
e^{r\ln(r)}
is a smooth function, we then get a canonical identification between
\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}})
and the set of holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Note that due to Proposition
11.6
, this is precisely the set of polarized functions
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Remark 12.5
.

If for each
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
the map

\nabla\sigma:T^{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma\to\mathcal{L}

is continuous, then we can define a sheaf

\mathcal{S}(U)=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}|_{U})\ |\ \nabla_{v}\sigma=0\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{P}|_{U}\}

In this case, we could then use other definitions of quantization like Kostant’s cohomological wave functions

\mathcal{Q}^{\bullet}(X)=\bigoplus_{i}H^{i}(X,\mathcal{S})

making use of the sheaf cohomology of
\mathcal{S}
. For the purpose of this thesis, we will prefer the definition from Definition
12.3
.

Proposition 12.6
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{H},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
be quantization data. Let
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}^{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)
denote the set of stratified vector fields of the form

\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\{f_{1}V+f_{2}V_{2}\ |\ f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(X,\mathbb{C}),\ V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathcal{H}\}.

Furthermore, suppose the sections

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{P}}:=\Gamma(\mathcal{P})\cap\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C}

spans
\mathcal{P}
. Finally, let
S,R\subseteq\Sigma
be two strata with
S\subseteq\overline{R}
and suppose
\sigma\in\Gamma(L)
satisfies
\sigma|_{R}\in\mathcal{Q}(R)
. Then
\sigma|_{S}\in\mathcal{Q}(S)
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in S
and
v\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
. Choose a section
V\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{P}}
so that
V_{x}=v
. By assumption, we must have
(\nabla_{V}\sigma)|_{R}=\nabla^{R}_{V|_{R}}(\sigma|_{R})=0
. Let
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq R
be any sequence in
R
converging to
S
. Then, by continuity of
\nabla_{V}\sigma
,

0=\lim_{n\to\infty}\nabla_{V}\sigma(x_{n})=\nabla_{V}\sigma(x)=\nabla^{S}_{v}(\sigma|_{S}).

Since
x
and
v
were arbitrary, we conclude that
\sigma|_{S}\in\mathcal{Q}(S)
.
∎

Corollary 12.7
.

In the set up of Proposition
12.6
, given
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
, we have
\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(X)

\iff

\sigma|_{S}\in\mathcal{Q}(S)
for each regular stratum
S\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

Every stratum is contained in the closure of some regular stratum. Now apply Proposition
12.6
.
∎

Remark 12.8
.

This is essentially what we already saw in Example
12.4
, where the quantization of the whole space was determined by the quantization of the open dense stratum.

50

[Q,R]=0

We would now like to set up a general statement for quantization commuting with reduction. As we shall see, this is a general framework which we can apply cases like toric manifolds.

Let’s now return to the setting of singular reduction. For all that follows, we fix the following.

•

A connected Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

•

A proper Hamiltonian
G
-space
J:(M,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}

•

A
G
-invariant polarization
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T^{\mathbb{C}}M
.

Write
\pi:J^{-1}(0)\to J^{-1}(0)/G=M_{0}
for the quotient map,
\mathcal{L}_{0}=(\mathcal{L}|_{J^{-1}(0)})/G
, and

\mathcal{P}_{0}=\pi_{*}(\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))).

Recall that if
(\mathcal{L},\nabla)
is equivariantly generated over
J^{-1}(0)
, i.e.

J^{-1}(0)\times\Gamma(L)^{G}\to L|_{J^{-1}(0)};\quad(x,\sigma)\mapsto\sigma(x)

is surjective, then we get a surjective map

\kappa:\Gamma(L)^{G}\to\Gamma(L_{0})

(114)

With this set up, we can now state the following.

Theorem 12.9
.

Suppose
(L,\nabla)
is equivariantly generated over
J^{-1}(0)
and suppose
\mathcal{P}
is reducible at level zero (see Definition
11.9
). Then
\kappa
induces a linear map

\kappa:\mathcal{Q}(M)^{G}\to\mathcal{Q}(M_{0}).

(115)

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(M)^{G}
,
x\in J^{-1}(0)
, and
v\in(\mathcal{P}_{0})_{[}x]
an element of the reduced polarization. We can lift
v
to an element of
\widetilde{v}\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{G_{x}}
. By construction,

\nabla^{0}_{v}\kappa(\sigma)=\kappa(\nabla_{\widetilde{v}}\sigma)=\kappa(0)=0.

Thus,
\kappa
defines a map

\kappa:\mathcal{Q}(M)^{G}\to\mathcal{Q}(M_{0}).

∎

Definition 12.10

(Quantization Commutes With Reduction)
.

If the map
\kappa
in Equation (
115
) is a bijection, then we say quantization commutes with reduction. (In symbols, this is usually denoted
[Q,R]=0
).

To end this chapter, we will now move on to prove that
[Q,R]=0
for two of the major classes of examples examined throughout this thesis: toric manifolds and cotangent bundles.

51
Toric Manifolds and
[Q,R]=0

Let
\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Delzant polytope and
J_{\Delta}:(M_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the induced toric
T^{n}
-manifold from Delzant’s construction as detailed in Section
11.1
. Writing

\Delta=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ \langle x,v_{i}\rangle\geq\lambda_{i}\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,N\},

let
k
be the kernel of the map
\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
mapping
e_{i}
to
v_{i}
for each of the standard basis vectors
e_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\iota:\mathfrak{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
, the inclusion, and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Then, if we define

J:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N};\quad(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(|z_{1}|^{2},\dots,|z_{N}|^{2})-\lambda,

it follows that

M_{\Delta}=(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)/K

is a toric
T^{N}/K
manifold with moment map

J_{\Delta}:M_{\Delta}\to\mathbb{R}^{N};\quad[x]\mapsto J(x),

where
K=\exp(\mathfrak{k})\subseteq T^{N}
. We also saw in Section
20.2
that the canonical complex structure
I^{can}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
is reducible with respect to the Hamiltonian action of
K
and hence descends to a
T^{N}/K
equivariant Kähler structure
I_{\Delta}
.

Now for the prequantum line bundle. As we saw in Equation (
106
), if
\lambda
and
\iota^{*}(\lambda)
are integral, then we get an induced homomorphism

\rho:T^{N}\to S^{1}

which makes the canonical prequantum line bundle
\mathbb{C}^{N}\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
into a reducible prequantum line bundle, allowing us to define prequantum line bundle

\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}=((\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)\times\mathbb{C})/K

over
M_{\Delta}
. Furthermore, as we saw, for any subtorus
H\leq\ker(\rho)
,
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
is singularly reducible with respect to the induced action of
H
on
M_{\Delta}
. Write

J_{H}:M_{\Delta}\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}

for this induced Hamiltonian
H
-structure. Furthermore, the canonical Kähler structure
I_{\Delta}
on
M_{\Delta}
coming from Delzant’s construction is equivariant hence reducible as well. Thus, we have a quantization map

\mathcal{Q}(M_{\Delta})^{H}\to\mathcal{Q}((M_{\Delta})_{0}),

where
(M_{\Delta})_{0}:=J_{H}^{-1}(0)/H
is the symplectic reduction of
M_{\Delta}
with respect to the action of
H
. We show now that this is a bijection, provided some assumptions are met. Let us first discuss an alternate construction for
M_{\Delta}
.

For each facet
F\subseteq\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, define

I_{F}:=\{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\ |\ \langle x,v_{i}\rangle=\lambda_{i}\text{ for all }x\in F\}.

Using this, we can define a closed subset
Z_{F}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{N}
by

Z_{F}:=\{(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\ |\ z_{i}=0\text{ for all }i\in I_{F}\}.

Now set

U_{\Delta}:=\mathbb{C}^{N}\setminus\bigcup_{F}Z_{F}.

Let
K_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{N}
be the complexification of
K
. Then
K_{\mathbb{C}}
naturally acts on
U_{\Delta}
. Furthermore,
J^{-1}(0)\subseteq U_{\Delta}
.

Proposition 12.11

(
[
Kir98
]
)
.

The inclusion
(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)\subseteq U_{\Delta}
induces a diffeomorphism

M_{\Delta}\to U_{\Delta}/K_{\mathbb{C}}.

This can also be extended to the equivariant line bundle as well. Recall that the defining homomorphism for
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
is given by

\rho:T^{N}\to S^{1};\quad(t_{1},\dots,t_{N})\mapsto t_{1}^{-\lambda_{1}}\cdots t_{N}^{-\lambda_{N}},

where
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
. This trivially extends to the complexifications

\rho_{\mathbb{C}}:(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{N}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times};\quad(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\mapsto z_{1}^{-\lambda_{1}}\cdots z_{N}^{-\lambda_{N}}

Thus, we get a
T^{N}
-equivariant homomorphism of line bundles

(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)\times\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow U_{\Delta}\times\mathbb{C},

where
U_{\Delta}\times\mathbb{C}
has the
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{N}
action induced by
\rho_{\mathbb{C}}
. Since
K_{\mathbb{C}}
acts freely on the left-hand side, we obtain in conjunction with Proposition
12.11
the following result

Proposition 12.12
.

The inclusion
(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)\subseteq U_{\Delta}
induces an isomorphism of complex line bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{\Delta}\times\mathbb{C})/K_{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\Delta}/K_{\mathbb{C}}}

where the bottom arrow is the diffeomorphism from Proposition
12.11
.

For this reason, the action of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{N}
descends to an action on
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}\to M_{\Delta}
, extending the action of
T^{N}
. Hence
H_{\mathbb{C}}
, the complexification of
H
, naturally acts on
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}\to M_{\Delta}
.

Proposition 12.13
.

Let
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})
. Then,
i\xi\in i\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\text{Lie}(H_{\mathbb{C}})
acts on
\sigma
by the following formula

(i\xi)\cdot\sigma=\nabla^{\Delta}_{I_{\Delta}(\xi_{M_{\Delta}})}\sigma

In particular, if
\sigma
is holomorphic, then

(i\xi)\cdot\sigma=i(\xi\cdot\sigma).

Proof.

Fix
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
. By Proposition
2.39
, the section
\sigma
lifts to a
K_{\mathbb{C}}
-equivariant section of the trivial bundle
U_{\Delta}\times\mathbb{C}\to U_{\Delta}
, which can be identified with a
K_{\mathbb{C}}
equivariant function
f:U_{\Delta}\to\mathbb{C}
with respect to the extended
K_{\mathbb{C}}
action on
\mathbb{C}
. Since
\sigma
is obtained by restricting
f
to
(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)
, we will show

(i\xi)\cdot f|_{(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)}=\nabla^{can}_{I^{can}(\xi_{\mathbb{C}^{N}})}f|_{(\iota^{*}\circ J)^{-1}(0)}

Recall that after letting
(r_{1},\theta_{1},\dots,r_{N},\theta_{N})
denote polar coordinates on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, we have

I^{can}(\partial_{\theta_{j}})=\partial_{r_{j}}

In particular, writing
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we have

I^{can}(\xi_{\mathbb{C}^{N}})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}I^{can}(\partial_{\theta_{j}})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}\partial_{r_{j}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\nabla^{can}_{I^{can}(\xi_{\mathbb{C}^{N}})}f

\displaystyle=I^{can}(\xi_{\mathbb{C}^{N}})f+\frac{i}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}r_{j}^{2}d\theta_{j}(I^{can}(\xi_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}))f

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\xi_{j}\partial_{r_{j}}f+\frac{i}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}r_{j}^{2}d\theta_{j}\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}\partial_{r_{k}}\bigg)f

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\xi_{j}\partial_{r_{j}}f.

Conversely, if we fix
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\in J^{-1}(0)
then

\displaystyle(i\xi)\cdot f(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\rho(\exp(it\xi))f(\exp(-it\xi)\cdot z)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}e^{t\langle\lambda,\xi\rangle}f(e^{t\xi_{1}}z_{1},\dots,e^{t\xi_{N}}z_{N})

\displaystyle=\langle\lambda,\xi\rangle f(z)+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\xi_{j}\partial_{r_{j}}f(z)

Note that by definition, since
H\leq\ker(\rho)
it follows that
\langle\lambda,\xi\rangle\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. However, being in the kernel is a linear relationship at the level of Lie algebras and hence it follows that
\langle\lambda,\xi\rangle=0
.

Now to show the formula for holomorphic sections. Suppose
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})
is holomorphic and observe that

I_{\Delta}(\xi_{M_{\Delta}}+iI_{\Delta}(\xi_{M_{\Delta}}))=-i(\xi_{M_{\Delta}}+iI_{\Delta}(\xi_{M_{\Delta}}))

Thus,
\xi_{M_{\Delta}}+iI_{\Delta}(\xi_{M_{\Delta}})
is a section of the anti-holomorphic tangent bundle
T^{0,1}_{I_{\Delta}}M_{\Delta}
and so by definition of
\sigma
being holomorphic, we have

\nabla_{\xi_{M_{\Delta}}+iI_{\Delta}(\xi_{M_{\Delta}})}\sigma=0.

Hence by the linearity in the first entry of the connection,

\nabla_{\xi_{M_{\Delta}}}\sigma=-i\nabla_{I_{\Delta}(\xi_{M_{\Delta}})}\sigma

and hence the result follows.
∎

Corollary 12.14
.

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(M_{\Delta})^{H}
, then
\sigma
is
H_{\mathbb{C}}
-equivariant.

Proof.

Fix
\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(M_{\Delta})^{H}
. Since
H
is a subtorus of the standard torus
(S^{1})^{N}
, we have
H_{\mathbb{C}}=H\cdot\exp(i\mathfrak{h})
. And thus, since
H
is assumed to be connected, it suffices to show if
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}
, then

(\xi_{1}+i\xi_{2})\cdot\sigma=0.

In this case, we easily see that
\xi_{1}\cdot\sigma=0
since
\sigma
is
H
-equivariant. Furthermore, since
\sigma
is by definition holomorphic, it follows we have from Proposition
12.13
that

(i\xi_{2})\cdot\sigma=i(\xi_{2}\cdot\sigma)=0.

Thus,
\sigma
is
H_{\mathbb{C}}
-equivariant.
∎

Using this result, we can now nearly prove quantization commutes with reduction.

Theorem 12.15
.

Suppose

M_{\Delta}^{s}:=H_{\mathbb{C}}\cdot J_{H}^{-1}(0)

is an open subset. Then

\kappa:\mathcal{Q}(M_{\Delta})^{H}\to\mathcal{Q}((M_{\Delta})_{0})

as in Theorem
12.9
is injective.

Proof.

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}(M_{\Delta})^{H}
satisfies
\kappa(\sigma)=0
, then we have
\sigma|_{J_{H}^{-1}(0)}=0
since fibre-wise the map

\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}|_{J_{H}^{-1}(0)}\to(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})_{0}

is a bijection. By Corollary
12.14
,
\sigma
is
H_{\mathbb{C}}
equivariant and thus
\sigma|_{M_{\Delta}^{s}}=0
. Thus, we have a holomorphic section vanishing on an open set and so
\sigma=0
. Hence,
\kappa
is injective.
∎

52
Cotangent Bundles and
[Q,R]=0

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
Q
a proper
G
-space, and
J:T^{*}Q\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the Hamiltonian lift. We almost have a canonical choice of stratified quantization data on the reduced space
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
. In particular, letting
\mathcal{L}=T^{*}Q\times\mathbb{C}
be the trivial bundle with canonical prequantum connection
\nabla=L-i\theta_{Q}
, where
\theta_{Q}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}Q)
is the Liouville
1
-form, we have from Example
10.16
, that
\mathcal{L}
is singularly reducible over
J^{-1}(0)
and hence we get a canonical prequantum line bundle

\mathcal{L}_{0}=(T^{*}Q)_{0}\times\mathbb{C}.

Furthermore, letting
\tau_{Q}:T^{*}Q\to Q
be the bundle map, we have a canonical polarization

\mathcal{P}=\ker(T\tau_{Q})\otimes\mathbb{C}

As we saw in Example
11.10
,
\mathcal{P}
is in general not reducible. Nevertheless, we still get a pseudobundle

\mathcal{P}_{0}=T^{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{0}(\mathcal{P}\cap T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0)))

which restricts to a polarization on an open dense set inside each stratum of
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
. Hence, we can still define

\mathcal{Q}((T^{*}Q)_{0})=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{0})\ |\ \nabla_{v}\sigma=0\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\}

Note that in this setting, the sections of both
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}
can be identified with complex valued differentiable functions on
T^{*}Q
and
(T^{*}Q)_{0}
, respectively. Further, recall from Theorem
5.14
that
\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)=\tau_{Q}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C})
and
\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)^{G}=\tau_{Q}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q)^{G}
. Using the identification
\tau_{Q}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q)=C^{\infty}(Q)
and
C^{\infty}(Q,\mathbb{C})^{G}=C^{\infty}(Q/G,\mathbb{C})
, we can view
\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)^{G}
as the set of smooth complex-valued functions on the quotient
Q/G
. As it turns out, this insight allows us to show quantization commutes with reduction here as well.

Theorem 12.16
.

The map
\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)^{G}\to\mathcal{Q}((T^{*}Q)_{0})
is a bijection. In particular,
\mathcal{Q}((T^{*}Q)_{0})
can be identified with
C^{\infty}(Q/G)
.

Proof.

First, let us show
\kappa
is injective. Let
f\in\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)^{G}
satisfy
\kappa(f)=0
. Then
f\in\tau_{Q}^{*}C^{\infty}(Q)^{G}
and
f|_{J^{-1}(0)}=0
. Since
f
is constant on cotangent fibres and all cotangent fibres intersect
J^{-1}(0)
non-trivially, it follows that
f=0
. Hence
\kappa
is injective.

Now for surjective. Let
0_{Q}:Q\hookrightarrow T^{*}Q
be the zero section. Since
J^{-1}(0)
is a pseudobundle, the image of the zero section lies in
J^{-1}(0)
and is smooth and equivariant. In particular, we have

0_{Q}^{*}C^{\infty}(J^{-1}(0))^{G}\subseteq C^{\infty}(Q)^{G}.

Hence, we get a differentiable map between subcartesian spaces

\overline{0_{Q}}:Q/G\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}

Letting
f\in\mathcal{Q}((T^{*}Q)_{0})
and
\pi_{Q}:Q\to Q/G
, we get a function
\pi_{Q}^{*}0_{Q}^{*}f\in C^{\infty}(Q)
. Now let

h=\tau_{Q}^{*}\pi_{Q}^{*}\overline{0_{Q}}^{*}(f)\in\mathcal{Q}(T^{*}Q)^{G}

I claim
\kappa(h)=f
. Indeed, if we write
\pi_{0}:J^{-1}(0)\to(T^{*}Q)_{0}
for the reduction map, it suffices to show

h|_{J^{-1}(0)}=\pi_{0}^{*}f

and so in turn in suffices to show for any orbit-type stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(Q)
that
h|_{Z_{S}}=\pi_{0}^{*}f|_{Z_{S}}
where
Z_{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{G}(J^{-1}(0))
is the orbit-type stratum from Theorem
9.37
. Recall that we showed
J_{S}^{-1}(0)\subseteq Z_{S}
as an open dense set, where
J_{S}:T^{*}S\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is the Hamiltonian lift of the
G
-action on
S
. Hence, it suffices to show

h|_{J_{S}^{-1}(0)}=\pi_{0}^{*}f|_{J_{S}^{-1}(0)}.

This follows immediately from the non-singular reduction statement in Theorem
5.25
. Hence,
\kappa(h)=f
and thus
\kappa
is a bijection.
∎

\cleardoubleevenpage
